Two-sided heat kernel bounds for 8 / 3 \sqrt{8/3} -Liouville Brownian motion
Sebastian Andres, Naotaka Kajino, Konstantinos Kavvadias, Jason Miller

Abstract
. Liouville Brownian motion (LBM) is the canonical diffusion process on a Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) surface. In this work, we establish upper and lower bounds for the heat kernel for LBM when γ = 8 / 3 \gamma=\sqrt{8/3} in terms of the 8 / 3 \sqrt{8/3} -LQG metric which are sharp up to a polylogarithmic factor in the exponential.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 60J35, 60J55, 60J60, 60K37; Secondary: 31C25, 60J45, 60G15.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

During the past 15 years, substantial research activity has been focused on the study a random geometry induced by the two-dimensional Gaussian free field, commonly known under the banner of
Liouville quantum gravity
. For an overview, see, for instance, the recent surveys
[
17
,
89
,
42
]
.
Suppose that
h
is an instance of (some form of) the Gaussian free field (GFF) on a domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
. The Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) surface described by
h
formally refers to the random two-dimensional Riemannian manifold with metric tensor

(1.1)

e^{\gamma h(z)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})

where
\gamma\in(0,2]
is a parameter and
dx^{2}+dy^{2}
denotes the Euclidean metric on
D
.
This expression does not make literal sense because
h
is a distribution and not a function.
There has been a considerable amount of work in recent years aimed at making rigorous sense of (
1.1
).
The construction of the volume form of (
1.1
) is related to Kahane’s theory of
Gaussian multiplicative chaos

[
55
]
and appears in a number of places. The approach taken in
[
36
]
is to let
h_{\epsilon}(z)
be the average of
h
on
\partial B(z,\epsilon)
and then take

(1.2)

\mu_{h}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{\gamma^{2}/2}e^{\gamma h_{\epsilon}(z)}dz

where
dz
denotes Lebesgue measure on
D
, see
[
14
]
for an elementary approach establishing a universal limit measure for a general class of mollifications of the field.

The metric (i.e., two-point distance function) associated with (
1.1
) was first constructed in the case
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
in
[
76
,
73
,
78
,
77
]
, building on
[
74
]
and using the tools from
[
35
,
75
]
.
The construction is indirect and is based on defining a growth process called
quantum Loewner evolution
which turns out to describe the growth of metric balls in the resulting metric space.
The metric associated with (
1.1
) was subsequently constructed for all
\gamma\in(0,2)
as a limit of the type (
1.2
) in
[
31
,
34
,
48
,
47
,
50
,
49
]
but with
\gamma
replaced by
\xi=\gamma/d_{\gamma}
where
d_{\gamma}
is the exponent constructed in
[
33
]
. In the present work, we will focus on the case that
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
. This value is special because it turns out to be equivalent to the Brownian map
[
73
,
77
]
, which is the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov scaling limit of random quadrangulations
[
63
,
69
]
.

Our main focus will be on the relationship between
Liouville Brownian motion
(LBM) and the
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG metric.
Recall that LBM is the Brownian motion associated with (
1.1
) and was first constructed in
[
39
,
13
]
.
It is defined as a time change of a standard planar Brownian motion where the change of time depends on the underlying LQG surface.
By general theory, the LBM turns out to be symmetric with respect to the Liouville measure
\mu_{h}
.
In
[
38
]
Garban, Rhodes and Vargas also identified the Dirichlet form associated with the LBM and showed that its transition semigroup is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{h}
,
meaning that the Liouville heat kernel
p_{t}(x,y)
exists. Moreover, they observed that the intrinsic metric
generated by that Dirichlet form is identically zero, which indicates some non-Gaussian heat kernel behavior.
This degeneracy of the intrinsic metric is known to occur typically for diffusions on fractals, whose heat kernels indeed satisfy the so-called sub-Gaussian estimates; see e.g. the survey articles
[
11
,
57
]
and references therein.
The works
[
67
,
3
]
establish the continuity of the Liouville heat kernel
p_{t}(x,y)
in
(t,x,y)
and some upper and lower bounds on it.
The bounds in
[
3
]
have successfully identified the order of the on-diagonal part
p_{t}(x,x)
for small
t
as
t^{-1}
, up to a factor of a power of
\log t^{-1}
reflecting the randomness of the environment and except that the lower bound is proved only for
\mu_{h}
-a.e.
x
.
On the other hand, for the off-diagonal behavior of
p_{t}(x,y)
, the sub-Gaussian upper bounds obtained in
[
67
,
3
]
are stated in terms of the
Euclidean metric
and thereby are expected to be far from being sharp, and the known sub-Gaussian lower bound due to
[
67
]
gives a decay estimate only in
t
, with an exponent which is also not expected to be sharp, for each fixed
x,y
.
The work
[
33
]
is focused on the transition kernel for a random walk on a certain graph approximation to LQG between fixed points and uses it to construct the dimension exponent for LQG.

The definition of LBM is further motivated by recent works which have shown that it arises as the scaling limit of simple random walk on certain graph approximations to LQG. The convergence was first proved modulo time parameterization in
[
52
]
(for the so-called mated-CRT map) and
[
51
]
(for the Poisson-Voronoi tessellation of the Brownian map, equivalently
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG) using the invariance principle established in
[
53
]
. The convergence was upgraded to obtain the time parameterization in
[
15
]
.

Most results in LQG, including the present work, remain specific to two dimensions and have not yet been extended to
higher dimensions, which is mainly due to the critical role of conformal invariance in two-dimensional results that is not available in higher dimension. Nonetheless, recent
works
[
22
,
32
,
30
,
29
,
19
]
initiated the study of higher-dimensional analogs of LQG. In this context, higher dimensional version of LBM have been constructed in
[
30
,
19
]
, where in
[
19
]
the short-time asymptotics of the heat kernel along the diagonal and the spectral dimension are identified.

1.2.
Main results

The main contributions of the present work are sharp off-diagonal upper and lower bounds for the (continuous) Liouville heat kernel
p_{t}(u,v)
in the case
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
in terms of the
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG metric, denoted by
d_{h}
in the following, which hold for all points simultaneously for a.e. instance of the
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG surface and match up to polylog errors in the exponent.
We will state and prove our results in the case that the underlying LQG surface is the
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG sphere
[
35
,
28
]
. By absolute continuity, one can extract similar heat kernel bounds for other LQG surfaces.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a finite constant
\kappa>0
such that the following is true. For a.e. instance
\mathcal{S}=(\mathbb{S}^{2},h)
of the
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG sphere, there exist random positive constants
C_{i}=C_{i}(h)
,
i=1,2
, such that for all
u,v\in\mathcal{S}
and
t\in(0,1/2]
,

(1.3)

p_{t}(u,v)\leq\frac{C_{1}(\log t^{-1})^{\kappa}}{t}\exp\Biggl(-C_{2}\biggl(\frac{d_{h}(u,v)^{4}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{3}}\biggl(\log\biggl(e+\frac{d_{h}(u,v)}{t}\biggr)\biggr)^{-\kappa}\Biggr).

Theorem 1.2
.

There exists a finite constant
\kappa>0
such that the following is true. For a.e. instance
\mathcal{S}=(\mathbb{S}^{2},h)
of the
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG sphere, there exists a random constant
C=C(h)\in(0,1)
such that for all
u,v\in\mathcal{S}
and
0<t<Cd_{h}(u,v)
,

(1.4)

p_{t}(u,v)\geq\exp\Biggl(-\biggl(\frac{d_{h}(u,v)^{4}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{3}}\biggl(\log\biggl(\frac{d_{h}(u,v)}{t}\biggr)\biggr)^{\kappa}\Biggr).

Moreover, for all
u\in\mathcal{S}
and
t\in(0,1/2]
, we have that
p_{t}(u,u)\geq Ct^{-1}(\log t^{-1})^{-\kappa}
.

Remark 1.3
.

(i) The lower bound in Theorem
1.2
becomes effective in the regime
0<t<d_{h}(u,v)^{4}
, which reflects that the LBM needs to travel a sufficiently long distance so that the possibly bad local geometries around the starting point become irrelevant.
A similar phenomenon can be observed, for instance, for simple random walks on supercritical percolation clusters, where one has to allow the random walk some random time to exit bad parts of the cluster before Gaussian heat kernel decay emerges; see
[
10
]
.

(ii) While we do not discuss here the necessity of the polylogarithmic corrections in the established heat kernel bounds, we remark that in many instances of stochastic processes on random media, in particular for processes in low dimensions and models at criticality, heat kernel fluctuations are known to occur, caused by local irregularities in the random medium, see
[
2
]
for a recent review on this topic.

Off-diagonal upper and lower bounds of the heat kernel similar to (
1.3
) and (
1.4
)
have been proved in
[
26
,
27
,
12
,
7
,
6
]
,
for the canonical diffusions on various random fractals which are trees or sufficiently
close to being trees so that their heat kernel behavior can be described very well in terms of
the effective resistance metric as established in the general results in
[
25
]
.
Our main results, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
above, are in sharp contrast to
those preceding results in that the effective resistance metric is no longer well-defined
since the LBM a.s. does not hit a given point. To the best of our knowledge,
Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are the first result in the literature establishing
sharp sub-Gaussian heat kernel bounds for diffusions on random fractals
which do not admit well-defined effective resistance metrics.

1.3.
Strategy and outline

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on a criterion for off-diagonal sub-Gaussian heat kernel upper bounds, implied by results in
[
41
,
40
]
, in terms of an exit time estimate and an on-diagonal heat kernel upper bound.
However, since such estimates hold only with polylogarithmic corrections in the present context of LQG, in a first step we extend the relevant results in
[
41
,
40
]
.
More precisely, we derive a perturbed off-diagonal sub-Gaussian heat kernel upper bound which allows fluctuations in the exit time estimate and in the on-diagonal heat kernel upper bound to be given by a general class of perturbation functions, including polynomial and polylogarithmic corrections.
Assuming a similar upper bound on the volumes of metric balls instead of an on-diagonal heat kernel upper bound, we also deduce a similarly perturbed on-diagonal heat kernel lower bound by a standard method.
These are done in Section
2
, which is written in the framework of a general diffusion (without killing inside) as it might be of independent interest.
We refer to
[
25
]
for similar results on heat kernel bounds with fluctuations for local resistance forms (symmetric diffusions with well-defined effective resistance metrics).

After a review of the Brownian map, LQG and LBM in Section
3
,
we next establish volume estimates for
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG which hold with polylogarithmic corrections in Section
4
. More precisely, it was shown in earlier work by Le Gall
[
62
]
in the context of the Brownian map that for each
\delta>0
there a.s. exists
r_{0}>0
so that for all
r\in(0,r_{0})
the volume of every ball of radius
r
is between
r^{4+\delta}
and
r^{4-\delta}
. We improve this to show that there exists a constant
\kappa>0
and a.s. exists
r_{0}>0
so that the volume of every ball of radius
r\in(0,r_{0})
is between
r^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{-\kappa}
and
r^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{\kappa}
. The proofs in this part of the work are based purely on Brownian map techniques. By the equivalence of
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG and the Brownian map, the same estimates hold also for
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG. We remark that some of our volume estimates in Section
4
can also be extracted from more recent work of Le Gall
[
65
]
but we have included our proofs since the reader might find them of independent interest.

As a preparation for the proofs of the quenched exit time lower bound for LBM and
the off-diagonal heat kernel lower bound (
1.4
) in Theorem
1.2
,
in Section
5
we prove a percolation result for graphs
formed by tilings of
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG surfaces by chunks of
{\rm SLE}_{6}

(Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
).
Roughly speaking, they state that with high probability we can construct a strongly
supercritical configuration of such tiles each of which have a certain prescribed set of properties,
under the assumption that with sufficiently high probability each tile has that set of properties.
We first prove this result in the simpler setting of the half-plane
as Proposition
5.2
, and then
translate it into an analogous result, Proposition
5.1
,
for the setting of the disk.

We then proceed in Section
6
to establish the quenched exit time upper and lower bounds for LBM,
which states that there exists a constant
\kappa>0
and a.s. exists
r_{0}>0
so that for all
r\in(0,r_{0})
the conditional expectation (given the underlying LQG surface)
of the amount of time it takes an LBM to exit any ball of radius
r
when starting from its center is between
r^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{-\kappa}
and
r^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{\kappa}
.
The upper bound follows easily by combining the volume upper bound from Section
4
with the known two-sided Hölder continuity estimate for
d_{h}
with respect to the usual spherical metric due to
[
73
, Theorem 1.2]

and the fact that the LBM has the same Green functions as the Brownian motion on the sphere.
The proof of the lower bound is based on an application of Proposition
5.1

with the prescribed property for each tile being that with uniformly positive probability it takes LBM a certain amount of time to cross.
Altogether, this is enough to conclude Theorem
1.1
and the on-diagonal heat kernel lower bound in Theorem
1.2

thanks to the on-diagonal heat kernel upper bound in
[
3
]
, the general result from Section
2
and the volume upper bound from Section
4
.

We finish by establishing (
1.4
) in Theorem
1.2
using a chaining argument in Section
7
.
We will construct our chains out of annuli consisting of chunks of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
by applying Proposition
5.1
in a manner similar to the proof of the exit time lower bound above.
The special property that the chunks which make up these annuli will have is an a priori lower bound on the amount of time it takes the LBM to cross.

2.
Heat kernel bounds with fluctuations for general diffusions

The purpose of this section is to give some sufficient conditions for the heat kernel
(transition density) of a diffusion (without killing inside) on a general state space to satisfy
a sub-Gaussian type off-diagonal upper bound and an on-diagonal lower bound which possibly involve some
lower order correction terms that are typically polylogarithmic. The main results of
this section (Theorem
2.12
and Proposition
2.18
below),
stated in their simplest possible forms that are still applicable to the case of the

\sqrt{8/3}
-Liouville Brownian motion, yield the following theorem;
see
[
37
, Appendix A.2 and Section 4.5]
and
[
24
, Appendix A.1]
for the basics of Markov processes.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a compact metric space with at least two points,
let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
\mathcal{X}
with full support,
and let
X=(\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty)},\{P_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}})
be a conservative diffusion on
\mathcal{X}
which
admits a (unique) continuous function
p=p_{t}(x,y):(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to[0,\infty)

such that for any
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}
,

(2.1)

P_{x}[X_{t}\in dy]=p_{t}(x,y)\,\mu(dy)\quad\text{(as Borel measures on $\mathcal{X}$).}

Further set
\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X}:=\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}d(x,y)(\in(0,\infty))
,

B(x,r):=\{y\in\mathcal{X}\mid d(x,y)<r\}
for
(x,r)\in\mathcal{X}\times(0,\infty)
,

\tau_{A}:=\inf\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid X_{t}\not\in A\}

(
\inf\emptyset:=\infty
)
for
A\subset\mathcal{X}
,
let
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
,
\beta\in(1,\infty)
, and consider the following conditions:

\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}

(Volume upper bound)

There exist
\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}\in[0,\infty)
and
C_{\mathrm{V}}\in(0,\infty)

such that for any
(x,r)\in\mathcal{X}\times(0,\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X}]
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mu(B(x,r))\leq C_{\mathrm{V}}r^{\alpha}\bigl(\log(e+r^{-1})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}}.

\mathrm{(E)}

(Mean exit time estimate)

There exist
\kappa_{\mathrm{el}},\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}\in[0,\infty)
,
a_{\mathrm{e}}\in[1,\infty)

and
C_{\mathrm{e}}\in(0,\infty)
such that for any
(x,r)\in\mathcal{X}\times(0,a_{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X}]
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}r^{\beta}\bigl(\log(e+r^{-1})\bigr)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}}\leq E_{x}[\tau_{B(x,r)}]\leq C_{\mathrm{e}}r^{\beta}\bigl(\log(e+r^{-1})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}}.

\mathrm{(DU)}

(On-diagonal upper bound)

There exist
\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}\in[0,\infty)
and
C_{\mathrm{du}}\in(0,\infty)

such that for any
(t,x,y)\in(0,(\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})^{\beta}]\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,y)\leq C_{\mathrm{du}}t^{-\alpha/\beta}\bigl(\log(e+t^{-1})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}}.

(i)

(Off-diagonal upper bound)
Assume
\mathrm{(E)}
and
\mathrm{(DU)}
,
set
\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}:=(2+\beta)(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})
,
let
\varepsilon_{h}\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}<1
and
set
\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}^{\prime}:=(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha/\beta)
.
Then there exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)

such that for any
(t,x,y)\in(0,(\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})^{\beta}]\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
,

(2.5)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,y)\leq c_{1}\frac{\bigl(\log(e+t^{-1})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}^{\prime}}}{t^{\alpha/\beta}}\exp\Biggl(-c_{2}\biggl(\frac{d(x,y)^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}\biggl(\log\biggl(e+\frac{d(x,y)}{t}\biggr)\biggr)^{-\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}{\beta-1}}\Biggr).

(ii)

(On-diagonal lower bound)
Assume
\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}
,
\mathrm{(E)}
and that
X
is
\mu
-symmetric,
i.e.,
p_{t}(x,y)=p_{t}(y,x)
for any
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Set
\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}:=(2+\beta)(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})
and

\kappa_{\mathrm{dl}}:=\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha/\beta
.
Then there exists
c_{3}\in(0,\infty)

such that for any
(t,x)\in(0,(\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})^{\beta}]\times\mathcal{X}
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,x)\geq c_{3}t^{-\alpha/\beta}\bigl(\log(e+t^{-1})\bigr)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{dl}}}.

Theorem
2.1
is obtained at the end of this section as a corollary of the
main results of this section (Theorem
2.12
and Proposition
2.18
below),
which, for potential future applications, we state and prove in the general setting
of a diffusion without killing inside on a locally compact separable metric space.

The rest of this section is organized as follows.
First in Subsection
2.1
, we introduce the general setting and
suitable generalizations of conditions
\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}
,
\mathrm{(E)}
and
\mathrm{(DU)}

of Theorem
2.1
. In Subsection
2.2
, we state
the generalization of Theorem
2.1
-(i) (Theorem
2.12
)
and prove it as an application of
[
40
, Theorems 6.2 and 6.4]
.
Subsection
2.3
states and proves the generalization of
Theorem
2.1
-(ii) (Proposition
2.18
),
which is a mere adaptation of a well-known argument to our setting. Then
Theorem
2.1
is deduced from Theorem
2.12
and
Proposition
2.18
in Subsection
2.4
.

2.1.
Setting and conditions

Throughout the rest of Section
2
, we assume that
(\mathcal{X},d)
is a locally compact
separable metric space, and that
\mu
is a Radon measure on
\mathcal{X}
with full support,
i.e., a Borel measure on
\mathcal{X}
which is finite on any compact subset of
\mathcal{X}
and
strictly positive on any non-empty open subset of
\mathcal{X}
.
We will refer to such a triple
(\mathcal{X},d,\mu)
as a
metric measure space
.
For
(x,r)\in\mathcal{X}\times(0,\infty)
, we set
B(x,r):=\{y\in\mathcal{X}\mid d(x,y)<r\}
,
and the closure of
B(x,r)
in
\mathcal{X}
is denoted by
\overline{B}(x,r)
.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{\partial}:=\mathcal{X}\cup\{\partial\}
be the one-point compactification of
\mathcal{X}
, so that
the Borel
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\partial})
of
\mathcal{X}_{\partial}
can be expressed,
in terms of that
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
of
\mathcal{X}
, as

\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\partial})=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})\cup\{A\cup\{\partial\}\mid A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})\}
.
In what follows,
[-\infty,\infty]
-valued functions on
\mathcal{X}
are always set
to be
0
at
\partial
unless their values at
\partial
are already defined:

f(\partial):=0
for
f:\mathcal{X}\to[-\infty,\infty]
.

Let

X=\bigl(\Omega,\mathscr{M},\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty]},\{P_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial}}\bigr)

be a diffusion without killing inside on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}))

with life time
\zeta
and shift operators
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty]}
.
By definition,
(\Omega,\mathscr{M})
is a measurable space,
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty]}

is a family of
\mathscr{M}/\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\partial})
-measurable maps
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathcal{X}_{\partial}

such that
[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial}
is continuous and

X_{t}(\omega)=\partial
for any
t\in[\zeta(\omega),\infty]
for each
\omega\in\Omega
,
where
\zeta(\omega):=\inf\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid X_{t}(\omega)=\partial\}
, and

\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty]}
is a family of maps
\theta_{t}:\Omega\to\Omega

satisfying
X_{s}\circ\theta_{t}=X_{s+t}
for any
s,t\in[0,\infty]
. The pair
X

of such a stochastic process
\bigl(\Omega,\mathscr{M},\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty]}\bigr)

and a family
\{P_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial}}
of probability measures
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{M})
is then called a

diffusion without killing inside
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}))

if and only if
X
is a normal Markov process on

(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}))
whose minimum completed admissible filtration

\mathscr{F}_{*}=\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty]}
is
right-continuous

and which is
strong Markov
with respect to
\mathscr{F}_{*}
; see

[
37
, Section A.2, (M.2)–(M.5), the paragraph before Lemma A.2.2, and (A.2.3)]

for the precise definitions of these notions.
We set
E_{x}[(\cdot)]:=\int_{\Omega}(\cdot)\,dP_{x}
for
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial}
.
For each
\sigma
-finite Borel measure
\nu
on
\mathcal{X}_{\partial}
, the function

\mathcal{X}_{\partial}\ni x\mapsto P_{x}[B]
is measurable with respect to the
\nu
-completion
of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\partial})
for any
B\in\mathscr{F}_{\infty}

by
[
24
, Exercise A.1.20-(i)]
, and associated with
\nu
is a measure
P_{\nu}
on

(\Omega,\mathscr{F}_{\infty})
given by
P_{\nu}[B]:=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial}}P_{x}[B]\,d\nu(x)
.
We also set
\dot{\sigma}_{B}(\omega):=\inf\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid X_{t}(\omega)\in B\}
(
\inf\emptyset:=\infty
)
and
\tau_{B}(\omega):=\dot{\sigma}_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial}\setminus B}(\omega)

for
B\subset\mathcal{X}_{\partial}
and
\omega\in\Omega
, so that
\dot{\sigma}_{B},\tau_{B}
are

\mathscr{F}_{*}
-stopping times if
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\partial})
by
[
37
, Theorem A.2.3]
.

The most general form of heat kernel bounds we aim to establish in this section involves
three functions
v(r)
,
\Psi(r)
and
h(r)
as introduced below, which correspond to the functions

r^{\alpha}
,
r^{\beta}
and
\log(e+r^{-1})
in Theorem
2.1
,
respectively.

Assumption 2.2
.

Throughout the rest of Section
2
, we fix homeomorphisms

v,\Psi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
and a non-increasing continuous function
h:(0,\infty]\rightarrow[1,\infty)
with the properties that there exist positive constants
C_{v},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},C_{\Psi},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},C_{h},\varepsilon_{h}

with
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}
and
1<\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}
such that the following hold:
for any
s,t\in(0,\infty)
with
s\leq t
,

(2.7)

\displaystyle C_{v}^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{v(t)}{v(s)}\leq C_{v}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{\alpha_{2}},

(2.8)

\displaystyle C_{\Psi}^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Psi(t)}{\Psi(s)}\leq C_{\Psi}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{\beta_{2}},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\frac{h(s)}{h(t)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{\varepsilon_{h}}.

Note that (
2.8
) is equivalent to the property that for any
s,t\in(0,\infty)
with
s\leq t
,

(2.10)

\displaystyle C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{2}}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{1/\beta_{2}}\leq\frac{\Psi^{-1}(t)}{\Psi^{-1}(s)}\leq C_{\Psi}^{1/\beta_{1}}\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{1/\beta_{1}}.

Example 2.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
and
\beta\in(1,\infty)
. Then the triple of functions

v,\Psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
and
h:(0,\infty]\to[1,\infty)
given by

(2.11)

\displaystyle v(r):=r^{\alpha},\quad\Psi(r):=r^{\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad h(r):=\log(e+r^{-1})

satisfies Assumption
2.2
with
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha
,
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=\beta
,
arbitrary
\varepsilon_{h}\in(0,\infty)
and
C_{h}=2+e^{-1}\varepsilon_{h}^{-1}
.
Indeed, to see (
2.9
), let
\varepsilon_{h}\in(0,\infty)
and let
s,t\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
s\leq t
.
Then clearly
h(s)/h(t)\leq\log(2e)\leq 2(t/s)^{\varepsilon_{h}}
for
s\geq e^{-1}
, whereas for
s\leq e^{-1}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\log(e+s^{-1})}{\log(e+t^{-1})}

\displaystyle\;=\;1+\frac{\log\bigl(\frac{e+s^{-1}}{e+t^{-1}}\bigr)}{\log(e+t^{-1})}\;\leq\;1+\log\Bigl(\frac{e+s^{-1}}{e+t^{-1}}\Bigr)\;\leq\;1+\log\Bigl(et+\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;1+\log\frac{2t}{s}\;\leq\;2+\log\frac{t}{s}\;\leq\;(2+e^{-1}\varepsilon_{h}^{-1})\Bigl(\frac{t}{s}\Bigr)^{\varepsilon_{h}}.

For the sake of the applicability of the main results of this section
(Theorem
2.12
and Proposition
2.18
below)
to diffusions on random non-compact spaces and to strongly local regular
symmetric Dirichlet forms, we further introduce the following assumption.

Assumption 2.4
.

Throughout the rest of Section
2
, we fix
R\in(0,\infty]
, a non-empty
open subset
\mathcal{Y}
of
\mathcal{X}
, and a Borel subset
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{X}
with the property that

(2.12)

\displaystyle P_{x}[\dot{\sigma}_{\mathcal{N}}=\infty]=1\qquad\text{for any $x\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N}$.}

Our conditions
\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}
,
\mathrm{(E)}
and
\mathrm{(DU)}
for heat kernel bounds,
which we state next, concern the behavior of the measure
\mu
and the diffusion
X

only within metric balls contained in
\mathcal{Y}
with radii at most
R
, and hence
can often be verified even for diffusions on random non-compact spaces by choosing
R

to be finite and
\mathcal{Y}
to be bounded. The set
\mathcal{N}
can be considered as being removed
from the set of starting points of the diffusion
X
by virtue of (
2.12
),
and is thereby going to play the role of a set of
“capacity zero with respect to
X
”
;
we remark that the presence of such
\mathcal{N}
is inevitable in analyzing symmetric diffusions on
the basis of the general theory of regular symmetric Dirichlet forms presented in
[
37
,
24
]
,
as illustrated in Remark
2.8
below.

Definition 2.5

(Volume upper bound)
.

We say that condition
\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}
holds if there exist constants

\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}\in[0,\infty)
,
a_{\mathrm{v}}\in[1,\infty)
and
C_{\mathrm{V}}\in(0,\infty)
such that
for any
(x,r)\in(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{v}}}\bigr)
with
B(x,a_{\mathrm{v}}r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mu(B(x,r))\leq C_{\mathrm{V}}v(r)h(r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}}.

Definition 2.6

(Mean exit time estimate)
.

We say that the
mean exit time estimate

\mathrm{(E)}
holds if there exist constants

\kappa_{\mathrm{el}},\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}\in[0,\infty)
,
a_{\mathrm{e}}\in[1,\infty)
and
C_{\mathrm{e}}\in(0,\infty)

such that for any
(x,r)\in(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{e}}}\bigr)
with
B(x,a_{\mathrm{e}}r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
,

(2.14)

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}\Psi(r)h(r)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}}\leq E_{x}[\tau_{B(x,r)}]\leq C_{\mathrm{e}}\Psi(r)h(r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}}.

Definition 2.7

(On-diagonal upper bound)
.

We say that condition
\mathrm{(DU)}
holds if there exist constants
\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}\in[0,\infty)
,

a_{\mathrm{du}}\in[1,\infty)
and
C_{\mathrm{du}}\in(0,\infty)
such that for any

(x_{0},r)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{du}}}\bigr)
with
B(x_{0},a_{\mathrm{du}}r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
,
any
t\in(0,\Psi(r))
and any Borel subset
A
of
B(x_{0},r)
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle P_{x}[X_{t}\in A,\,t<\tau_{B(x_{0},r)}]\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{du}}}{v(\Psi^{-1}(t))}h(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}}\mu(A)\quad\text{for any $x\in B(x_{0},r)\setminus\mathcal{N}$.}

Remark 2.8
.

Assume that
X
is
\mu
-symmetric and associated with a regular symmetric Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mu)

(see
[
37
, Sections 1.1, 1.4, 4.1 and 4.2]
for the precise definitions of these notions).
In this case,
the validity of condition
\mathrm{(DU)}
for some Borel subset
\mathcal{N}

of
\mathcal{X}
satisfying
\mu(\mathcal{N})=0
and (
2.12
) follows from
\mathrm{(DU)}
with
“any
x\in B(x_{0},r)\setminus\mathcal{N}
” in (
2.15
) replaced by “
\mu
-a.e.
x\in B(x_{0},r)
”
.

Indeed, for each
(x_{0},r)\in\mathcal{Y}\times(0,\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{du}}})
with
B(x_{0},a_{\mathrm{du}}r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
,

[
40
, Theorem 5.4]
implies the existence of
\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},r}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})

satisfying
\mu(\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},r})=0
and (
2.12
) such that (
2.15
) with

\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},r}
in place of
\mathcal{N}
holds for all
t\in(0,\Psi(r))
and all Borel subset
A
of
B(x_{0},r)
.
Then, choosing a countable dense subset
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
of
\mathcal{Y}
, we see from
[
37
, Theorem 4.1.1]

that there exists
\mathcal{N}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
with the properties
\mu(\mathcal{N})=0
, (
2.12
) and

\bigcup_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}_{0},\,r\in(0,R/a_{\mathrm{du}})\cap\mathbb{Q},\,B(x_{0},a_{\mathrm{du}}r)\subset\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},r}\subset\mathcal{N}
.
Now it is elementary to see that
\mathrm{(DU)}
holds with this
\mathcal{N}
and the same
constants
a_{\mathrm{du}},C_{\mathrm{du}},\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}
.

2.2.
Off-diagonal upper bounds of the heat kernel

The statement of our main result on off-diagonal upper bounds of the heat kernel
(Theorem
2.12
below) requires the following definition.

Definition 2.9
.

For any
\kappa\in[0,\infty)
, we define a lower semi-continuous function

\Phi_{\kappa}:[0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty]
by

(2.16)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t):=\sup_{s\in(0,\infty)}\biggl(\frac{r}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})}-\frac{t}{s}\biggr),

so that for any
r,t\in(0,\infty)
,
\Phi_{\kappa}(\cdot,t)
is non-decreasing,

\Phi_{\kappa}(r,\cdot)
is non-increasing and
\Phi_{\kappa}(0,t)=0<\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t)<\infty

by the upper inequality in (
2.10
),
\beta_{1}>1
and the assumption
that
h
is
[1,\infty)
-valued and non-increasing.

Example 2.10
.

Let
\beta\in(1,\infty)
and assume that
\Psi(r)=r^{\beta}
for any
r\in[0,\infty)
.
Then an elementary differential calculus easily shows that
for any
(r,t)\in[0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(r,t)=c_{\beta}\biggl(\frac{r^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}},

where
c_{\beta}:=\beta^{-\beta/(\beta-1)}(\beta-1)=\beta^{-1/(\beta-1)}-\beta^{-\beta/(\beta-1)}
. On the other hand,
for each
\kappa\in[0,\infty)
, the effect of the correction term
h(s)^{\kappa}
in (
2.16
) can be estimated as

(2.18)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t)\geq c_{\beta}\biggl(\frac{r^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr)^{-\frac{\kappa}{\beta-1}}=\Phi_{0}(r,t)h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr)^{-\frac{\kappa}{\beta-1}}

(
t/0:=\infty
) for any
(r,t)\in[0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
.
Indeed, noting that (
2.18
) is obvious for
r=0
and
that
h
is
[1,\infty)
-valued and non-increasing, let
r,t\in(0,\infty)
and set

\displaystyle s:=\beta^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr)^{\frac{\kappa}{\beta-1}}\in\bigl[(t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}},\infty\bigr),

so that
h(s)\leq h\bigl((t/r)^{\beta/(\beta-1)}\bigr)
. Then by this last inequality and (
2.16
),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})}-\frac{t}{s}=\frac{r}{s^{1/\beta}h(s)^{\kappa/\beta}}-\frac{t}{s}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{r^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr)^{-\frac{\kappa}{\beta-1}}\biggl(\beta^{-\frac{1}{\beta-1}}h(s)^{-\kappa/\beta}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr)^{\kappa/\beta}-\beta^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\geq\biggl(\frac{r^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr)^{-\frac{\kappa}{\beta-1}}\Bigl(\beta^{-\frac{1}{\beta-1}}-\beta^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\Bigr),

proving (
2.18
).

In fact, the lower bound on the ratio
\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t)/\Phi_{0}(r,t)
exhibited in (
2.18
)
extends to the case of general
\Psi
, as follows.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\kappa\in[0,\infty)
. Then there exists
C_{\Phi_{\kappa}}\in(0,\infty)

such that for any
(r,t)\in[0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
,

(2.19)

\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t)\geq C_{\Phi_{\kappa}}\Phi_{0}(r,t)h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Bigr)^{-\frac{\kappa}{\beta_{1}-1}}.

Proof.

Set
\kappa^{\prime}:=(\beta_{1}-1)^{-1}\kappa
. (
2.19
) is obvious for
r=0
, so let
r,t\in(0,\infty)
,
and set
r^{\prime}:=C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{1}}r
. Since
\{sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}\mid s\in(0,\infty)\}=(0,\infty)

and
\Phi_{0}(r^{\prime},t)>0
, in view of (
2.16
) we can choose
s\in(0,\infty)
so that

(2.20)

\displaystyle\frac{r^{\prime}}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}})}-\frac{t}{sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}}>\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{0}(r^{\prime},t)>0.

Then by (
2.10
),
r^{\prime}=C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{1}}r
and
\kappa^{\prime}=(\beta_{1}-1)^{-1}\kappa
we have

(2.21)

\displaystyle\frac{r^{\prime}h(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}})}\leq\frac{C_{\Psi}^{1/\beta_{1}}r^{\prime}h(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})}\biggl(\frac{sh(s)^{\kappa}}{sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}}\biggr)^{1/\beta_{1}}=\frac{r}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})},

and therefore from
[
80
, Lemma 2.10]
(see also (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) below),
(
2.20
), (
2.21
) and (
2.16
) we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(r,t)h(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi}^{\frac{1}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Bigl(\frac{r}{r^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Phi_{0}(r^{\prime},t)h(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}\leq 2C_{\Psi}^{\frac{2}{\beta_{1}-1}}\biggl(\frac{r^{\prime}h(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}})}-\frac{t}{s}\biggr)

(2.22)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\Psi}^{\frac{2}{\beta_{1}-1}}\biggl(\frac{r}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})}-\frac{t}{s}\biggr)\leq 2C_{\Psi}^{\frac{2}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t).

On the other hand, setting
C_{\Psi}^{\prime}:=\bigl(C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{1}}\Psi^{-1}(1)\bigr)^{\beta_{1}/(\beta_{1}-1)}\wedge 1

and noting that

\displaystyle\frac{r^{\prime}}{\Psi^{-1}(u)}-\frac{t}{u}=\frac{t}{\Psi^{-1}(u)}\biggl(\frac{r^{\prime}}{t}-\frac{\Psi^{-1}(u)}{u}\biggr)\leq\frac{t}{\Psi^{-1}(u)}\biggl(\frac{r^{\prime}}{t}-\Bigl(\frac{C_{\Psi}^{\prime}}{u}\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta_{1}-1}{\beta_{1}}}\biggr)

for any
u\in(0,1]
by (
2.10
), we have

(2.23)

\displaystyle\frac{r^{\prime}}{\Psi^{-1}(u)}-\frac{t}{u}\leq 0\qquad\text{for any $u\in\biggl(0,C_{\Psi}^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{t}{r^{\prime}}\wedge 1\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\biggr]$.}

It then follows from (
2.23
) and (
2.20
) that

s\geq sh(s)^{-\kappa^{\prime}}>C_{\Psi}^{\prime}\bigl(\frac{t}{r^{\prime}}\wedge 1\bigr)^{\beta_{1}/(\beta_{1}-1)}
,
and hence further from (
2.9
) that, with

C_{\Psi,h}:=C_{h}\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(1)\wedge 1\bigr)^{-\varepsilon_{h}\beta_{1}/(\beta_{1}-1)}
,

\displaystyle h(s)\leq\begin{cases}h\Bigl(C_{\Psi}^{\prime}(t/r^{\prime})^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Bigr)\leq C_{\Psi,h}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Bigr)&\text{if $r^{\prime}\geq t$,}\\
h(C_{\Psi}^{\prime})\leq h(C_{\Psi}^{\prime})h(\infty)^{-1}h\Bigl((t/r)^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Bigr)&\text{if $r^{\prime}\leq t$,}\end{cases}

which together with (
2.22
) shows (
2.19
).
∎

Now we can state the main result of this subsection; note that by Lemma
2.11
we can replace

\exp\bigl(-c_{2}\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(d(x,y)\wedge R_{y},t\bigr)\bigr)
in the right-hand side of (
2.24
) below by

\displaystyle\exp\Biggl(-c_{2}C_{\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}\Phi_{0}\bigl(d(x,y)\wedge R_{y},t\bigr)h\biggl(\Bigl(\frac{t}{d(x,y)\wedge R_{y}}\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\biggr)^{-\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}{\beta_{1}-1}}\Biggr).

Theorem 2.12

(Off-diagonal upper bound)
.

Assume
\mathrm{(E)}
,
\mathrm{(DU)}
and that
\overline{B}(x,r)
is compact
for any
(x,r)\in(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times(0,\frac{R}{2a_{\mathrm{e}}})
with
B(x,2(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
.
Set
\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}:=(2+\beta_{2})(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})
and
assume that
\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}<1
. Then there exists a Borel measurable function

p=p_{t}(x,y):(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow[0,\infty)

such that the following hold:

(i)

For any
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N})
,

\displaystyle P_{x}[X_{t}\in dy]=p_{t}(x,y)\,\mu(dy).

(ii)

There exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)

such that, with

\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}^{\prime}:=(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha_{2}/\beta_{1})
,
for any
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\mathcal{Y}
,

(2.24)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,y)\leq\frac{c_{1}h\bigl(t\wedge\Psi(R_{y})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}^{\prime}}}{v\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(t)\wedge R_{y}\bigr)}\exp\Bigl(-c_{2}\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(d(x,y)\wedge R_{y},t\bigr)\Bigr),

where
R_{y}:=R\wedge\inf_{z\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{Y}}d(y,z)

(
\inf\emptyset:=\infty
)

and
\Psi(\infty):=\infty
.

The proof of Theorem
2.12
is concluded at the end of this subsection. For this purpose,
we need some preliminary results on basic properties of
\Phi_{\kappa}
and on upper bounds on

P_{x}[\tau_{B(x,r)}\leq t]
. First, for
\Phi_{\kappa}
we have the following lemma,
which asserts that, provided
\varepsilon_{h}\kappa<1
, the function

\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})
in (
2.16
) is comparable to
\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(s)

for some homeomorphism
\Psi_{\kappa}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
that still satisfies
(
2.8
) (for some different constants) even though
\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})

might not be itself strictly increasing.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\kappa\in[0,\varepsilon_{h}^{-1})
and define a homeomorphism

\Psi_{\kappa}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by

(2.25)

\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(t):=\sup_{s\in(0,t]}\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})+\Psi^{-1}(t),

which can be defined since
\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})=0
by

\varepsilon_{h}\kappa<1
and (
2.9
). Then there exists
C>0
such that

(2.26)

C\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(t)\leq\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})\leq\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(t)\qquad\text{for any $t\in(0,\infty)$,}

and
\Psi_{\kappa}
satisfies (
2.8
) with

\beta_{2}
replaced by
\beta_{\kappa,2}:=(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa)^{-1}\beta_{2}
and

C_{\Psi}
by some
C_{\Psi,\kappa}\in(0,\infty)
. Moreover, with
\beta_{\kappa,1}:=\beta_{1}
and

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}(r,t):=\sup_{s\in(0,\infty)}\bigl(r/\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(s)-t/s\bigr)
for

(r,t)\in[0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
, there exists
C^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
such that for any
r,t,s\in(0,\infty)
with
s\leq r
,

(2.27)

\displaystyle C^{\prime-1}\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\biggl(\frac{\Psi_{\kappa}(r)}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{\kappa,j}-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}(r,t)\leq C^{\prime}\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\biggl(\frac{\Psi_{\kappa}(r)}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{\kappa,j}-1}},

(2.28)

\displaystyle C^{\prime-1}\Bigl(\frac{r}{s}\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta_{\kappa,2}}{\beta_{\kappa,2}-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}(r,t)}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}(s,t)}\leq C^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r}{s}\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta_{\kappa,1}}{\beta_{\kappa,1}-1}},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}(r,t)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi_{\kappa}(r,t)\leq C^{\prime}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa}(r,t).

Proof.

Let
t\in(0,\infty)
. Clearly
\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})\leq\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(t)
.
By (
2.10
) and (
2.9
),
for any
s\in(0,t]
, if
sh(s)^{\kappa}\geq th(t)^{\kappa}
then

(2.30)

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{s}{t}\Bigr)^{1/\beta_{1}}\leq 1\leq\frac{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})}{\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})}\leq c\biggl(\frac{sh(s)^{\kappa}}{th(t)^{\kappa}}\biggr)^{1/\beta_{1}}\leq cC_{h}^{\kappa}\Bigl(\frac{s}{t}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa}{\beta_{1}}}\leq cC_{h}^{\kappa}\Bigl(\frac{s}{t}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa}{\beta_{2}}},

and if
sh(s)^{\kappa}\leq th(t)^{\kappa}
then

(2.31)

\displaystyle c^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{s}{t}\Bigr)^{1/\beta_{1}}\leq c^{-1}\biggl(\frac{sh(s)^{\kappa}}{th(t)^{\kappa}}\biggr)^{1/\beta_{1}}\leq\frac{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})}{\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})}\leq c\biggl(\frac{sh(s)^{\kappa}}{th(t)^{\kappa}}\biggr)^{1/\beta_{2}}\leq cC_{h}^{\kappa}\Bigl(\frac{s}{t}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa}{\beta_{2}}}.

In particular, we see from (
2.30
) and (
2.31
) that

\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})\leq cC_{h}^{\kappa}\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})
for any
s\in(0,t]
and hence that

\Psi_{\kappa}^{-1}(t)\leq 2\sup_{s\in(0,t]}\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})\leq cC_{h}^{\kappa}\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})
,
proving (
2.26
). It follows from (
2.30
), (
2.31
) and
(
2.26
) that
\Psi_{\kappa}
satisfies (
2.8
) with
\beta_{2}
replaced by

\beta_{\kappa,2}=(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa)^{-1}\beta_{2}
and
C_{\Psi}
by some constant
C_{\Psi,\kappa}\in(0,\infty)
.
Finally, we have (
2.27
) by
[
41
, Lemma 3.19]
and

[
40
, Lemma 5.7]
, (
2.28
) by
[
80
, Lemma 2.10]
, and
then (
2.29
) by (
2.26
) and
(
2.28
).
∎

Next, we prove some upper bounds on
P_{x}[\tau_{B(x,r)}\leq t]
, which is a key condition
for applying
[
40
, Theorems 6.2 and 6.4]
to conclude Theorem
2.12
.
For any open set
U\subset\mathcal{X}
we set

\displaystyle\overline{E}(U)\;\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\;\sup_{x\in U\setminus\mathcal{N}}E_{x}[\tau_{U}].

Lemma 2.14
.

For any open subset
U
of
\mathcal{X}
with
\overline{E}(U)<\infty

and any
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N})
,

\displaystyle P_{x}[\tau_{U}<t]\leq 1-\frac{E_{x}[\tau_{U}]}{\overline{E}(U)}+\frac{t}{\overline{E}(U)}.

Proof.

See
[
41
, Lemma 3.12]
.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Assume that condition
\mathrm{(E)}
holds, and set
\kappa_{1}:=\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}
.
Then there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that for any

(x,r)\in(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{2a_{\mathrm{e}}}\bigr)
with

B(x,(2a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
and any
\lambda\in[c_{1}h(r)^{2\kappa_{1}}/\Psi(r),\infty)
,

\displaystyle E_{x}\bigl[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}\bigr]\leq 1-c_{2}h(r)^{-\kappa_{1}}.

Proof.

For
r
and
x
as in the statement set
B\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B(x,r)
. Then, by Lemma
2.14
we have for all
t,\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle E_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B}}\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;E_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{B}<t\}}\big]+E_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{B}\geq t\}}\big]\;\leq\;P_{x}\big[\tau_{B}<t\big]+e^{-\lambda t}

\displaystyle\;\leq\ 1-\frac{E_{x}[\tau_{B}]}{\overline{E}(B)}+\frac{t}{\overline{E}(B)}+e^{-\lambda t}.

Next, note that for any
y\in B\setminus\mathcal{N}
we have
B(y,2a_{\mathrm{e}}r)\subset B(x,(2a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
and therefore by condition
\mathrm{(E)}
we obtain

\overline{E}(B)\geq E_{x}[\tau_{B}]\geq C_{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}\Psi(r)h(r)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}}
and

\displaystyle\overline{E}(B)

\displaystyle\;=\sup_{y\in B\setminus\mathcal{N}}E_{y}\big[\tau_{B}\big]\;\leq\sup_{y\in B\setminus\mathcal{N}}E_{y}\big[\tau_{B(y,2r)}\big]\;\leq\;C_{\mathrm{e}}\,\Psi(2r)\,h(2r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;c\,\Psi(r)\,h(r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}}\;\leq\;c\,h(r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}}\,E_{x}\big[\tau_{B}\big].

Hence, setting
\kappa_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}}
we get

\displaystyle E_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}\big]\;\leq\;1-c_{3}\,h(r)^{-\kappa_{1}}+c_{4}\,t\,\Psi(r)^{-1}\,h(r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}}+e^{-\lambda t}.

Now choose
t
such that
c_{3}h(r)^{-\kappa_{1}}=2c_{4}\,t\,\Psi(r)^{-1}\,h(r)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}}
, so that

\displaystyle E_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}\big]\;\leq\;1-\frac{1}{2}c_{3}h(r)^{-\kappa_{1}}+e^{-\lambda t}.

Finally, note that for all
\lambda\geq c_{1}h(r)^{2\kappa_{1}}/\Psi(r)
with
c_{1}>0
sufficiently large and
t
as chosen above we have
e^{-\lambda t}\leq\frac{1}{4}c_{3}h(r)^{-\kappa_{1}}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.16
.

Assume that condition
\mathrm{(E)}
holds, and set

\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(2+\beta_{2})(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})
.
Then there exist
C_{5},\gamma\in(0,\infty)
such that, for any

(x,r)\in(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{2a_{\mathrm{e}}}\bigr)
with
\overline{B}(x,r)

compact and
B(x,2(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
, and for any
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle E_{x}\bigl[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}\bigr]\leq C_{5}\exp\biggl(-\frac{\gamma r}{\Psi^{-1}\bigl(\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigr)}\biggr).

Proof.

We follow the arguments in
[
41
, Lemma 3.14]
.

Step 1.
Let
\lambda>0
, fix some
\rho<r
to be specified later and set
n=\lfloor\frac{r}{\rho}\rfloor
. Further, set
\tau\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tau_{B(x,r)}
and

\displaystyle u(y)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{y}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau}\big],\qquad m_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sup_{\overline{B}(x,k\rho)\setminus\mathcal{N}}u,\qquad k=1,2,\ldots,n.

For abbreviation define
\varepsilon\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=C_{2}h(\rho)^{-\kappa_{1}}
(cf. Lemma
2.15
) and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varepsilon/2
. Let
y_{k}
be a point in
\overline{B}(x,k\rho)\setminus\mathcal{N}
such that

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\,m_{k}\;\leq\;u(y_{k})\;\leq\;m_{k}\;\leq\;1.

For
k\leq n-1
notice that
B(y_{k},\rho)\subset B(x,(k+1)\rho)\subset B(x,r)
.
Consider the function
v_{k}(y)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{y}[e^{-\lambda\tau_{k}}]
defined for
y\in B(y_{k},\rho)

where
\tau_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tau_{B(y_{k},\rho)}
. Then, since
\overline{B}(x,r)
is compact,
by the continuity of
[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial}
for each

\omega\in\Omega
, for all
y\in B(y_{k},\rho)\setminus\mathcal{N}
we have

X_{\tau_{k}}\in\overline{B}(y_{k},\rho)\subset\overline{B}(x,(k+1)\rho)

P_{y}
-a.s. on
\{\tau_{k}<\infty\}
.
By the strong Markov property
[
24
, Theorem A.1.21]
of
X
,

\displaystyle u(y)

\displaystyle\;=\;E_{y}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{k}}e^{-\lambda(\tau-\tau_{k})}\big]\;=\;E_{y}\Big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{k}}E_{X_{\tau_{k}}}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau}\big]\Big]

\displaystyle\;=\;E_{y}\big[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{k}<\infty,X_{\tau_{k}}\in\overline{B}(x,(k+1)\rho)\setminus\mathcal{N}\}}e^{-\lambda\tau_{k}}u(X_{\tau_{k}})\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;E_{y}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{k}}\big]\,\sup_{\overline{B}(x,(k+1)\rho)\setminus\mathcal{N}}u\;=\;v_{k}(y)\,m_{k+1}.

In particular, by choosing
y=y_{k}
, we get
u(y_{k})\leq v_{k}(y_{k})m_{k+1}
and therefore

(2.33)

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\,m_{k}\;\leq\;v_{k}(y_{k})\,m_{k+1}.

If additionally

(2.34)

\displaystyle\lambda\;\geq\;C_{1}\frac{h(\rho)^{2\kappa_{1}}}{\Psi(\rho)},

since
B(y_{k},(2a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)\rho)\subset B(x,2(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
we may apply Lemma
2.15
to
B(y_{k},\rho)
and obtain that
v_{k}(y_{k})\leq 1-\varepsilon
, which combined with (
2.33
) shows that

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\,m_{k}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\,m_{k+1},\qquad\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}.

By iteration we get that

\displaystyle u(x)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;m_{1}\;\leq\;\Big(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Big)^{n-1}\,m_{n}\;\leq\;\Big(\frac{1-2\varepsilon^{\prime}}{1-\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Big)^{n-1}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\exp\Big(-(n-1)\log\Big(1+\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{1-2\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Big)\Big)\;\leq\;\exp\big(-(n-1)\log(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\big),

where we used in the last step that
m_{n}\leq 1
. Since we may assume that
C_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and therefore
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and since
n\geq\frac{r}{\rho}-1
,
using the definition of
\varepsilon^{\prime}
we obtain that

(2.35)

\displaystyle u(x)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\exp\Big(-(n-1)\log\big(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\big)\Big)\;\leq\;C\exp\Big(-C\frac{r}{\rho h(\rho)^{\kappa_{1}}}\Big)

provided (
2.34
) is satisfied.

Step 2: Choice of
\rho
.
In order to choose an appropriate
\rho\in(0,r)
, set
\kappa_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=2\kappa_{1}
and
C_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=C_{\Psi}^{-1}\vee(C_{1}h(\Psi^{-1}(1))^{\kappa_{0}})
, where
C_{\Psi}>0
is such that
\Psi^{-1}(t)\geq C_{\Psi}(t^{1/\beta_{1}}\wedge t^{1/\beta_{2}})
for all
t>0
. Let
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{0}})
. We claim that for this choice of
\rho
,

\displaystyle\lambda\;\geq\;C_{1}\,\frac{h(\rho)^{\kappa_{0}}}{\Psi(\rho)},

in particular, (
2.34
) holds. To see this, let us first consider the case
\lambda\geq C_{0}
. Then, since
\beta_{2}\geq\beta_{1}>1
and

(2.36)

\displaystyle\rho\geq\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1})\geq C_{\Psi}\left((C_{0}\lambda^{-1})^{1/\beta_{1}}\wedge(C_{0}\lambda^{-1})^{1/\beta_{2}}\right)\geq C_{\Psi}\,C_{0}\,\lambda^{-1}\geq\lambda^{-1},

we have

\displaystyle\frac{h(\rho)^{\kappa_{0}}}{\Psi(\rho)}=\frac{\lambda}{C_{0}}\,h(\lambda^{-1})^{-\kappa_{0}}h(\rho)^{\kappa_{0}}\leq\frac{\lambda}{C_{0}}\leq\frac{\lambda}{C_{3}}.

On the other hand, if
\lambda\leq C_{0}
, then

(2.37)

\displaystyle\rho\geq\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1})\geq\Psi^{-1}(1)

and hence

\displaystyle\frac{h(\rho)^{\kappa_{0}}}{\Psi(\rho)}\leq\frac{\lambda}{C_{0}}\,h(\Psi^{-1}(1))^{-\kappa_{0}}h(\rho)^{\kappa_{0}}\leq\frac{\lambda}{C_{3}}.

Thus, (
2.34
) is satisfied for this choice of
\rho
and by (
2.35
) we have that

(2.38)

\displaystyle E_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}\big]\;\leq\;C\exp\Big(-C\frac{r}{\rho h(\rho)^{\kappa_{1}}}\Big)

provided
\rho<r
.

Step 3: Conclusion.
In order to deduce the desired inequality let us assume first that
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{0}})<r
. We need an upper estimate on
\rho h(\rho)^{\kappa_{1}}
.

Let us again consider the case
\lambda\geq C_{0}
first. Then, by (
2.36
) we get

\displaystyle\rho\,h(\rho)^{\kappa_{1}}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\rho\,h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{1}}\;=\;\Psi^{-1}\big(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1}\big)^{\kappa_{0}})\,h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{1}}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;C\,\Psi^{-1}\big(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1}\big)^{\kappa_{0}+\beta_{2}\kappa_{1}})\,\bigg(\frac{C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1}\big)^{\kappa_{0}}}{C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1}\big)^{\kappa_{0}+\beta_{2}\kappa_{1}}}\bigg)^{\!\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{1}}

(2.39)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;C\,\Psi^{-1}\big(\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\big)

with
\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\kappa_{0}+\beta_{2}\kappa_{1}=(2+\beta_{2})(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})
,
where we used that
\Psi^{-1}(r_{2})/\Psi^{-1}(r_{1})\leq C(r_{2}/r_{1})^{1/\beta_{2}}
for any
0<r_{1}\leq r_{2}
.

On the other hand, if
\lambda\leq C_{0}
, then
\rho\geq\Psi^{-1}(1)
by (
2.37
) and therefore

\displaystyle\rho\,h(\rho)^{\kappa_{1}}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\rho\,h(\Psi^{-1}(1))^{\kappa_{1}}\;=\;\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{0}})\,h(\Psi^{-1}(1))^{\kappa_{1}}

(2.40)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;C\,\Psi^{-1}\big(\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1}\big)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}).

The claim now follows combining (
2.38
) with (
2.2
) and (
2.2
).

Now assume that
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{0}})\geq r
. Then,

\displaystyle r\;\leq\;\Psi^{-1}(C_{0}\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{0}})\;\leq\;C\,\Psi^{-1}\big(\lambda^{-1}h(\lambda^{-1})^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\big),

and the desired estimate follows from
e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}\leq 1
by adjusting the constants
\gamma
and
C_{5}
.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

Suppose that condition
\mathrm{(E)}
holds, and set

\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(2+\beta_{2})(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})
.
Then there exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for any

(x,r)\in(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{N})\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{2a_{\mathrm{e}}}\bigr)
with
\overline{B}(x,r)

compact and
B(x,2(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
, and for any
t\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle P_{x}[\tau_{B(x,r)}\leq t]\;\leq\;c_{1}\exp\bigl(-\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(c_{2}r,t)\bigr).

Proof.

For
x
and
r
as in the statement and any
s,t\in(0,\infty)
, by Proposition
2.16

\displaystyle P_{x}[\tau_{B(x,r)}\leq t]\;=\;P_{x}\big[e^{-\tau_{B(x,r)}/s}\geq e^{-t/s}\big]\;

\displaystyle\leq\;e^{t/s}\,E_{x}\big[e^{-\tau_{B(x,r)}/s}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\;C_{5}\exp\biggl(\frac{t}{s}-\frac{cr}{\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})}\biggr).

Now the assertion follows by taking the infimum in
s\in(0,\infty)
of the right-hand side of
this inequality and recalling the definition (
2.16
) of
\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.12
.

If
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}
and
R=\infty
, then by Lemma
2.13
along with

\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}<1
, Corollary
2.17
along with
(\mathrm{E})
, and

\mathrm{(DU)}
along with
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\leq\Psi
, all the assumptions of
[
40
, Theorem 6.4]

with
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
in place of
\Psi
are satisfied, and hence
[
40
, Theorem 6.4]

together with (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) yields the assertions.

Thus we may assume that either
\mathcal{Y}\not=\mathcal{X}
or
R<\infty
holds,
so that
R_{y}\in(0,\infty)
for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
Let
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable dense subset of
\mathcal{Y}
.
For each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
set
R_{y}^{\prime}:=R_{y}/\bigl((2a_{\mathrm{du}})\vee(4a_{\mathrm{e}}+4)\bigr)
, so that

R_{y}^{\prime}\in(0,\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{du}}})
,
B(y,a_{\mathrm{du}}R_{y}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{Y}
,

R_{y}^{\prime}\in(0,\frac{R}{2a_{\mathrm{e}}})
and
B(x,2(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}

for any
(x,r)\in B(y,R_{y}^{\prime}/2)\times(0,R_{y}^{\prime})
. Therefore for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
Lemma
2.13
along with
\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}<1
,
Corollary
2.17
along with
(\mathrm{E})
, and

\mathrm{(DU)}
along with
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\leq\Psi

together imply that the open subset
B(y_{n},R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}/2)
of
\mathcal{X}
and the function
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}

satisfy all the assumptions of
[
40
, Theorem 6.2]
, and hence we see from
[
40
, (6.4) and (6.5)]
,
(
2.27
), (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) that for any

(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N})
and any Borel subset
A
of
B(y_{n},R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}/4)
,

\displaystyle P_{x}[X_{t}\in A]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A}\frac{ch\bigl(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}}}{v\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}))\bigr)}\exp\Bigl(-\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(c\bigl(d(x,y)\wedge R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}\bigr),t\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mu(dy)

(2.41)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A}\frac{ch\bigl(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}}}{v\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}))\bigr)}\exp\Bigl(-c_{2}\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(d(x,y)\wedge R_{y},t\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mu(dy).

On the other hand, for any
t\in(0,\infty)
,

C\Psi(t)\leq\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(t)h(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(t))^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\leq\Psi(t)

by (
2.26
) and (
2.8
), hence

h(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(t))\leq h\bigl(C\Psi(t)h(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(t))^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigr)\leq Ch(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(t))^{\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}h(\Psi(t))

by (
2.9
), therefore

h(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(t))\leq Ch(\Psi(t))^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}
and thus

(2.42)

h\bigl(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime})\bigr)\leq Ch\bigl(\Psi(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}(t))\wedge\Psi(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}\leq C^{\prime}h\bigl(t\wedge\Psi(R_{y})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}

for any
y\in B(y_{n},R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}/4)
by
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}\geq\Psi^{-1}
,
(
2.9
) and (
2.8
). Also for any

t\in(0,\infty)
, by
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}\geq\Psi^{-1}
, (
2.7
),
(
2.26
) and (
2.10
) we have

\frac{v(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}(t))}{v(\Psi^{-1}(t))}\leq C_{v}\biggl(\frac{\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}(t)}{\Psi^{-1}(t)}\biggr)^{\alpha_{2}}\leq C^{\prime}\biggl(\frac{\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})}{\Psi^{-1}(t)}\biggr)^{\alpha_{2}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}h(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha_{2}/\beta_{1}},

and therefore for any
y\in B(y_{n},R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}/4)
we further obtain

(2.43)

\frac{1}{v\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}))\bigr)}\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}h\bigl(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha_{2}/\beta_{1}}}{v\bigl(\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}(t)\wedge R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}\bigr)}\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime\prime}h\bigl(t\wedge\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(R_{y_{n}}^{\prime})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha_{2}/\beta_{1}}}{v\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(t)\wedge R_{y}\bigr)}

by
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}\geq\Psi^{-1}
and (
2.7
). Combining
(
2.41
), (
2.42
) and (
2.43
),
for any
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{N})
and
any Borel subset
A
of
B(y_{n},R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}/4)
we get

(2.44)

P_{x}[X_{t}\in A]\leq\int_{A}\frac{c_{1}h\bigl(t\wedge\Psi(R_{y})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}^{\prime}}}{v\bigl(\Psi^{-1}(t)\wedge R_{y}\bigr)}\exp\Bigl(-c_{2}\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(d(x,y)\wedge R_{y},t\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mu(dy),

which then holds for any Borel subset
A
of
\mathcal{Y}
since

\mathcal{Y}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}B(y_{n},R_{y_{n}}^{\prime}/4)
. Now the assertions
follow from (
2.44
) and
[
40
, Proposition 5.6]
.
∎

2.3.
On-diagonal lower bounds of the heat kernel

In the present general setting, our result on on-diagonal lower bounds of the heat kernel
is formulated as in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.18

(On-diagonal lower bound)
.

Assume
\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}
and
\mathrm{(E)}
, set

\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}:=(2+\beta_{2})(\kappa_{\mathrm{el}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{eu}})

and assume
\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}<1
. Also set

\kappa_{\mathrm{dl}}:=\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}+\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha_{2}/\beta_{1}

and
\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}:=(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}})^{-1}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}
.
Then there exist
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}\in(0,1)
and
C_{\mathrm{dl}}\in(0,\infty)
such that,
for any
(x_{0},r)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\bigl(0,\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{v}}\vee a_{\mathrm{e}}}\bigr)
with

\overline{B}(x_{0},r)
compact and
B\bigl(x_{0},((a_{\mathrm{v}}\vee a_{\mathrm{e}})+2)r\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Y}
,
and for any
(t,x)\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}\Psi(r)h(\Psi(r))^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}}]\times(B(x_{0},r/2)\setminus\mathcal{N})

with
P_{x}[X_{t}\in dy,\,t<\tau_{B(x_{0},r)}]=p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}(y)\,\mu(dy)

for some Borel measurable function
p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}:B(x_{0},r)\to[0,\infty)
,

(2.45)

\displaystyle\int_{B(x_{0},r)}p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}(y)^{2}\,\mu(dy)\geq\frac{C_{\mathrm{dl}}}{v(\Psi^{-1}(t))}h(t)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{dl}}}.

We need the following lemma for the proof of Proposition
2.18
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\kappa\in[0,\varepsilon_{h}^{-1})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then there exists
\varepsilon\in(0,1)

such that
th(t)^{\kappa}\leq\Psi(\frac{\delta}{2}r)
for any
r,t\in(0,\infty)

with
t\leq\varepsilon\Psi(r)h(\Psi(r))^{-\kappa/(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa)}
.

Proof.

Set
\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}:=(1-\varepsilon_{h}\kappa)^{-1}\kappa
.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, which we will choose to be sufficiently small later in this proof,
and let
r,t\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
t\leq\varepsilon\Psi(r)h(\Psi(r))^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}}
.
Then, setting
s:=\Psi(r)h(\Psi(r))^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}}\in(t,\Psi(r)]
, by (
2.9
) we have

(2.46)

\displaystyle sh(s)^{\kappa}\leq s\bigl(C_{h}h(\Psi(r))(\Psi(r)/s)^{\varepsilon_{h}}\bigr)^{\kappa}=C_{h}^{\kappa}\Psi(r).

Therefore we see from (
2.30
), (
2.31
),

t\leq\varepsilon s
, (
2.46
) and (
2.10
) that

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa})\leq C(t/s)^{1/\beta_{2,\kappa}}\Psi^{-1}(sh(s)^{\kappa})\leq C\varepsilon^{1/\beta_{2,\kappa}}\Psi^{-1}(C_{h}^{\kappa}\Psi(r))\leq C^{\prime}\varepsilon^{1/\beta_{2,\kappa}}r

for a constant
C^{\prime}\in[1,\infty)
independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and hence
the assertion follows by assuming that
\varepsilon
has been chosen to be

\varepsilon:=(\delta/(2C^{\prime}))^{\beta_{2,\kappa}}\in(0,1)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.18
.

We follow the standard argument for proving on-diagonal lower bounds as presented, e.g.,
in
[
41
, Proof of Lemma 5.13]
. Set
\kappa:=\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\in[0,\varepsilon_{h}^{-1})
,
let
\delta\in(0,1)
, which we will choose to be sufficiently small later in this proof,
let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be as in Lemma
2.19
and set

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}:=\varepsilon
. Let
(x_{0},r),(x,t)
be as in the statement
and set
\rho:=\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})/\delta
, so that
\rho\in(0,\frac{r}{2}]

by the property of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}=\varepsilon
from Lemma
2.19
.
Then
C\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}^{-1}(t)\leq\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})=\delta\rho

by (
2.26
), hence

t\leq\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(C^{-1}\delta\rho)\leq C^{\prime}\delta^{\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}}\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(c_{2}\rho)

by (
2.8
) for
\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
proved in Lemma
2.13
,
with
c_{2}
as in Corollary
2.17
, and therefore

(2.47)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(c_{2}\rho,t)\geq\widetilde{\Phi}_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(c_{2}\rho,t)\geq C^{\prime\prime}\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\biggl(\frac{\Psi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(c_{2}\rho)}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},j}-1}}\geq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\delta^{-\frac{\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}}{\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}-1}}

by (
2.29
) and (
2.27
).
On the other hand,
\rho\leq\frac{r}{2}<\frac{R}{2a_{\mathrm{e}}}
,
the compactness of
\overline{B}(x_{0},r)
implies that of
\overline{B}(x,\rho)

by
\overline{B}(x,\rho)\subset\overline{B}(x_{0},r)
, and we also have

B(x,2(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)\rho)\subset B(x,(a_{\mathrm{e}}+1)r)\subset B(x_{0},(a_{\mathrm{e}}+2)r)\subset\mathcal{Y}
,
so that by Corollary
2.17
and (
2.47
) we get

(2.48)

\displaystyle P_{x}[\tau_{B(x,\rho)}\leq t]\leq c_{1}\exp\bigl(-\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}(c_{2}\rho,t)\bigr)\leq c_{1}\exp\Bigl(-C^{\prime\prime\prime}\delta^{-\frac{\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}}{\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}-1}}\Bigr)

with
c_{1}
as in Corollary
2.17
. Since
c_{1},C^{\prime\prime\prime}
in (
2.48
)
are independent of
\delta\in(0,1)
, we may assume that
\delta
has been chosen to satisfy

c_{1}\exp\bigl(-C^{\prime\prime\prime}\delta^{-\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}/(\beta_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}},2}-1)}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
and then by (
2.48
) we have

(2.49)

\displaystyle P_{x}[t<\tau_{B(x,\rho)}]=1-P_{x}[\tau_{B(x,\rho)}\leq t]\geq\frac{1}{2}.

It therefore follows from
B(x,\rho)\subset B(x_{0},r)
and (
2.49
) that

\displaystyle\int_{B(x_{0},r)}p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}(y)^{2}\,\mu(dy)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{B(x,\rho)}p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}(y)^{2}\,\mu(dy)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,\rho))}\biggl(\int_{B(x,\rho)}p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}(y)\,\mu(dy)\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,\rho))}P_{x}[X_{t}\in B(x,\rho),\,t<\tau_{B(x_{0},r)}]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,\rho))}P_{x}[t<\tau_{B(x,\rho)}]^{2}

(2.50)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4\mu(B(x,\rho))}.

Finally, noting that
\Psi^{-1}(t)\leq\Psi^{-1}(th(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})/\delta=\rho\leq\frac{r}{2}<\frac{R}{a_{\mathrm{v}}}
and that

B(x,a_{\mathrm{v}}\rho)\subset B(x_{0},(a_{\mathrm{v}}+1)\frac{r}{2})\subset\mathcal{Y}
, we see from

\mathrm{(V)}_{\leq}
, (
2.7
) and (
2.10
) that

\displaystyle\mu(B(x,\rho))\leq C_{\mathrm{V}}v(\rho)h(\rho)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{V}}C_{v}v(\Psi^{-1}(t))h(\Psi^{-1}(t))^{\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\Psi^{-1}(t)}\Bigr)^{\alpha_{2}}

(2.51)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}v(\Psi^{-1}(t))h(\Psi^{-1}(t))^{\kappa_{\mathrm{vu}}}h(t)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}\alpha_{2}/\beta_{1}}.

Now (
2.45
) follows from (
2.50
), (
2.51
)
and the fact that
h(\Psi^{-1}(t))\leq h\bigl(C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{2}}\bigr)\leq h\bigl(C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{2}}\bigr)h(t)

by (
2.10
) if
t\geq 1
and

h(\Psi^{-1}(t))\leq h\bigl(C_{\Psi}^{-1/\beta_{1}}\Psi^{-1}(1)t^{1/\beta_{1}}\bigr)\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime\prime\prime}h(t)

by (
2.10
) and (
2.9
) if
t\leq 1
.
∎

2.4.
Proof of Theorem
2.1

We conclude this section with deducing Theorem
2.1

from Theorem
2.12
and Proposition
2.18
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Define
v,\Psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
and
h:(0,\infty]\to[1,\infty)
by (
2.11
),
so that Assumption
2.2
holds with arbitrary
\varepsilon_{h}\in(0,\infty)

by Example
2.3
. Set
R:=2\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X}\in(0,\infty)
,
\mathcal{Y}:=\mathcal{X}
and

\mathcal{N}:=\emptyset
, which satisfy Assumption
2.4
.

For (i), since
R_{y}=R\wedge\inf_{z\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{Y}}d(y,z)=2\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X}
for any
y\in\mathcal{X}
, by Theorem
2.12

there exist a Borel measurable function
\tilde{p}=\tilde{p}_{t}(x,y):(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to[0,\infty)

and
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that for each
(t,x)\in(0,(\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})^{\beta}]\times\mathcal{X}
, for
\mu
-a.e.
y\in\mathcal{X}
,

(2.52)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,y)=\tilde{p}_{t}(x,y)\leq c_{1}t^{-\alpha/\beta}\bigl(\log(e+t^{-1})\bigr)^{\kappa_{\mathrm{du}}^{\prime}}\exp\Bigl(-c_{2}\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(d(x,y),t\bigr)\Bigr).

Then by the continuity of
p_{t}(x,\cdot)
and the lower semi-continuity of
\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
,
the upper bound on
p_{t}(x,y)
in (
2.52
) extends to any

(t,x,y)\in(0,(\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})^{\beta}]\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
, from which we obtain (
2.5
)
by noting that (
2.18
) in Example
2.10
yields

\displaystyle\Phi_{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\bigl(d(x,y),t\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq c_{\beta}\biggl(\frac{d(x,y)^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}\biggl(\log\biggl(e+\Bigl(\frac{d(x,y)}{t}\Bigr)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\biggr)\biggr)^{-\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}{\beta-1}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{\beta}(1-\beta^{-1})^{\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}{\beta-1}}\biggl(\frac{d(x,y)^{\beta}}{t}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}\biggl(\log\biggl(e+\frac{d(x,y)}{t}\biggr)\biggr)^{-\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}{\beta-1}}.

For (ii), note first that for any
(x_{0},r),(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}\times(0,\infty)
a function

p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}
as in Proposition
2.18
exists and satisfies

p^{B(x_{0},r)}_{t,x}\leq p_{t}(x,\cdot)

\mu
-a.e. on
B(x_{0},r)

by (
2.1
) and the Radon–Nikodym theorem. Therefore,
choosing any
\varepsilon_{h}\in(0,\infty)
with
\varepsilon_{h}\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}<1
,
we immediately see that all the assumptions of Proposition
2.18
are satisfied and thus that,
with
\kappa_{\mathrm{t}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}},C_{\mathrm{dl}}
as in Proposition
2.18
,
(
2.45
) holds for any
(x_{0},r)\in\mathcal{X}\times(0,a_{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})
and any

(t,x)\in\bigl(0,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}r^{\beta}\bigl(\log(e+r^{-\beta})\bigr)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}}\bigr]\times B(x_{0},r/2)
.
Now set
r_{0}:=a_{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X}
,

t_{0}:=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}r_{0}^{\beta}\bigl(\log(e+r_{0}^{-\beta})\bigr)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}}

and let
(t,x)\in(0,t_{0})\times\mathcal{X}
. Then

t\in\bigl(0,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{t}}r^{\beta}\bigl(\log(e+r^{-\beta})\bigr)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{t}}}\bigr]

for some
r\in(0,r_{0})
, and hence from
p_{t}(x,\cdot)=p_{t}(\cdot,x)
on
\mathcal{X}
,

p_{t}(x,\cdot)\geq p^{B(x,r)}_{t,x}\geq 0

\mu
-a.e. on
B(x,r)
and (
2.45
) we obtain

\displaystyle p_{2t}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{t}(x,y)p_{t}(y,x)\,\mu(dy)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{t}(x,y)^{2}\,\mu(dy)\geq\int_{B(x,r)}p_{t}(x,y)^{2}\,\mu(dy)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{B(x,r)}p^{B(x,r)}_{t,x}(y)^{2}\,\mu(dy)\geq C_{\mathrm{dl}}t^{-\alpha/\beta}\bigl(\log(e+t^{-1})\bigr)^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{dl}}},

proving (
2.6
) for
(t,x)\in(0,2t_{0})\times\mathcal{X}
. Finally,
(
2.6
) for
(t,x)\in[2t_{0},(\mathop{\text{diam}}\mathcal{X})^{\beta}]\times\mathcal{X}

follows since, for each
s\in(0,\infty)
, we can define a bounded self-adjoint operator

T_{s}:L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mu)\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mu)
satisfying
T_{s}{\bf 1}={\bf 1}
by

T_{s}f:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{s}(\cdot,y)f(y)\,\mu(dy)
, then

\int_{\mathcal{X}}|T_{s}f|^{2}\,d\mu=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\bigl|T_{s}f-\frac{1}{\mu(\mathcal{X})}\int_{\mathcal{X}}f\,d\mu\bigr|^{2}\,d\mu+\frac{1}{\mu(\mathcal{X})}\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{X}}f\,d\mu\bigr)^{2}

for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mu)
and hence for any
(t,x)\in(s,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle p_{2t}(x,x)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{t}(\cdot,x)^{2}\,d\mu=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\bigl|T_{s}(p_{t-s}(\cdot,x))\bigr|^{2}\,d\mu\geq\frac{\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{t-s}(\cdot,x)\,d\mu\bigr)^{2}}{\mu(\mathcal{X})}=\frac{1}{\mu(\mathcal{X})}.

We have thus completed the proof of Theorem
2.1
.
∎

3.
Background on the Brownian map and Liouville quantum gravity

3.1.
A review on the Brownian map

3.1.1.
Definition

The Brownian map is a random metric measure space which was shown by Le Gall
[
63
]
and Miermont
[
69
]
to arise as the Gromov-Hausdorff scaling limit of certain types of uniformly random planar maps. The name Brownian map was introduced by Marckert and Mokkadem in
[
68
]
who proved a weaker form of convergence of rescaled uniformly random quadrangulations. The standard (unit area) Brownian map is defined from the Brownian snake using the following procedure. Let
X
be a normalized Brownian excursion on
[0,1]
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be the instance of the continuum random tree (CRT)
[
1
]
which is encoded by
X
. That is, for
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, we let

m_{X}(s,t)=X_{s}+X_{t}-2\inf_{r\in[s,t]}X_{r}.

Then
m_{X}
defines a pseudometric on
[0,1]
. We say that
s\sim t
if
m_{X}(s,t)=0
. Then
\mathcal{T}
is given by the metric quotient of
[0,1]
with respect to the equivalence relation
\sim
. Let
\rho_{\mathrm{CRT}}\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{T}
be the natural projection map. Given
X
, we let
Y
be the mean-zero Gaussian process with

\text{cov}(Y_{s},Y_{t})=\inf_{r\in[s,t]}X_{r}

so that
\mathbb{E}[(Y_{s}-Y_{t})^{2}]=m_{X}(s,t)
. In particular, if
s\sim t
then
Y_{s}=Y_{t}
, so that
Y
induces a Brownian process on the branches of the CRT instance
\mathcal{T}
, and is called the
Brownian snake
. We note that
Y
is a.s.
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for any
\alpha<1/4
. This follows from the usual Kolmogorov-Centsov argument.

For
s,t\in[0,1]
with
s<t
and
[t,s]=[0,1]\setminus(s,t)
, let

d^{\circ}(s,t)=Y_{s}+Y_{t}-2\max\left(\inf_{r\in[s,t]}Y_{r},\inf_{r\in[t,s]}Y_{r}\right).

For
a,b\in\mathcal{T}
, we set

d_{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(a,b)=\min\{d^{\circ}(s,t):\rho_{\mathrm{CRT}}(s)=a,\rho_{\mathrm{CRT}}(t)=b\}.

Finally, for
a,b\in\mathcal{T}
, we set

d(a,b)=\inf\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}d_{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(a_{j-1},a_{j})\right\}

where the infimum is over all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{0}=a,a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}=b
in
\mathcal{T}
. We say that
a\cong b
if and only if
d(a,b)=0
. The Brownian map
(\mathcal{S},d)
is then defined to be the metric quotient
\mathcal{T}/\cong
. Let
\rho\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}
be the natural projection map associated with this metric quotient and let
\rho_{\mathrm{BM}}=\rho\circ\rho_{\mathrm{CRT}}
. Let
\nu
denote the pushforward of Lebesgue measure from
[0,1]
to
\mathcal{S}
by
\rho_{\mathrm{BM}}
so that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu)
is a metric measure space.

The Brownian map is also naturally marked by two points. The first marked point
x
is called the
root
and is equal to
\rho_{\mathrm{BM}}(s^{*})
where
s^{*}
is the a.s. unique point in
[0,1]
at which
Y
attains its infimum
[
68
, Lemma 16]
(see also
[
66
, Proposition 2.5]
). The second marked point
y
is called the
dual root
and is equal to
\rho_{\mathrm{BM}}(0)=\rho_{\mathrm{BM}}(1)
. The reason for this terminology is that
x
is the root of the tree of geodesics from every point
z\in\mathcal{S}
to
x
and
y
is the root of the dual tree, where the tree encoded by
X
is called the
dual tree
and the tree encoded by
Y
is called the
geodesic tree
(tree of geodesics from every point back to
x
).
It turns out that the conditional law of
x,y
given
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu)
is that of independent picks from
\nu

[
62
, Section 8]
. That is, the law of
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is invariant under the operation of resampling
x,y
independently using
\nu
.

We let
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=1}
denote the law of
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
. The superscript
A=1
serves to emphasize that
\nu(\mathcal{S})=1
as
X
is defined on
[0,1]
. If
a>0
is fixed then we define
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=a}
by replacing
X
with a Brownian excursion of length
a
and in this case we have that
\nu(\mathcal{S})=a
.

As we will see later, in many situations it is more convenient to consider the Brownian map with
random
rather than fixed (unit) area. The starting point for the definition of the (doubly marked) Brownian map with random area is the infinite measure on Brownian excursions
[
82
]
. We remind the reader that one can “sample” from this distribution using the following two steps:

•

Pick a lifetime
t
from the measure
ct^{-3/2}dt
where
c>0
is a constant and
dt
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

•

Given
t
, pick a Brownian excursion
X
of length
t
.

Recall that a Brownian excursion
X
of length
t
can be constructed by first sampling a Brownian excursion
\widetilde{X}
of unit length and then by setting
X_{s}=\sqrt{t}\widetilde{X}_{s/t}
.

We define
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
to be the law of
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
where
X
in the definition of the standard (unit area) Brownian map is replaced by a sample from the infinite measure on Brownian excursions. Since the law on Brownian excursions is an infinite measure, so is
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
. However, if we condition
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
on
\nu(\mathcal{S})=a
for
a>0
then we obtain the probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=a}
. It is also possible to condition
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
on other events to obtain a probability measure. Another important example which we will discuss momentarily in more detail is the event
d(x,y)>1
. More precisely, it follows from
[
77
, Theorem 1.1]
that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[d(x,y)>1]\in(0,\infty)
. In particular,
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[d(x,y)>1]^{-1}
times the restriction of
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
to
\{d(x,y)>1\}
is a probability measure. In fact, we have that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})>1]\in(0,\infty)
. Indeed, as we noted earlier, the Brownian snake
Y
used to construct a sample
(\mathcal{S},d)
from
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=1}
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for any
\alpha<1/4
. Also, the Kolmogorov-Centsov argument implies that
\widetilde{Y}=\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}|Y_{t}|\in L^{p}
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
. Furthermore we have that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})>1]=c\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=t}[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})>1]t^{-3/2}dt
for some constant
c\in(0,\infty)
which combined with scaling and change of coordinates implies that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})>1]=4c\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=1}[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})>s]sds
. Fix
p>2
. Since
\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})\leq 2\widetilde{Y}
by construction and
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=1}[\widetilde{Y}>s]\lesssim s^{1-p}
by Markov’s inequality, we obtain that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})>1]\lesssim 1+\int_{s=1}^{\infty}s^{1-p}ds\lesssim 1
.

We now record an estimate for the volume of metric balls in the Brownian map. This result will involve a polynomial correction, which we will improve later to a polylogarithmic correction.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is an instance of the Brownian map. For each
u>0
there a.s. exists
r_{0}>0
so that

r^{4+u}\leq\nu(B(z,r))\leq r^{4-u}\quad\text{for all}\quad r\in(0,r_{0})\quad\text{and all}\quad z\in\mathcal{S}.

Proof.

The upper bound is proved in
[
62
, Corollary 6.2]
. The lower bound follows from the Hölder continuity of the Brownian snake. ∎

3.1.2.
Breadth-first construction

We will now review the basic properties of the breadth-first construction of the Brownian map developed in
[
77
]
.

Continuous state branching processes

Recall that a
continuous state branching process
(CSBP) with
branching mechanism

\psi
is the cadlag Markov process
Y
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
whose law is characterized by its Laplace transforms

(3.1)

\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\lambda Y_{t})\,|\,Y_{s}]=\exp(-Y_{s}u_{t-s}(\lambda))\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq s\leq t

where

\frac{\partial u_{t}}{\partial t}(\lambda)=-\psi(u_{t}(\lambda))\quad\text{for}\quad u_{0}(\lambda)=\lambda.

See
[
58
, Chapter 10]
or
[
61
]
for an introduction to CSBPs. There is a correspondence between CSBPs and Lévy processes via the so-called
Lamperti transform

[
59
]
. In particular, if
Y
is a Lévy process stopped upon hitting
(-\infty,0)
with only upward jumps and Laplace exponent
\psi
and we define the time-change

(3.2)

s(t)=\inf\{r>0:\int_{0}^{r}\frac{1}{Y_{u}}\,du\geq t\}

then the process
Y_{s(t)}
is a CSBP with branching mechanism
\psi
. Conversely, if
Y
is a CSBP with branching mechanism
\psi
and we set

(3.3)

s(t)=\inf\{r>0:\int_{0}^{r}Y_{u}\,du\geq t\}

then the time-changed process
Y_{s(t)}
is a Lévy process stopped upon hitting
(-\infty,0)
with only upward jumps and Laplace exponent
\psi
.

In this work, we will be primarily interested in the case that
\psi(\lambda)=c\lambda^{\alpha}
where
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
is a constant. We will call the corresponding CSBP an
\alpha
-stable CSBP
since the associated Lévy process is
\alpha
-stable. In this case, we have the explicit formula

(3.4)

u_{t}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{1-\alpha}+ct)^{1/(1-\alpha)}.

Note that the explicit form of
u_{t}
combined with (
3.1
) implies that the following is true. If
Y
is an
\alpha
-stable CSBP starting from
Y_{0}>0
then for each
c>0
we have that
t\mapsto Y_{c^{\alpha-1}t}
has the same law as
cY
, up to a change of starting point.

Let
Y
be an
\alpha
-stable CSBP and let
\zeta=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}=0\}
be its extinction time. The formula (
3.1
) yields an explicit formula for the distribution of
\zeta
, which is as follows

(3.5)

\mathbb{P}[\zeta>t]=\mathbb{P}[Y_{t}>0]=1-\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[e^{-\lambda Y_{t}}]=1-\exp(-ct^{1/(1-\alpha)}Y_{0}).

Boundary length and conditional independence of inside and outside of filled metric balls

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is distributed according to
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
. For each
r\geq 0
, we let
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
be the complement of the
y
-containing component of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,r)}
, where we set
B(x,0)=\emptyset
. We call
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
the
filled metric ball
of radius
r
centered at
x
. In other words,
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
is defined as the closure of the union of
B(x,r)
together with all of the components of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,r)}
which do not contain
y
.

On the event
\{d(x,y)>r\}
, it is shown in
[
77
]
how to associate with
\partial B^{\bullet}(x,r)
a
boundary length

L_{r}
in a manner which is measurable with respect to
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
. It turns out that the marginal law on
L_{r}
can be “sampled” from by first “picking” a
3/2
