-stable CSBP excursion from the infinite measure on such excursions and then taking the cadlag modification of the time-reversal.
The infinite measure on
3/2
-stable CSBP excursions can be described as follows. Recall from
[
20
, Chapter VIII, Section 4]
that the infinite measure on
3/2
-stable Lévy excursions with only upward jumps can be sampled from as follows.

•

Pick a lifetime
t
from the measure
ct^{-5/3}dt
where
c>0
is a constant and
dt
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

•

Given
t
, pick a
3/2
-stable Lévy excursion with only upward jumps of time-length
t
.

The infinite measure on
3/2
-stable CSBPs can be sampled from by first sampling from the infinite measure on
3/2
-stable Lévy excursions and then applying the Lamperti transform (
3.2
).

For each
r>0
, we can view
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
as a metric measure space which is marked by
x
and equipped with the restriction of
\nu
to
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
. The metric that we put on
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
is the
interior-internal metric
, which is defined by setting the distance between points
u,v\in B^{\bullet}(x,r)
to be the infimum of the
d
-length of paths which connect
u,v
and stay in the interior of
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
except possibly at their endpoints. We can similarly view
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,r)
as a metric measure space which is marked by
y
and equipped with the interior-internal metric.

It is shown in
[
77
]
that
L_{r}
is a.s. determined by
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
, that
L_{r}
is a.s. determined also by
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,r)
and, moreover, that
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
and
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,r)
are conditionally independent given
L_{r}
. On the event that
\{d(x,y)>r\}
, the same also holds if we replace
r
by
s=d(x,y)-r
. In this case,
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,s)
is the region which has been explored after performing
r
units of
reverse metric exploration
. It is also shown in
[
77
]
that for each
r>0
the metric measure spaces corresponding to
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,r)}
are conditionally independent given their boundary lengths. The conditional law of the components of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,r)}
which do not contain
y
are given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
(where
\ell
is the hole boundary length) and the conditional law of the component which contains
y
is given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
(where
\ell
is again the hole boundary length);
here
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
denote the law of a Brownian disk with boundary length equal to
\ell
and that of a Brownian disk weighted by its area, respectively, introduced below at the end of Subsection
3.1.2
.
This in fact follows from the conditional independence of
B^{\bullet}(x,r)
and
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,r)
and a re-rooting argument. These statements hold more generally if
r
is replaced by a stopping time
\tau
for the boundary length process of the whole collection of components of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,s)}
,
s\geq 0
.

Metric bands

The inside-outside independence of filled metric balls allows us to decompose an instance of the Brownian map into conditionally independent
metric bands
. More precisely, suppose that we have fixed
r_{0}=0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\cdots<r_{k}
and we let
s_{j}=d(x,y)-r_{j}
for each
0\leq j\leq k
. Then we can view each
\mathcal{B}_{j}=B^{\bullet}(x,s_{j-1})\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,s_{j})
,
1\leq j\leq k
, as a metric measure space with its interior-internal metric
d_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}
and measure
\nu_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}=\nu|_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}
, where we set
B(x,r)=B^{\bullet}(x,r)=\emptyset
for
r\leq 0
. Note that each
\mathcal{B}_{j}
is either a topological disk or annulus. Its
inner
(resp.
outer
) boundary is the component of
\partial\mathcal{B}_{j}
whose distance to
x
is equal to
s_{j-1}
(resp.
s_{j}
). We will denote the inner (resp. outer) boundary of
\partial\mathcal{B}_{j}
by
\partial_{\mathrm{In}}\mathcal{B}_{j}
(resp.
\partial_{\mathrm{Out}}\mathcal{B}_{j}
). We also note that
\partial_{\mathrm{In}}\mathcal{B}_{j}
is naturally marked by the point visited by the a.s. unique geodesic connecting
x
and
y
. The
width
of
\mathcal{B}_{j}
is
s_{j-1}-s_{j}=r_{j}-r_{j-1}
. We note that the independence property for the reverse metric exploration implies that
\mathcal{B}_{j}
is conditionally independent of
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{j-1}
given the boundary length of
\partial_{\mathrm{In}}\mathcal{B}_{j}
. Moreover, the conditional law of
\mathcal{B}_{j}
given that its inner boundary length is equal to
\ell
depends only on
\ell
and the width
r_{j}-r_{j-1}
.

We let
\mu_{{\mathrm{Band}}}^{L=\ell,W=w}
be the law on metric bands
(\mathcal{B},d_{\mathcal{B}},\nu_{\mathcal{B}},z)
with inner boundary length
\ell
, width
w
, and marked by a point
z
on the inner boundary of
\mathcal{B}
. We note that if
(\mathcal{B},d_{\mathcal{B}},\nu_{\mathcal{B}},z)
has law
\mu_{{\mathrm{Band}}}^{L=\ell,W=w}
,
a>0
and we rescale distances by
a
, boundary lengths by
a^{2}
, and areas by
a^{4}
, then we obtain a sample from
\mu_{{\mathrm{Band}}}^{L=a^{2}\ell,W=aw}
.

Brownian disks and the metric net

The
metric net
of
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is defined as
{\mathrm{MetNet}}(\mathcal{S}):=\bigcup_{r\geq 0}\partial B^{\bullet}(x,r)
. The components of
\mathcal{S}\setminus{\mathrm{MetNet}}(\mathcal{S})
are each topological disks. They correspond to the downward jumps of the boundary length process
L_{r}
where the magnitude of a given downward jump gives the boundary length of the component. The components are conditionally independent given their boundary lengths and are instances of the
Brownian disk
with the given boundary length (equipped with their interior-internal metric). For
\ell>0
, we let
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
denote the law of a Brownian disk with boundary length equal to
\ell
. We also let
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
denote the law of a Brownian disk weighted by its area. In other words, the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
with respect to
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
is equal to a normalizing constant times the area of the surface. If
r>0
, we have that
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,r)
has the law
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
where
\ell=L_{r}
.

It turns out that the law of the area of a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
is equal to the law of the amount of time it takes a standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
starting from
0
to hit
-\ell

[
21
]
. Recall that the density for this law with respect to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
at
a
is given by

(3.6)

\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{2\pi a^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2a}\right).

The law of the area of a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
thus has density with respect to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
at
a
given by

(3.7)

\frac{\ell^{3}}{\sqrt{2\pi a^{5}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2a}\right).

We note that the Brownian disk can be constructed using a variant of the Brownian snake and this perspective is developed in
[
21
]
. We will not describe this construction further here because in what follows we will only need to know that Brownian disks arise as the complementary components when performing a metric exploration of the Brownian map. The equivalence between these two perspectives was proved in
[
64
]
.

3.1.3.
Explorations of Brownian disks

Recall from the above that if
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
has distribution
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
and
r>0
then the conditional law of
\mathcal{S}\setminus B^{\bullet}(x,r)
given
L_{r}
is equal to
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=L_{r}}
. This implies that there is a natural exploration of an instance
(\mathcal{D},d,\nu,y)
sampled from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
,
\ell>0
, which is given by considering for each
r\geq 0
the
y
-containing complementary component
\mathcal{D}_{r}
of the (closed)
r
-neighborhood of
\partial\mathcal{D}
. The boundary length
L_{r}
of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{r}
is then well-defined and evolves in the same manner as in the case of a metric exploration of the Brownian map. Moreover, the successive components disconnected from
y
correspond to the downward jumps of
L_{r}
and are conditionally independent Brownian disks with boundary length equal to the size of the corresponding downward jump. Finally, for each
r\geq 0
, the conditional law given
L_{r}
of the metric measure space given by
\mathcal{D}_{r}
and marked by
y
, the restriction of
\nu
to
\mathcal{D}_{r}
, and the interior-internal metric in
\mathcal{D}_{r}
is equal to
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=L_{r}}
.

In the case of
(\mathcal{D},d,\nu)
sampled from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
, there is another exploration which is natural to consider and is called the
center exploration
in
[
77
]
. It is analogous to the targeted exploration described just above except that it always continues into the complementary component with the largest boundary length. It can more precisely be constructed as follows. Suppose that
y\in\mathcal{D}
is picked according to
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{D})
independently of everything else. Then one can consider the metric exploration starting from
\partial\mathcal{D}
and targeted at
y
up until the first time a component
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is disconnected from
y
with boundary length larger than the
y
-containing component. At this time, we pick a point
y_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
from
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{D}_{1})
independently of everything else and then continue the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
towards
y_{1}
until the first time a component
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is disconnected from
y_{1}
with boundary length larger than the
y_{1}
-containing component.
We then continue iterating the above procedure to have a sequence
\{\mathcal{D}_{j}\}_{j}
of components inside of which we continue the exploration at each step, until the boundary length of the target component first hits
0
. We note that this time
R
is a.s. finite since it is at most the diameter of
\mathcal{D}
, and that a.s. the sequence
\{\mathcal{D}_{j}\}_{j}
is infinite but only finitely many of them appear by exploration time
r
for any
r<R
.

Let us now record some important properties of the center exploration.
By the
target component
of the center exploration at exploration time
r
we mean the component in which the exploration continues after
r
units of exploration, and each component which remains after the termination of the exploration is called a
component cut off by the center exploration
.
Let
M_{r}
denote the boundary length of the target component at exploration time
r
.
Then the downward jumps of
M_{r}
correspond to components which are disconnected from the target component. Moreover, the components are conditionally independent Brownian disks (given the realization of
M
) where the boundary length of the disk is given by the length of the corresponding jump. Furthermore, the conditional law of the target component given
M_{r}
is given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=M_{r}}
.

Let
(a_{i})
denote the sequence of downward jumps made by the center exploration run until
M_{r}
hits
0
and let
\alpha>0
. Then it is shown in
[
77
, Section 4.6]
that

(3.8)

\ell^{2}=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sum_{i}a_{i}^{2}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\ell^{\alpha}>\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sum_{i}a_{i}^{\alpha}\right]\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha>2

where both expectations are under the law
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
. Moreover, by the scale invariance of the Brownian disk we note that the value of
\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i}(a_{i}/\ell)^{\alpha}]
does not depend on
\ell
. The first equality in (
3.8
) can be seen because the conditional expectation of the amount of area inside of
\mathcal{D}
given the center exploration up to some time evolves as a martingale and, as explained above, the complementary components are conditionally independent Brownian disks. The inequality in (
3.8
) can be seen by a direct calculation with the jump law for
M_{r}
(which is explicitly identified in
[
77
]
).

3.2.
Liouville quantum gravity review

3.2.1.
Basic definitions

Suppose that
h
is an instance of (some form of) the Gaussian free field (GFF) on a domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
(e.g., with Dirichlet or free boundary conditions, defined on the whole-plane, any of the above plus a harmonic function). The Liouville quantum gravity surface described by
h
refers to the random two-dimensional Riemannian manifold with metric tensor

(3.9)

e^{\gamma h(z)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})

where
\gamma\in(0,2)
is a parameter and
dx^{2}+dy^{2}
denotes the Euclidean metric on
D
. This expression does not make literal sense because
h
is a distribution and not a function. The volume form associated with (
3.9
) was constructed in
[
36
]
. The construction proceeds by letting
h_{\epsilon}(z)
be the average of
h
on
\partial B(z,\epsilon)
and then taking

(3.10)

\mu_{h}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{\gamma^{2}/2}e^{\gamma h_{\epsilon}(z)}dz

where
dz
denotes Lebesgue measure on
D
. In the case that
D
has free boundary conditions on a linear segment
L
, one can similarly define a boundary length measure on
L
by setting

(3.11)

\nu_{h}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{\gamma^{2}/4}e^{\gamma h_{\epsilon}(z)/2}dz

where
dz
denotes Lebesgue measure on
L
. The limiting procedure (
3.10
) implies that the measures
\mu_{h}
satisfy the following change of coordinates formula. If
\varphi\colon\widetilde{D}\to D
is a conformal transformation and

(3.12)

\widetilde{h}=h\circ\varphi+Q\log|\varphi^{\prime}|\quad\text{where}\quad Q=\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma}{2}

then
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(A)=\mu_{h}(\varphi(A))
for all Borel sets
A
. The same is also true with
\nu_{h}
in place of
\mu_{h}
. In particular, this gives a way to define
\nu_{h}
on boundary segments which are not necessarily linear because we can conformally map to such a domain and then compute the boundary length there.

We say that two domain/field pairs
(D,h)
and
(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h})
are equivalent as quantum surfaces if
h
,
\widetilde{h}
are related as in (
3.12
).
A
quantum surface
is an equivalence class of domain/field pairs under this equivalence relation.
An
embedding
of a quantum surface is a particular choice of representative, and a quantum surface with an embedding
(D,h)
for a domain
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is said to be
parameterized
by
D
.
We will also discuss
marked quantum surfaces
which refer to quantum surfaces with extra marked points and the equivalence relation (
3.12
) is generalized so that
the map
\varphi
must also take the marked points associated with the surface described by
\widetilde{h}
to the marked points associated with the surface described by
h
.
It is not always immediate that two domain/field pairs describe equivalent quantum surfaces, so there is some subtlety to this definition.
For example, two very different looking constructions of the quantum sphere have been given in
[
35
]
and
[
28
]
and proved to be equivalent in
[
8
]
.

3.2.2.
Quantum disks, spheres, and wedges

We will now describe the construction of a quantum
disk
and a quantum
sphere
, as introduced in
[
35
]
.
The precise definitions will not be important for what follows, but we include them here for completeness.
Throughout Subsection
3.2.2
,
\gamma\in(0,2)
is arbitrary, and for functions
f,g
with
L^{2}
gradients we define the
Dirichlet inner product
by

(3.13)

(f,g)_{\nabla}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\nabla f(x)\cdot\nabla g(x)\,dx.

Let
\|\cdot\|_{\nabla}
be the associated norm.

The starting point for the definition of the quantum surfaces which we will discuss is the excursion measure for a Bessel process, which we recall is defined as follows. Suppose that
\delta\in(-\infty,2)
. Then one can sample from the measure on Bessel excursions using the following procedure:

(i)

Pick a lifetime
T
from the measure
c_{\delta}T^{\delta/2-2}\,dT
where
dT
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
c_{\delta}>0
is a constant.

(ii)

Sample an independent normalized excursion
\widetilde{Z}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
of a Bessel process of dimension
\delta
.

(iii)

Take
Z
to be
t\mapsto T^{1/2}\widetilde{Z}(t/T)
.

We note that the Bessel excursion measure is an infinite measure (as the measure
c_{\delta}T^{\delta/2-2}\,dT
is an infinite measure). It is also defined even for
\delta\leq 0
, although in this case it is not possible to concatenate a Poisson point process of such Bessel excursions to obtain a continuous process.

While reading what follows, the reader may find it helpful to look at
[
35
, Figure 1.2]
.

Quantum disks

We will now describe the construction of a
quantum disk
. This is a finite volume quantum surface which is homeomorphic to the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
and is naturally marked by two points. It is easiest to give the definition of the quantum disk when the surface is parameterized by the infinite horizontal strip
{\mathscr{S}}=\mathbb{R}\times[0,\pi]
. We let
\mathcal{H}({\mathscr{S}})
be the closure of the
C^{\infty}
functions on
{\mathscr{S}}
with
L^{2}
gradient with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\nabla}
, viewed modulo additive constant. Then
\mathcal{H}({\mathscr{S}})
admits the orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{1}({\mathscr{S}})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}({\mathscr{S}})
where
\mathcal{H}_{1}({\mathscr{S}})
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{2}({\mathscr{S}})
) contains those functions in
\mathcal{H}({\mathscr{S}})
which are constant (resp. have mean-zero) on vertical lines.

A
quantum disk
is a quantum surface
({\mathscr{S}},h,-\infty,+\infty)
whose law can be sampled from using the following steps:

•

Take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}({\mathscr{S}})
to be given by
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log Z
where
Z
is a Bessel excursion of dimension
3-\tfrac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
, reparameterized to have quadratic variation
2\,dt
.

•

Take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}({\mathscr{S}})
to be independently given by the corresponding projection of a GFF on
{\mathscr{S}}
with free boundary conditions.

The above specifies the embedding of the surface modulo one free parameter (since as we will explain below, the points at
\pm\infty
are marked), which corresponds to the horizontal translation. There are various ways of fixing the horizontal translation. One possibility is to let
X
denote the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}({\mathscr{S}})
and then choose the horizontal translation so that
\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}}X_{u}
is attained at
u=0
.

Since the measure on Bessel excursions of dimension
3-\tfrac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
is an infinite measure, this defines an infinite measure on quantum surfaces which we denote by
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}
. One can obtain a probability measure by conditioning, for example, on the boundary length
\nu_{h}(\partial{\mathscr{S}})
or area
\mu_{h}({\mathscr{S}})
being equal to a given value. We let
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}^{L=\ell}
denote the probability measure obtained when we condition on the boundary length
\nu_{h}(\partial{\mathscr{S}})
being
\ell>0
.

The two marked points at
+\infty,-\infty
are uniformly random given the quantum surface structure
[
35
, Proposition A.8]
. This means that the law of
({\mathscr{S}},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is invariant under the operation of sampling
x,y
independently from
\nu_{h}
, letting
\varphi\colon{\mathscr{S}}\to{\mathscr{S}}
be a conformal transformation which takes
+\infty
to
x
and
-\infty
to
y
, and then replacing
h
with
h\circ\varphi+Q\log|\varphi^{\prime}|
.

We will often refer to the law on quantum disks which is obtained by weighting the law
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}^{L=\ell}
by its total volume
\mu_{h}({\mathscr{S}})
and then adding an extra marked point in the interior chosen at random from the quantum measure
\mu_{h}
. We let
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=\ell}
denote this probability measure.

Quantum spheres

We will now describe the construction of the
quantum sphere
. This is a finite volume surface which is homeomorphic to the two-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and is naturally marked by two points. It is easiest to give the definition of the quantum sphere when parameterized by the infinite cylinder
\mathscr{C}=\mathbb{R}\times[0,2\pi]
with the top and bottom identified. Let
\mathcal{H}(\mathscr{C})
be the closure of the
C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C})
functions with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\nabla}
, defined modulo additive constant. Then
\mathcal{H}(\mathscr{C})
admits the orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{C})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{C})
where
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{C})
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{C})
) contains those functions in
\mathcal{H}(\mathscr{C})
which are constant (resp. have mean-zero) on vertical lines.

Let
h
be the field on
\mathscr{C}
whose law can be sampled from as follows:

•

Take its projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{C})
to be given by
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log Z
where
Z
is a Bessel excursion of dimension
4-\tfrac{8}{\gamma^{2}}
, reparameterized to have quadratic variation
dt
.

•

Take its projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{C})
to be independently given by the corresponding projection of a whole-plane GFF on
\mathscr{C}
.

As in the case of the quantum disk, this specifies the embedding of the surface modulo one free parameter. One possible way of fixing it is to let
X
denote the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{C})
and then choose the horizontal translation so that
\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}}X_{u}
is attained at
u=0
.

We emphasize that since the Bessel excursion measure is an infinite measure, the measure on quantum spheres that we have just defined is also an infinite measure. We let
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
denote this infinite measure. One can obtain a probability measure by conditioning on its total volume.

The two marked points at
+\infty,-\infty
turn out to be uniformly random given the quantum surface structure
[
35
, Proposition A.13]
. This means that the law of
(\mathscr{C},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is invariant under the operation of sampling
x,y
independently from
\mu_{h}
, letting
\varphi\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
be a conformal transformation which takes
+\infty
to
x
and
-\infty
to
y
, and then replacing
h
with
h\circ\varphi+Q\log|\varphi^{\prime}|
.

Quantum wedges

We will now describe the construction of a
quantum wedge
. This is a doubly-marked infinite volume surface which is homeomorphic to the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}
and is naturally marked by two points (an “origin” point and an “infinity” point). Bounded neighborhoods of the origin point a.s. have a finite amount of mass and neighborhoods of the infinity point a.s. have an infinite amount of mass. It can be convenient to describe a quantum wedge parameterized either by
\mathbb{H}
(as this is the setting in which
{\rm SLE}
is easiest to describe) or by
{\mathscr{S}}
(as in this case the field is easiest to describe).

Let us first describe the sampling procedure in the case of
\mathbb{H}
. We let
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{H})
be the Hilbert space closure of the
C^{\infty}
functions on
\mathbb{H}
with
L^{2}
gradient with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\nabla}
, viewed modulo additive constant. Then we can write
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{H})=\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathbb{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
where
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
) denotes the subspace of functions which are radially symmetric (resp. have zero mean on origin-centered semicircles). The law of the quantum wedge
(\mathbb{H},h,0,\infty)
parameterized by
\mathbb{H}
where
0
is the origin point and
\infty
is the infinity point can be sampled from using the following procedure.

First, we define a process
A_{s}
as follows. For
s\geq 0
, we let
A_{s}=B_{2s}+\gamma s
where
B
is a standard Brownian motion with
B_{0}=0
. For
s\leq 0
, we let
A_{s}=\widehat{B}_{-2s}+\gamma s
where
\widehat{B}
is a standard Brownian motion independent of
B
with
\widehat{B}_{0}=0
conditioned so that
\widehat{B}_{2u}+(Q-\gamma)u>0
for all
u>0
.

•

We take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
to be the function whose common value on
\partial B(0,e^{-s})\cap\mathbb{H}
is given by
A_{s}
as described above.

•

We take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
to be independently given by the corresponding projection of a GFF on
\mathbb{H}
with free boundary conditions.

We note that if we have a quantum wedge parameterized by
\mathbb{H}
, then taking the origin point to be
0
and the infinity point to be
\infty
specifies the embedding up to one free parameter which corresponds to the scaling. In the above construction, we have taken the free parameter so that if we let
r=\sup\{s>0:h_{s}(0)+Q\log s=0\}
, where
h_{s}(0)
denotes the average of
h
on
\partial B(0,s)\cap\mathbb{H}
, then
r=1
. This is sometimes called the
circle average embedding
. There are other ways of fixing this scaling, but the circle average embedding is often convenient because then the restriction of
h
to
\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}
has the same law as the corresponding restriction to
\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}
of a free boundary GFF on
\mathbb{H}
plus
-\gamma\log|\cdot|
with the additive constant fixed so that its average on
\partial\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}
vanishes.

We can sample from the law of a quantum wedge parameterized by
{\mathscr{S}}
using the following procedure:

•

Take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}({\mathscr{S}})
to be given by
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log Z
,
Z
a Bessel process of dimension
1+\tfrac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
, parameterized to have quadratic variation
2dt
.

•

Take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}({\mathscr{S}})
to be independently given by the corresponding projection of a free boundary GFF on
{\mathscr{S}}
.

We will use the notation
({\mathscr{S}},h,-\infty,+\infty)
where
-\infty
(resp.
+\infty
) denotes the origin (resp. infinity) point. This actually specifies the embedding of the surface into
{\mathscr{S}}
modulo horizontal translation. It is often convenient to take the horizontal translation so that if
X
denotes the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}({\mathscr{S}})
then
\inf\{u\in\mathbb{R}:X_{u}=0\}=0
.

We let
\mu_{\mathrm{QW}}^{W=2}
denote the law of a quantum wedge. The reason for the superscript
W=2
is that one can in fact consider variants of the quantum wedge which are obtained using a Bessel process of dimension
1+\tfrac{2}{\gamma^{2}}W
and the parameter
W>0
is called the
weight
. In this article, we will only need the
W=2
quantum wedge so we will not discuss the other variants in further detail.

The weight-
2
quantum wedge has the special feature that it is invariant under the operation of translating its boundary point by a fixed amount of quantum length. More precisely, suppose that
(\mathbb{H},h,0,\infty)
is a quantum wedge,
L>0
, and
x>0
is such that
\nu_{h}([0,x])=L
. Then
(\mathbb{H},h,x,\infty)
is again a quantum wedge.

3.2.3.
Review of the metric

A metric for
\sqrt{8/3}
-Liouville quantum gravity which is isometric to the Brownian surfaces is constructed in
[
76
,
73
,
78
]
using the process
{\rm QLE}(8/3,0)
first defined in
[
74
]
.

If we have a quantum surface described by the field
h
on the domain
D
, we will let
d_{h}
denote the corresponding metric and
B_{h}(z,r)
the metric ball with respect to
d_{h}
centered at
z
of radius
r
. If we have a marked point
y
, then we will let
B_{h,y}^{\bullet}(z,r)
denote the filled metric ball relative to
y
with respect to
d_{h}
. In other words,
B_{h,y}^{\bullet}(z,r)
is the complement of the
y
-containing component of
D\setminus\overline{B_{h}(z,r)}
.

If
(\mathscr{C},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is a quantum sphere, then the associated doubly-marked metric measure space
(\mathscr{C},d_{h},\mu_{h},-\infty,+\infty)
has distribution
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
. Moreover, the field
h
which describes the quantum sphere can be measurably recovered from the metric measure space structure. Similarly, if
({\mathscr{S}},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is a quantum disk then the associated metric measure space
({\mathscr{S}},d_{h},\mu_{h})
has the law of a Brownian disk.

There are also infinite volume versions of this, whereby the so-called quantum cone is equivalent to the Brownian plane and a quantum wedge is equivalent to the Brownian half-plane.

3.2.4.
{\rm SLE}_{6}
explorations of quantum wedges, disks and spheres

We will now review the basic properties of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
explorations of quantum disks and spheres when
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
. These results come from
[
35
]
and
[
75
]
.

Figure 1.
Shown is a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
process
\eta^{\prime}
(red) on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
\infty
drawn up to time
t
. The
hull

K_{t}
of
\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
is the set of points disconnected from
\infty
by
\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
. The
top
of
K_{t}
is the part of
\partial K_{t}
in
\mathbb{H}
. The left (resp. right) side of the top of
K_{t}
is the part of the top which is to the left (resp. right) of
\eta^{\prime}(t)
and shown in teal (resp. orange). The
bottom
of
K_{t}
is the part of
\partial K_{t}
in
\partial\mathbb{H}
. The left (resp. right) side of the bottom of
K_{t}
is the part which is to the left (resp. right) of
0
and is shown in blue (resp. dark green). We set
L_{t}
to be the quantum length of the top left minus the bottom left and
R_{t}
to be the quantum length of the top right minus the bottom right. The quantum length of the top left is
L_{t}-\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}L_{s}
and the quantum length of the bottom left is
-\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}L_{s}
.

Suppose that
(\mathbb{H},h,0,\infty)
is a weight-
2
quantum wedge. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be an independent
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
\infty
. We assume that
\eta^{\prime}
is parameterized according to quantum natural time. Recall that this is not the standard capacity parameterization but rather a time parameterization which comes from the quantum surface structure defined by
h
. It is the continuum analog of the time parameterization of an interface from a statistical mechanics model on a random planar map where the interface is explored one edge at a time. Suppose that
t\geq 0
and that
\mathbb{H}_{t}
is the unbounded component of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
and let
K_{t}=\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{H}_{t}
be the associated hull. The
top
of
K_{t}
is
K_{t}\cap\partial\mathbb{H}_{t}=\partial K_{t}\cap\mathbb{H}
and the
bottom
is
\partial K_{t}\cap\partial\mathbb{H}
.
We let
L_{t}=L_{t}(\eta^{\prime})
denote the difference in the quantum length of the part of the top which is to the left of
\eta^{\prime}(t)
and the part of the bottom which is to the left of
0
. We similarly let
R_{t}=R_{t}(\eta^{\prime})
denote the difference in the quantum length of the part of the top which is to the right of
\eta^{\prime}(t)
and the part of the bottom which is to the right of
0
. See Figure
1
for an illustration of these definitions. Then
L,R
evolve as a pair of independent
3/2
-stable Lévy processes with only downward jumps occurring whenever
\eta^{\prime}
disconnects a bubble from
\infty
on its left (resp. right) side. The magnitude of the downward jump corresponds to the boundary length of the bubble.

Moreover, for each a.s. finite
\{\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{W}}_{t+}\}_{t\in[0,\infty)}
-stopping time
\tau
(with
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{W}}_{t}
denoting the
\sigma
-field generated by the quantum surfaces disconnected by
\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,t]}
from
\infty

and
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{W}}_{t+}:=\bigcap_{s\in(t,\infty)}\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{W}}_{s}
)
we have that the quantum surface parameterized by the unbounded component of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,\tau])
and marked by
\eta^{\prime}(\tau)
and
\infty
is a weight-
2
quantum wedge independent of
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{W}}_{\tau+}
.
The quantum surfaces parameterized by the bounded components of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,\tau])
correspond to the downward jumps of
L|_{[0,\tau]}
and
R|_{[0,\tau]}
(depending on whether they are to the left or right of
\eta^{\prime}
) and are conditionally independent quantum disks given
L|_{[0,\tau]}
,
R|_{[0,\tau]}
with boundary length given by the size of the corresponding jump.
Here and in what follows, for a quantum surface with an embedding
(D,h)
, we consider each open subset
D_{0}
of
D
as parameterizing a quantum surface by equipping
D_{0}
with the field
h|_{D_{0}}
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},x,y)
is a doubly marked quantum sphere. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be a whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{6}

from
x
to
y
which is sampled independently of
\mathcal{S}
and then reparameterized by quantum natural time, and set
t_{\eta^{\prime}}:=\inf(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}(y)
, so that
t_{\eta^{\prime}}<\infty
a.s.
For
t\in[0,\infty)
, we let
L_{t}
be the quantum boundary length of the connected component of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
containing
y
and let
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{S}}_{t}
denote the
\sigma
-field generated by the quantum surfaces disconnected by

\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,t]}
from
y
, and set
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{S}}_{t+}:=\bigcap_{s\in(t,\infty)}\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{S}}_{s}
.
Then,
[
75
, Proposition 6.4]
implies that for each
\{\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{S}}_{t+}\}_{t\in[0,\infty)}
-stopping time
\tau
with
\tau<t_{\eta^{\prime}}
a.s.,
conditionally given
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{S}}_{\tau+}
, the quantum surface parameterized by the
y
-containing component of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,\tau])
has law
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=L_{\tau}}
and the location of
\eta^{\prime}(\tau)
on its boundary is uniformly random from the quantum boundary measure.
Moreover, the boundary length process
\{L_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{\eta^{\prime}})}
evolves as the time-reversal of a
3/2
-stable Lévy excursion.
The same facts apply if
\ell>0
is fixed and we explore an instance of
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=\ell}
using a radial
{\rm SLE}_{6}
starting from a uniformly random boundary point according to quantum boundary length measure and targeted at the marked interior point.

In the case of the quantum sphere, the distribution of the amount of time that it takes
\eta^{\prime}
to go from
x
to
y
is the same as the lifetime distribution for the infinite excursion measure on
3/2
-stable Lévy excursions with only upward jumps. Recall from just after (
3.5
) that this distribution is given by a constant times
t^{-5/3}dt
where
dt
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

3.3.
Review of Liouville Brownian Motion

The Liouville Brownian motion has been constructed in
[
39
,
13
]
as the canonical diffusion process under the geometry induced
by the measure
\mu_{h}
, where
h
is a zero-boundary GFF on a planar domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
. It is defined for any choice of the parameter
\gamma\in(0,2)

as the time change of the planar Brownian motion on
D
in terms of the right-continuous inverse of the positive continuous additive functional (PCAF) associated with
\mu_{h}
.

More precisely, let
B=(B_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the planar Brownian motion on
D
defined as the coordinate process on the Wiener space
(C([0,\infty),D),(\mathcal{G}_{t})_{t\geq 0},(P_{x})_{x\in D})
with transition kernel denoted by
q_{t}(x,y)
,
t>0
,
x,y\in D
.
In
[
39
]
, Garban, Rhodes and Vargas constructed
a PCAF
F=\{F_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of
B

with the Liouville measure
\mu_{h}
as the associated Revuz measure, that is

\displaystyle\int_{D}f(y)\,d\mu_{h}(y)\;=\;\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\int_{D}E_{x}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{t}f(B_{s})\,dF_{s}\Bigr]\,dx

for any non-negative Borel function
f
on
D
.
Similarly as
\mu_{h}
, the PCAF
F
can be obtained via a regularization procedure from the circle average
h_{\epsilon}
, i.e. for all
x\in D
,

\displaystyle F_{t}\;=\;\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{t}\epsilon^{\gamma^{2}/2}\,e^{\gamma h_{\epsilon}(B_{s})}\,ds\qquad\text{in $P_{x}$-probability}

in the space
C([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
equipped with the topology of uniform
convergence on compact sets (cf.
[
39
, Theorem 2.7]
).
Moreover, for all
x\in D
,
P_{x}
-a.s.,
F
is strictly increasing and
satisfies
\lim_{t\to\infty}F_{t}=\infty
.

Then the
(
\gamma
-)Liouville Brownian motion

(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, abbreviated as
(
\gamma
-)LBM
, is defined as
X_{t}=B_{F^{-1}_{t}}
.
By the general theory of time
changes of Markov processes we have the following properties of the LBM:
First, it is a recurrent diffusion on
D
by
[
37
, Theorems A.2.12 and 6.2.3]
.
Furthermore by
[
37
, Theorem 6.2.1 (i)]
(see also
[
39
, Theorem 2.18]
),
the LBM is
\mu_{h}
-symmetric, i.e. its transition semigroup
(P_{t})_{t>0}
given by

\displaystyle P_{t}(x,A):=E_{x}[X_{t}\in A]

for
t\in(0,\infty)
,
x\in D
and a Borel set
A\subset D
,
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{D}P_{t}f\cdot g\,d\mu_{h}=\int_{D}f\cdot P_{t}g\,d\mu_{h}

for all Borel measurable functions
f,g:\ D\rightarrow[0,\infty]
.
By
[
38
, Theorem 0.4]
the Liouville semigroup
(P_{t})_{t>0}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Liouville measure, so there exists the
Liouville heat kernel

p=p_{t}(x,y):(0,\infty)\times D\times D\to[0,\infty)
so that

\displaystyle P_{t}f(x):=E_{x}[f(X_{t})]=\int_{D}p_{t}(x,y)f(y)\,d\mu_{h}(y),\qquad x\in D.

Furthermore, by
[
3
, Theorem 1.1]
the Liouville heat kernel admits a jointly continuous version is
(0,\infty)
-valued,
in particular the LBM is irreducible (cf.
[
67
]
for similar results).
Moreover, the transition semigroup
(P_{t})_{t>0}

is strong Feller, i.e.
P_{t}f
is continuous for any bounded Borel measurable
f:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We note that the aforementioned properties of the LBM still hold if we replace
h
by a random field on
D
whose law is locally absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
h
. We also note that the fields that we are considering in this paper are locally absolutely continuous with respect to the zero-boundary GFF.

The killed Liouville Brownian motion

Let
U
be a non-empty open subset of
D
and let
U\cup\{\partial_{U}\}

be its one-point compactification. We denote by

T_{U}:=\inf\{s\geq 0:\,B_{s}\not\in U\}
the exit time of the
Brownian motion
B
from
U
and by

\tau_{U}:=\inf\{s\geq 0:\,X_{s}\not\in U\}

that of the LBM
X
, where
\inf\emptyset:=\infty
.
Since by definition
X_{t}=B_{F^{-1}_{t}}
,
t\geq 0
, and

F
is a homeomorphism on
[0,\infty)
, we have
\tau_{U}=F_{T_{U}}
. Let now

B^{U}=(B^{U}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
X^{U}=(X^{U}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

denote the Brownian motion and the LBM, respectively,
killed upon exiting
U
. That is, they are diffusions on
U
defined by

\displaystyle B^{U}_{t}:=\begin{cases}B_{t}&\text{if $t<T_{U}$,}\\
\partial_{U}&\text{if $t\geq T_{U}$,}\end{cases}\qquad X^{U}_{t}:=\begin{cases}X_{t}&\text{if $t<\tau_{U}$,}\\
\partial_{U}&\text{if $t\geq\tau_{U}$.}\end{cases}

Then for
t\in(0,\infty)
, the semigroup operator
P^{U}_{t}
associated with the killed LBM
X^{U}
is expressed as
P^{U}_{t}f(x):=E_{x}\bigl[f(X^{U}_{t})\bigr]
,
x\in D
, for each Borel function
f:U\rightarrow[-\infty,\infty]
with the convention
f(\partial_{U}):=0
for which the expectation exist. By
[
3
, Proposition 5.1]
there exists a (unique) jointly continuous function

p^{U}=p^{U}_{t}(x,y):(0,\infty)\times U\times U\rightarrow[0,\infty)

such that for all
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times U
,

P_{x}[X^{U}_{t}\in dy]=p^{U}_{t}(x,y)\,d\mu_{h}(y)
,
which we refer to as the
Dirichlet Liouville heat kernel
on
U
.
Furthermore, the semigroup operator
P_{t}^{U}
is strong Feller, i.e. it maps Borel measurable bounded functions on
U
to continuous bounded functions on
U
.

If
U
is bounded, as a time change of
B^{U}
the
killed LBM
X^{U}
has the same integral kernel for its Green operator

G^{U}
as
B^{U}
, namely for any non-negative Borel function

f:U\rightarrow[0,\infty]
and
x\in D
,

(3.14)

\displaystyle G^{U}f(x):=E_{x}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{\tau_{U}}f(X_{t})\,dt\Bigr]=E_{x}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{T_{U}}f(B_{t})\,dF_{t}\Bigr]=\int_{U}g_{U}(x,y)f(y)\,d\mu_{h}(y)

Here
g_{U}
denotes the Euclidean Green kernel given by

(3.15)

\displaystyle g_{U}(x,y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}q^{U}_{t}(x,y)\,dt,\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

for the heat kernel
q^{U}_{t}(x,y)
of
B^{U}
:

q^{U}=q^{U}_{t}(x,y):(0,\infty)\times U\times U\to[0,\infty)
is the jointly
continuous function such that
P_{x}[B^{U}_{t}\in dy]=q^{U}_{t}(x,y)\,dy
for
t>0
and

x\in U
, and we set
q^{U}_{t}(x,y):=0
for
t>0
and
(x,y)\in(U\times U)^{c}
. Again we note that the aforementioned properties still hold if we replace
h
by a a random field whose law is locally absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
h
.
Finally, we recall (see e.g.
[
37
, Example 1.5.1]
) that
the Green function
g_{B(x_{0},R)}
over a ball
B(x_{0},R)
is of the form

(3.16)

\displaystyle g_{B(x_{0},R)}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\pi}\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}+\Psi_{x_{0},R}(x,y),\qquad x,y\in B(x_{0},R),

for some continuous function
\Psi_{x_{0},R}:B(x_{0},R)\times B(x_{0},R)\to\mathbb{R}
.

4.
Quenched estimates on the volume growth

For an instance
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu)
of the Brownian map, it was shown by Le Gall
[
62
, Corollary 6.2]
that
\nu(B(x,r))
is of order
r^{4}
up to a polynomial correction for all
r>0
small enough and every
x
(recall also Lemma
3.1
). We will now improve this estimate to polylogarithmic corrections.

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu)
is an instance of the unit area Brownian map. For each
u>0
there a.s. exists
r_{0}\in(0,1)
so that

r^{4}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{-6-u}\leq\nu(B(x,r))\leq r^{4}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{8+u},\qquad\forall r\in(0,r_{0}),\,x\in\mathcal{S}.

The proofs of the lower and upper bounds will be presented separately in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, respectively. In what follows, we will use several times the following standard concentration result for Poisson random variables. Namely, if
Z
is Poisson with mean
\lambda>0
then we have (cf.
[
73
, Lemma 2.9]
)

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[Z\leq\alpha\lambda]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\lambda(\alpha-\alpha\log\alpha-1)},\qquad\forall\alpha\in(0,1),

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[Z\geq\alpha\lambda]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\lambda(\alpha-\alpha\log\alpha-1)},\qquad\forall\alpha\in(1,\infty).

4.1.
Lower bound

We will begin by working towards proving the lower bound in Theorem
4.1
. The starting point is Lemma
4.3
, which is a pointwise lower bound for the volume in a metric ball when we condition on the event that the filled metric ball boundary length process is not too small. We will then extend this in Lemma
4.5
when we condition instead on the event that the distance between
x
and
y
is at least
1
, from which the lower bound in Theorem
4.1
easily follows. First, we will state the following lemma which allows us to compare the laws of a weighted quantum disk and a quantum wedge when both restricted in a small metric neighborhood around the point chosen uniformly according to the quantum boundary length measure. Its proof is essentially the same as the proof of
[
44
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Fix
\ell,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
with
\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}
and let
(\mathbb{H},d)
be a Brownian disk with area
\alpha
and boundary length
\ell
(in the sense of
[
44
, Section 3.5]
), where
\alpha\in[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]
is fixed. Let
x\in\partial\mathbb{H}
be sampled uniformly according to the boundary length measure induced by
(\mathbb{H},d)
. Let
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{d})
be a Brownian half-plane (equivalently weight-
2
quantum wedge). Then, for each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
there exists
\widetilde{\alpha}>0
depending only on
\epsilon,\ell,\alpha_{1}
, and
\alpha_{2}
, and a coupling of
(\mathbb{H},d)
and
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{d})
so that the following is true. With probability at least
1-\epsilon
, we have that the metric spaces
B_{d}(x,\widetilde{\alpha})
and
B_{\widetilde{d}}(0,\widetilde{\alpha})
agree in the sense of
[
44
]
.

Proof.

It follows from the argument used to prove
[
44
, Proposition 4.2]
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
r>0
, and suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is distributed according to
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
conditioned so that
if
Y_{s}
denotes the boundary length of
\partial B^{\bullet}(x,d(x,y)-s)
then
\sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s}\geq r^{2}
.
There exist constants
c_{0},M_{0}>0
which are independent of
r
so that

\mathbb{P}[\nu(B(x,r))\leq r^{4}/c^{4}]\leq c_{0}\exp(-M_{0}c^{2/3})\quad\text{for all}\quad c\geq 1.

Proof.

Let
c>1
.

Step 1.
We will first argue that with high probability under
\mathbb{P}
there exists
s\in[0,r]
so that the boundary length of
\partial B^{\bullet}(x,s)
is at least
r^{2}/c^{2}
, i.e.,
\sup_{s\in[0,r]}Y_{(d(x,y)-s)\vee 0}\geq r^{2}/c^{2}
with high probability. We note that it follows from
[
20
, Chapter IV, Section 4]
that if
\tau
is the first time that
Y
enters
[r^{2},\infty)
, then the conditional law of
Y_{t+\tau}
given
\tau<\infty
is that of a
3/2
-stable CSBP starting from
Y_{\tau}
. Also, it is not difficult to see from the scaling properties of the
3/2
-stable Lévy process that
the distribution of the supremum under the
3/2
-stable Lévy excursion measure is given by a constant times
t^{-2}\,dt
;
see, e.g.,
[
83
, Corollary 1]
for a careful proof. In particular, we have that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau<\infty]\in(0,\infty)
. Next, we define stopping times as follows.
We let
\tau_{0}=\inf\{s\geq 0:\sup_{u\in[0,s]}Y_{u}\geq r^{2},\ Y_{s}\leq r^{2}/c^{2}\}
.
Given that
\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{k}
have been defined, we let
\tau_{k+1}=\inf\{s\geq\tau_{k}+r/c:Y_{s}\leq r^{2}/c^{2}\}
.
Let
K=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\tau_{k}<\zeta\}
, where
\zeta=\inf\{s>0:Y_{s}=0\}
.
Note that
\tau<\tau_{0}

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. and that
Y_{\tau_{k}}\leq r^{2}/c^{2}
for each
k\geq 1

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. on the event that
\tau_{k}<\infty
while
Y_{\tau_{0}}=r^{2}/c^{2}
since
Y
has only upward jumps. It follows that conditionally on
\tau_{k}<\infty
and
Y|_{[0,\tau_{k}]}
, the random variable
\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}
is stochastically dominated from above by
\tau_{1}-\tau_{0}
. Set
\widetilde{Y}_{t}=C^{-1}Y_{C^{1/2}t}
with
C=r^{2}/c^{2}
. Then the scaling property of
Y
implies that
\widetilde{Y}
has the law of a
3/2
-stable CSBP starting from
1
. Moreover, by (
3.5
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau_{k+1}<\infty\,|\,\tau_{k}<\infty]\leq\mathbb{P}[\tau_{1}<\infty]\leq\mathbb{P}[\zeta>r/c]=p_{0}

where
p_{0}\in(0,1)
is a constant which does not depend on
r
or
c
. Therefore we obtain that
K
is stochastically dominated by a geometric random variable with parameter
p_{0}
. It follows that there exists a universal constant
M_{0}>0
so that

(4.3)

\mathbb{P}[K\geq c^{\prime}]\leq e^{-2M_{0}c^{\prime}}\quad\text{for all}\quad c^{\prime}\geq 1.

As the event that
\sup_{s\in[0,r]}Y_{(d(x,y)-s)\vee 0}\leq r^{2}/c^{2}
and
d(x,y)\geq r
implies
K\geq\lfloor c-\rfloor
, where
\lfloor c-\rfloor:=\max(\mathbb{Z}\cap(-\infty,c))
, we thus see from (
4.3
) that

(4.4)

\mathbb{P}[\sup_{s\in[0,r]}Y_{(d(x,y)-s)\vee 0}\leq r^{2}/c^{2},\ d(x,y)\geq r]\leq e^{-M_{0}c}\quad\text{for all}\quad c>1.

We note that
d(x,y)\leq r
implies that
\sup_{s\in[0,r]}Y_{(d(x,y)-s)\vee 0}=\sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s}\geq r^{2}>r^{2}/c^{2}
. Combining this with (
4.4
), we thus have

(4.5)

\mathbb{P}[\sup_{s\in[0,r]}Y_{(d(x,y)-s)\vee 0}\leq r^{2}/c^{2}]\leq e^{-M_{0}c}\quad\text{for all}\quad c>1.

Step 2.
We emphasize that
Y
has upward but no downward jumps. Therefore it hits points at its running infimum continuously. We now let
\zeta_{0}=\inf\{s\geq 0:\sup_{u\in[0,s]}Y_{u}\geq r^{2},\ Y_{s}=r^{2}/c^{2}\}
and
\xi_{0}=\inf\{s\geq\zeta_{0}:Y_{s}=r^{2}/(2c^{2})\}
.
Given that
\zeta_{0},\xi_{0},\ldots,\zeta_{k},\xi_{k}
have been defined, we let
\zeta_{k+1}=\inf\{s\geq\xi_{k}:Y_{s}>r^{2}/c^{2}\}
and
\xi_{k+1}=\inf\{s\geq\zeta_{k+1}:Y_{s}=r^{2}/(2c^{2})\}
. Let
N=\max\{k\geq 0:\zeta_{k}<\infty\}
.
Note that
\mathbb{P}[\zeta_{k+1}<\infty\,|\,\zeta_{k}<\infty]=1-\mathbb{P}[X_{\widehat{\tau}}=-\frac{1}{2}]
, where
X
has the law of a
3/2
-stable Lévy process starting from
0
with only upward jumps and
\widehat{\tau}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\notin[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]\}
. This follows by scaling and the strong Markov property of a
3/2
-stable CSBP, together with the Lamperti transform. Also,
[
20
, Chapter VII, Theorem 8]
implies that
\mathbb{P}[X_{\widehat{\tau}}=-\frac{1}{2}]>0
. In particular, there exists a universal constant
p_{1}\in(0,1)
so that
N
is stochastically dominated by a geometric random variable with parameter
p_{1}
. Thus, there exists a universal constant
M_{1}>0
such that

(4.6)

\mathbb{P}[N\geq n]\leq e^{-M_{1}n}\quad\text{for all}\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

By (
4.5
), off an event with probability at most
e^{-M_{0}c}
, there exists
k\geq 0
so that

d(x,y)-r<\xi_{k}<\infty
and so that
Y|_{[\xi_{k},\xi_{k}^{\prime}]}
, where
\xi_{k}^{\prime}=\inf\{s\geq\xi_{k}:Y_{s}=r^{2}/(4c^{2})\}
,
describes the boundary length evolution of
\partial B^{\bullet}(x,s)
for some interval of
s\in[0,r]
.

Step 3.
Let
Z
be a
3/2
-stable Lévy process with only upward jumps starting from
r^{2}/(2c^{2})
(run even after hitting
(-\infty,0)
) and let
\xi=\inf\{t\geq 0:Z_{t}=r^{2}/(4c^{2})\}
.
For each
k\geq 0
, by the Lamperti transform, conditionally on the event
\{\zeta_{k}<\infty\}=\{\xi_{k}<\infty\}
we have for each
u>0
that the number of upward jumps made by
Y|_{[\xi_{k},\xi_{k}^{\prime}]}
with size at least
u
is equal in distribution to the number of jumps that
Z|_{[0,\xi]}
makes of size at least
u
.

Let
t>0
. Recalling the form of the Lévy measure for
Z
, we know that the number of upward jumps of size at least
u
made by
Z|_{[0,tr^{3}/c^{3}]}
is Poisson with parameter given by
c_{0}(tr^{3}/c^{3})u^{-3/2}
for a constant
c_{0}>0
.
In particular, if we fix
\lambda\geq 1
and let
u=(\tfrac{c_{0}tr^{3}}{c^{3}\lambda})^{2/3}
then by (
4.1
) the probability that
Z|_{[0,tr^{3}/c^{3}]}
makes fewer than
\lambda/2
such jumps is at most

(4.7)

\exp(-a_{0}\lambda)\quad\text{for}\quad a_{0}=1+\tfrac{1}{2}\log\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}>0.

Note that
\mathbb{P}[\xi\leq tr^{3}/c^{3}]=\mathbb{P}[T(r^{2}/(4c^{2}))\leq tr^{3}/c^{3}]
, where
T(r^{2}/(4c^{2}))
is the first time that a
3/2
-stable Lévy process with only upward jumps and starting from
0
hits
-r^{2}/(4c^{2})
. Set
q=c_{1}(tr/c)^{-3}
for
c_{1}>0
sufficiently small (to be chosen). Then, the proof of
[
20
, Chapter VII, Corollary 2]
implies that

(4.8)

\mathbb{P}[T(r^{2}/(4c^{2}))\leq tr^{3}/c^{3}]=\mathbb{P}[\exp(-qT(r^{2}/(4c^{2})))\geq\exp(-qtr^{3}/c^{3})]\leq\exp(-a_{1}t^{-2}),

where we choose
c_{1}
sufficiently small so that
a_{1}=c_{1}^{2/3}(\frac{1}{4}-c_{1}^{1/3})>0
.

Take
t=\lambda^{-1/2}
so that
t^{-2}=\lambda
. By decreasing the value of
a_{1}>0
if necessary and combining (
4.7
) and (
4.8
),
we see for each
k\geq 0
that conditionally on
\{\zeta_{k}<\infty\}=\{\xi_{k}<\infty\}=\{N\geq k\}
the probability that
Y|_{[\xi_{k},\xi_{k}^{\prime}]}
makes fewer than
\lambda/2
jumps of size at least
u
is at most

(4.9)

2e^{-a_{1}\lambda}.

Note that with the choice
t=\lambda^{-1/2}
, we have that
u=c_{1}\lambda^{-1}r^{2}/c^{2}
where
c_{1}=c_{0}^{2/3}
.

By a union bound and combining (
4.9
) with (
4.6
) we have that, the probability that there exists
0\leq k\leq N
such that
Y|_{[\xi_{k},\xi_{k}^{\prime}]}
makes fewer than
\lambda/2
upward jumps of size at least
u
, is for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
at most

(4.10)

2e^{-a_{1}\lambda}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}[\xi_{k}<\infty]+e^{-M_{1}n}\leq c_{2}e^{-a_{1}\lambda}+e^{-M_{1}n}

with
M_{1}>0
as in (
4.6
) and
c_{2}=2(1-e^{-M_{1}})^{-1}
and is thus at most
c_{2}e^{-a_{1}\lambda}
by letting
n\to\infty
in (
4.10
).

Combining this with (
4.5
) implies that the probability that the metric exploration started from
x
and targeted at
y
run for time
r
disconnects fewer than
\lambda/2
components with boundary length at least
c_{1}\lambda^{-1}r^{2}/c^{2}
is at most
c_{2}e^{-a_{1}\lambda}+e^{-M_{0}c}
. We take
\lambda=(\frac{c_{1}}{c_{3}})c
for some constants
c_{3},c_{4}>0
(to be chosen) so that the holes have boundary length at least
c_{3}r^{2}/c^{3}
.

Step 4.

Next we consider the components with boundary length at least
c_{3}r^{2}/c^{3}
,
disconnected by the metric exploration started from
x
and targeted at
y
run for time
r
. Then
there exists
p_{2}\in(0,1)
which does not depend on
r,c
so that conditionally on their boundary lengths
each such component independently has probability at least
p_{2}
of having area at least
r^{4}/c^{6}
within distance
r
from its boundary. Indeed, each component is a Brownian disk conditionally given its boundary length which is at least
c_{3}r^{2}/c^{3}
. So the claim becomes that there exists
p_{2}\in(0,1)
which does not depend on
r,c
such that conditionally on the boundary lengths of the above holes, each such hole independently has probability at least
p_{2}
of having area at least
r^{4}/c^{6}
within metric distance
r
from the boundary. By the scaling properties of Brownian disks this is equivalent to the statement that a Brownian disk with boundary length
\ell\in[c_{3},\infty)
has area at least
1
in the
c^{3/2}
-neighborhood of its boundary, which is further equivalent to the statement that a Brownian disk with boundary length
1
has area at least
\ell^{-2}
in the
c^{3/2}\ell^{-1/2}
-metric neighborhood of its boundary, where
c\geq c_{4}
and
\ell\geq c_{3}
. We will first prove the claim for a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
. Note that we have the disintegration
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=1}[\cdot]=\int_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{A=\alpha,L=1}[\cdot]s\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{3}}}\exp(-1/(2\alpha))d\alpha
. Thus it suffices to prove the claim for a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{A=\alpha,L=1}
where the constant
p_{2}
can be made to uniform in
\alpha\in[1,2]
. Fix
\alpha\in[1,2],\ell\geq c_{3},c\geq c_{4}
and let
\widetilde{\alpha}
be as in Lemma
4.2
with
\alpha_{1}=1,\alpha_{2}=2
and
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}
. Suppose that we have a coupling between a sample
(\mathbb{H},h)
from
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{A=\alpha,L=1}
and a quantum wedge of weight 2
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{h},0,\infty)
such that with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
, the metric spaces
B_{d_{h}}(w,\widetilde{\alpha})
and
B_{d_{\widetilde{h}}}(0,\widetilde{\alpha})
agree in the sense of Lemma
4.2
, where
w
is chosen uniformly with respect to the boundary length measure
\nu_{h}
on
\mathbb{H}
and suppose that this event holds. Hence, it suffices to prove the corresponding claim for
B_{d_{\widetilde{h}}}(0,\widetilde{\alpha})
with high probability if we take
c_{3},c_{4}
sufficiently large. To show this, first we assume that
c^{3/2}\ell^{-1/2}\leq\widetilde{\alpha}
and note that
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{h},0,\infty)
is scale invariant in the sense that
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{h},0,\infty)
and
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{h}+\widetilde{C},0,\infty)
have the same law for each
\widetilde{C}\in\mathbb{R}
(see
[
35
, Proposition 4.7]
). Note that adding a constant
\widetilde{C}
to the field scales areas by
e^{\gamma\widetilde{C}}
and distances by
e^{\gamma\widetilde{C}/4}
. We pick
\widetilde{C}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
c^{3/2}\ell^{-1/2}e^{\gamma\widetilde{C}/4}=1
, and so the statement for
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{h},0,\infty)
becomes equivalent to the statement that the
1
-metric neighborhood of
0
has area at least
c^{-6}
. Note that the LQG metric induces the Euclidean topology on
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
(see
[
54
, Theorem 1.3]
) and that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}
gives positive mass to every open subset of
\mathbb{H}
a.s. Hence, we can pick
c_{4}>0
sufficiently large such that with probability at least
2/3
, we have that the
1
-metric neighborhood of
0
with respect to
d_{\widetilde{h}}
has area at least
c^{-6}
, which implies that the claim holds for
(\mathbb{H},h)
with probability at least
1/6
. Suppose now that
c^{3/2}\ell^{-1/2}\geq\widetilde{\alpha}>0
. In that case, we need to bound from below the probability of the even that the
\widetilde{\alpha}
-metric neighborhood of
0
with respect to
d_{\widetilde{h}}
has area at least
\ell^{-2}
. Then it suffices to bound from below the probability that the
\widetilde{\alpha}
-metric neighborhood of
0
with respect to
d_{\widetilde{h}}
has area at least
c_{3}^{-2}
. Therefore, using the scale invariance of
(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{h},0,\infty)
and arguing as before, we obtain that we can choose
c_{3}>0
sufficiently large (depending only on
\widetilde{\alpha}
) such that the probability of the latter event is at least
2/3
. It follows that the claim holds for
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{A=\alpha,L=1}
with probability at least
1/6
, for each
\alpha\in[1,2]
, and hence the same is true for
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=1}
by possibly taking the lower bound on the probability to be smaller. Finally, to deduce the claim for
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=1}
, we note that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=1}=\Big(\int\nu(\mathcal{D})d\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=1}\Big)^{-1}\nu(\mathcal{D})d\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=1}

and that
\nu(\mathcal{D})
has finite moments of all orders under
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=1}
. Therefore, the claim holds by combining with Hölder’s inequality.

Step 5.
Note that the event
\nu(B(x,2r))\leq r^{4}/c^{6}
implies that either there are fewer than
\lambda/2=c_{1}c/(2c_{3})
disks of boundary length at least
c_{3}r^{2}/c^{3}
cut off by
the metric exploration started from
x
and targeted at
y
run for time
r
or there are at least
\lambda/2
disks cut off and each of them has area less than
r^{4}/c^{6}
at distance
r
from its boundary.
As we showed above in Step 3, the former event has probability at most
c_{2}e^{-a_{1}\lambda}+e^{-M_{0}c}
. Since the disks are conditionally independent given their boundary length, the conditional probability that all of the disks cut off
have area less than
r^{4}/c^{6}
each at distance
r
from their boundary given there are at least
\lambda/2
of them is at most
e^{-a_{2}\lambda}
for a constant
a_{2}>0
. Altogether, this gives that

\mathbb{P}[\nu(B(x,2r))\leq r^{4}/c^{6}]\leq c_{2}\exp(-a_{1}c)+\exp(-a_{2}c)+\exp(-M_{0}c).

Replacing
c
with
c^{2/3}
and
r
with
r/2
implies the result.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
c>0
and suppose that
Y
is a
3/2
-stable CSBP starting from
Y_{0}\in[0,c]
. Let
\zeta=\inf\{r\geq 0:Y_{r}=0\}
. There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
so that

\mathbb{P}\big[\sup_{r}Y_{r}\leq c,\zeta\geq T\big]\leq\exp(-c_{0}\tfrac{T}{c^{1/2}})\quad\text{for all}\quad T\geq c^{1/2}.

Proof.

Let
c=1
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{r}=\sigma(Y_{s}:s\leq r)
. Then there exists
p_{0}\in(0,1)
so that

\mathbb{P}[\zeta\leq r+1\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{r}]\,{\bf 1}_{\{\zeta\geq r,Y_{r}\leq 1\}}\geq p_{0}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\zeta\geq r,Y_{r}\leq 1\}}.

Iterating this implies the desired bound for
c=1
. The general case
c>0
follows from the case
c=1
and the scaling property of
3/2
-stable CSBPs (see just after (
3.4
)).
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is sampled from
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
conditioned on
D=\{d(x,y)>1\}
.
Suppose that
u\in\mathcal{S}
is picked independently from
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{S})
and let
M_{r,c}=\{\nu(B(u,r))\leq r^{4}/c^{4}\}

for
r\in(0,1)
and
c\geq 1
. For each
\rho\in(0,1)
there exist constants
c_{0},M_{0},M_{1}>0
such that for all
r\in(0,1)
and
c\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}[M_{r,c},\ \nu(B(u,1/8))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\rho]\leq c_{0}r^{-2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{2}\exp(-M_{0}c^{2/3})+\exp(-M_{1}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{2}).

Proof.

In the proof, we will work under the measure
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
and denote it conditioned on an event
E
with
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[E]\in(0,\infty)
by
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\cdot\,|\,E]
. Let
(y_{j})
be an i.i.d. sequence in
\mathcal{S}
picked from
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{S})
. Let
D_{j}=\{d(u,y_{j})\geq 1/8\}
. Let
Y^{j}
be the boundary length process associated with the metric exploration from
u
targeted at
y_{j}
and let
E_{j}
be the event that
\sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s}^{j}\geq r^{2}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c},\ \nu(B(u,1/8))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\rho\,|\,D\big]

\displaystyle\mspace{36mu}\leq\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\bigg[M_{r,c}\cap\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}D_{j}\,\Big|\,D\bigg]+\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\bigg[\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}D_{j}^{c},\ \nu(B(u,1/8))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\rho\,\Big|\,D\bigg]

\displaystyle\mspace{36mu}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c}\cap D_{j}\cap E_{j}^{c}\,|\,D\big]+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c}\cap E_{j}\,|\,D\big]

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mspace{65mu}+\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\bigg[\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}D_{j}^{c},\ \nu(B(u,1/8))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\rho\,\Big|\,D\bigg].

To bound the first term in the right hand side of (
4.11
), we let
E_{j,k}=\{e^{-k-1}\leq\sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s}^{j}<e^{-k}\}
and let
\zeta_{j}
be the lifetime of
Y^{j}
. With
c_{0}=1/\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[D]
, we note that

(4.12)

\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c}\cap D_{j}\cap E_{j}^{c}\,|\,D\big]\leq\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[D_{j}\cap E_{j}^{c}\,|\,D\big]\leq c_{0}\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[D_{j}\,|\,E_{j,k}\big]\,\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[E_{j,k}],

where
N=\log r^{-2}
. For
\epsilon\in(0,1)
small, we set
\tau_{\epsilon,j}=\inf\{s\geq 0:Y_{s}^{j}\geq\epsilon\}
. Then, for each
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[D_{j}\cap E_{j,k}\,|\,\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100\big]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[D_{j}\cap E_{j,k}\cap\{\zeta_{j}-\tau_{\epsilon,j}\geq 1/8\}\,|\,\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100,\tau_{\epsilon,j}<1/100,Y_{\tau_{\epsilon,j}}^{j}<e^{-k-1}\big].

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,e^{-k-1})
small. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[D_{j}\cap E_{j,k}\cap\{\zeta_{j}-\tau_{\epsilon,j}\geq 1/8\}\,|\,\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100,\tau_{\epsilon,j}<1/100,Y_{\tau_{\epsilon,j}}^{j}<e^{-k-1}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\left[\zeta_{j}-\tau_{\epsilon,j}\geq 1/8,\ \sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s+\tau_{\epsilon,j}}^{j}\leq e^{-k}\,|\,\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100,\tau_{\epsilon,j}<1/100,Y_{\tau_{\epsilon,j}}^{j}<e^{-k-1}\right].

Also, the resampling property of the Brownian disk combined with Lemma
4.4
imply that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\left[\zeta_{j}-\tau_{\epsilon,j}\geq 1/8,\ \sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s+\tau_{\epsilon,j}}^{j}\leq e^{-k}\,|\,\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100,\tau_{\epsilon,j}<1/100,Y_{\tau_{\epsilon,j}}^{j}<e^{-k-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s}^{j}\leq e^{-k},\ \zeta_{j}\geq 1/8\,|\,\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big[\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100\big]^{-1}\mathbb{P}\big[\sup_{s\geq 0}Y_{s}^{j}\leq e^{-k},\zeta_{j}\geq 1/8\big]

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\exp(-c_{0}(1/8)e^{k/2})

with
c_{1}=(\mathbb{P}[\zeta_{j}\geq 1/100])^{-1}
and
c_{0}
the constant in Lemma
4.4
. Therefore, combining with (
4.12
) we obtain that there exist universal constants
a_{1},a_{2}>0
so that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c}\cap D_{j}\cap E_{j}^{c}\big]\leq a_{1}\exp(-a_{2}r^{-1}).

We turn to bound the second term in (
4.11
). We have that

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c}\cap E_{j}\,|\,D\big]=c_{0}\,\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[M_{r,c}\cap E_{j}\cap D\big]\leq c_{0}\,\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[M_{r,c}\,|\,E_{j}\big]\,\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[E_{j}].

After possibly decreasing the value of
a_{2}>0
, Lemma
4.3
gives that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[M_{r,c}\,|\,E_{j}]\leq c_{1}\exp(-a_{2}c^{2/3})
for a constant
c_{1}>0
.
Recall that under
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
we have that
Y^{j}
is distributed as a
3/2
-stable CSBP excursion. By the Lamperti transform, the amount of mass that the
3/2
-stable CSBP excursion measure puts on excursions with maximum in a given interval is the same as the amount of mass that the
3/2
-stable Lévy excursion measure (with only upward jumps) puts on such excursions.
Recall that the distribution of the supremum under the
3/2
-stable Lévy excursion measure is given by a constant times
t^{-2}\,dt
.
Therefore the same is also true for the
3/2
-stable CSBP excursion measure. Hence we have that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[E_{j}]=b_{0}r^{-2}
for a constant
b_{0}>0
. Combining, we have that (
4.13
) is at most a constant times
r^{-2}\exp(-a_{2}c^{2/3})
.

The last term in (
4.11
) is at most
\rho^{n}
, since on the event

\{\nu(B(u,1/8))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\rho\}
the
\nu
-probability of
D_{j}^{c}
is at most
\rho

and
(D_{j}^{c})_{j}
are independent. Taking
n=(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{2}
completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
, lower bound.

It suffices to prove the assertion for a sample
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
from
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}

conditioned on
\{d(x,y)>1\}
, since
(\mathcal{S},\delta d,\delta^{4}\nu,x,y)
is then a sample
from
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
conditioned on
\{d(x,y)>\delta\}
for each
\delta\in(0,\infty)
and
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\nu(\mathcal{S})>0\,|\,d(x,y)>1\big]=1
.
Let
(x_{j})
be an i.i.d. sequence chosen from
\nu
. Fix
\rho\in(0,1)
and let
E_{\rho}
be the event that
\sup_{z\in\mathcal{S}}\nu(B(z,1/8))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\rho
.
Note that
\mathbb{P}[E_{\rho}]\to 1
as
\rho
increases to
1
. Fix
u^{\prime}>u>0
. Lemma
4.5
implies that
\mathbb{P}[\nu(B(x_{j},r))\leq r^{4}/(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{6+u},\ E_{\rho}]
decays to
0
faster than any power of
r
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, since
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\nu(\mathcal{S})<\infty\,|\,d(x,y)>1\big]=1
, it is a.s. the case under
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\cdot\,|\,d(x,y)>1\big]
that there exists
r_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\nu(B(z,r))/\nu(\mathcal{S})\geq r^{4+u}
for each
r\in(0,r_{0}),z\in\mathcal{S}
, by the corresponding property of the unit area Brownian map. Suppose that the above holds and pick
z\in\mathcal{S}
uniformly sampled from
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{S})
and independent of
(x_{j})
. Set
N=r^{-4-u^{\prime}}
and
A=\mathcal{S}\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{N}B(x_{j},r/2),\widetilde{A}=\mathcal{S}\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{N}B(x_{j},r)
. Then, the probability under
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{S})
that
z\notin\cup_{j=1}^{N}B(x_{j},r/2)
is at most
(1-(r/2)^{4+u})^{N}\leq\exp(-r^{u-u^{\prime}}/2^{4+u})
. In particular, we have that
\nu(A)/\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq\exp(-r^{u-u^{\prime}}/2^{4+u})
. Suppose that
\widetilde{A}\neq\emptyset
and fix
w\in\widetilde{A}
. Then
\nu(B(w,r/2)/\nu(\mathcal{S})\geq(r/2)^{4+u}
. If
B(w,r/2)\subseteq A
, then
(r/2)^{4+u}\leq\exp(-r^{u-u^{\prime}}/2^{4+u})
and that is a contradiction for
r>0
sufficiently small. Hence there exists
1\leq j\leq N
and
\widetilde{w}\in B(x_{j},r/2)\cap B(w,r/2)
which implies that
d(w,x_{j})<r
, and that is a contradiction. Therefore, we have that
\mathcal{S}=\cup_{j=1}^{N}B(x_{j},r)
with high probability if
r>0
is chosen sufficiently small. Suppose that both of the events
\{\mathcal{S}=\cup_{j=1}^{N}B(x_{j},r)\}
and
E_{\rho}\cap\bigl(\cap_{j=1}^{N}\{\nu(B(x_{j},r))\geq r^{4}\log(r^{-1})^{-6-u}\}\bigr)
hold. Then for each
w\in\mathcal{S}
, there exists
1\leq j\leq N
such that
w\in B(x_{j},r)
and so
B(x_{j},r)\subseteq B(w,2r)
. In particular, we have that
\nu(B(x,2r))\geq\nu(B(x_{j},r))\geq r^{4}\log(r^{-1})^{-6-u}
. The proof is then complete since the probability of
E_{\rho}\cap\bigl(\cap_{j=1}^{N}\{\nu(B(x_{j},r))\geq r^{4}\log(r^{-1})^{-6-u}\}\bigr)
can be made to be arbitrarily close to that of
E_{\rho}
if
r>0
is sufficiently small.
∎

4.2.
Upper bound

We will prove the upper bound in Theorem
4.1
by dominating the amount of area inside of the metric ball from above using a subcritical Galton-Watson tree with geometric offspring distribution. The branching structure will come from a metric exploration on the Brownian map or Brownian disk. We will begin in Lemma
4.6
by bounding the tail of the maximum of the boundary length of a filled metric ball explored up to radius
r
and then deduce from this in Lemma
4.7
an upper bound on the maximum boundary length of any of the complementary components a metric ball explored up to radius
r
. We will then proceed in Lemma
4.8
to prove a bound for the amount of area near the boundary of a Brownian disk, which will eventually be used to show that the dominating Galton-Watson tree is subcritical.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
has distribution
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
. Let
r>0
, and let

M_{r}=\sup_{s\in[0,r]}L_{s}
where
L_{s}
is the boundary length of
\partial B^{\bullet}(x,s)
.
There exist constants
c_{0},m_{0}>0
which are independent of
r
so that

\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[M_{r}\geq cr^{2}]\leq m_{0}e^{-c_{0}c}r^{-2}\quad\text{for all}\quad c\geq 1.

Proof.

Let
Y_{t}
be the boundary length of
\partial B^{\bullet}(x,s)
where
s=d(x,y)-t
so that
Y_{t}
evolves as a
3/2
-stable CSBP excursion. Let
\tau_{1}=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}\geq cr^{2}\}
and
\sigma_{1}=\inf\{t\geq\tau_{1}:Y_{t}=cr^{2}/2\}
. Given that
\tau_{1},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\tau_{j},\sigma_{j}
have been defined, let
\tau_{j+1}=\inf\{t\geq\sigma_{j}:Y_{t}\geq cr^{2}\}
and
\sigma_{j+1}=\inf\{t\geq\tau_{j+1}:Y_{t}=cr^{2}/2\}
. Let
J=\max\{j:\sigma_{j}<\infty\}
(
J=0
if
\sigma_{1}=\infty
) and
\zeta=\inf\{t>0:Y_{t}=0\}
. Note that the event that
M_{r}\geq cr^{2}
implies that
J\geq 1
and that
\zeta-\sigma_{J}\leq r
. Therefore it suffices to give an upper bound for
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[J\geq 1,\,\zeta-\sigma_{J}\leq r]
.

By the scaling properties of a
3/2
-stable CSBP (recall just after (
3.4
)), we note that there exists
p_{0}\in(0,1)
which does not depend on
c
or
r
so that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau_{j+1}=\infty\,|\,\sigma_{j}<\infty]\geq p_{0}
. Therefore
J
under
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\cdot\mid\sigma_{1}<\infty]
is stochastically dominated by a geometric random variable with parameter
p_{0}
. Moreover, by (
3.5
), we have for a constant
c_{0}>0
that

\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\zeta-\sigma_{j}\leq r\,|\,\sigma_{j}<\infty\big]=e^{-c_{0}c}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[J\geq 1,\,\zeta-\sigma_{J}\leq r]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[J\geq j,\,\zeta-\sigma_{j}\leq r]=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-c_{0}c}\,\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[J\geq j]

\displaystyle=e^{-c_{0}c}\,\int J\,d\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\leq m_{0}e^{-c_{0}c}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\sigma_{1}<\infty],

where
m_{0}
is the mean of a geometric random variable with parameter
p_{0}
.
We note that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\sigma_{1}<\infty]=\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau_{1}<\infty]
is the same as the measure under the infinite measure on
3/2
-stable Lévy excursions that the maximum is at least
cr^{2}
. This, in turn, is equal to a constant times
c^{-1}r^{-2}
as shown in the paragraph of (
4.13
).
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
is distributed according to
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
. Fix
u>0
. The
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
-measure of the event that there exists
s\in(0,r]
such that some component of
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,s)}
has boundary length larger than
r^{2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{1+u}
decays to
0
as
r\to 0
faster than any polynomial of
r
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be the first
s\geq 0
such that
\mathcal{S}\setminus\overline{B(x,s)}
has a component with boundary length at least
r^{2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{1+u}
. On
\tau\leq r
, we let
\mathcal{D}
be such a component, breaking ties in a measurable manner, and we let
\ell
be the boundary length of
\partial\mathcal{D}
. Then we know that the conditional law of
\mathcal{D}
given
\ell
is given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
if
y\notin\mathcal{D}
and is given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=\ell}
if
y\in\mathcal{D}
.
By (
3.7
), (
3.6
), and the strong Markov property of the metric exploration, conditionally on
\tau\leq r
, the probability that
\nu(\mathcal{D})\leq r^{4}
decays to
0
as
r\to 0
faster than any polynomial of
r
.
Next, we note that
0<\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[s\leq\tau\leq r\big]\leq\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})\geq s\big]<\infty
for each
r>0
,
s\in(0,r)
and the above imply that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\nu(\mathcal{D})\leq r^{4}\,|\,s\leq\tau\leq r\big]\leq\frac{1}{2}
for each
r>0
sufficiently small, uniformly in
s\in(0,r)
. Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[s\leq\tau\leq r\big]

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[s\leq\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{D})\leq r^{4}\big]+\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[s\leq\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{D})>r^{4}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[s\leq\tau\leq r\big]+\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[s\leq\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{D})>r^{4}\big],

for all
r\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, and all
s\in(0,r)
. Sending
s\to 0
gives that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\tau\leq r\big]\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\tau\leq r\big]+\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{D})>r^{4}\big]

which implies that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\tau\leq r\big]\leq 2\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}\big[\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{D})>r^{4}\big]
for all
r\in(0,1)
sufficiently small.
Let
p>0
and recall that the
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
mass of the event that
\nu(\mathcal{S})\geq r^{-p}
is
O(r^{p/2})
(as it is the same as the mass put by the infinite measure on Brownian excursions on those excursions with length at least
r^{-p}
).
On
\tau\leq r
,
\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq r^{-p}
and
\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq r^{4}
, the conditional probability that
y\in\mathcal{D}
is at least
r^{4+p}
as the conditional law of
y
given
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu)
is
\nu
normalized to be a probability measure (i.e.,
\nu/\nu(\mathcal{S})
). Let
M_{r}
be as in Lemma
4.6
. Altogether, we have shown that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau\leq r]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq r^{-p},\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq r^{4}]+2\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{S})>r^{-p}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq r^{-p},\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq r^{4}]+O(r^{p/2})

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{-4-p}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\tau\leq r,\nu(\mathcal{S})\leq r^{-p},\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq r^{4},y\in\mathcal{D}]+O(r^{p/2})

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{-4-p}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[M_{r}\geq r^{2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{1+u}]+O(r^{p/2}).

This completes the proof as Lemma
4.6
implies that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[M_{r}\geq r^{2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{1+u}]
decays to
0
as
r\to 0
faster than any polynomial of
r
and
p>0
was arbitrary.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

There exist
c_{0}\geq 1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
such that the following is true for all
\ell\in(0,1]
and
c\geq c_{0}
. Suppose that
(\mathcal{D},d,\nu)
has law given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
conditioned on having area at least
c
. Consider the center exploration from
\partial\mathcal{D}
run for one unit of time. Let
p_{c}
be the probability that this exploration cuts off a component with area at least
c
. Then
p_{c}\leq 1-\delta
.
The same also holds if we instead suppose that
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
has law
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
conditioned on having area at least
c
and consider the metric exploration starting at
x
and targeted at
y
.

Proof.

First, let
\ell\in(0,1]
and let
(\mathcal{D},d,\nu)
have law given by
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}
conditioned on having area at least
c
.
Let
E_{c}
denote the event that the center exploration from
\partial\mathcal{D}
which is targeted at
y
and run for one unit of time cuts off a component from
y
with area at least
c
.
Note that
p_{c}=\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}[E_{c}]/\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}[A_{c}]
where
A_{c}=\{\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq c\}
. By (
3.7
), for each
\epsilon>0
there exists
c_{0}\geq 1
so that
c\geq c_{0}
implies that

(4.14)

\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}[A_{c}]\geq(1-\epsilon)\frac{\sqrt{2}\ell^{3}}{3\sqrt{\pi}c^{3/2}}.

Let
(a_{i})
be the set of downward jumps made by the center exploration. It follows from a union bound, (
3.7
), and (
3.8
) (with
\alpha=3
) that there exists
0<\delta<1
so that

(4.15)

\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}[E_{c}]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i}\frac{\sqrt{2}a_{i}^{3}}{3\sqrt{\pi}c^{3/2}}\right]\leq\frac{(1-2\delta)\sqrt{2}\ell^{3}}{3\sqrt{\pi}c^{3/2}}.

Combining (
4.14
) and (
4.15
), we have that

p_{c}=\frac{\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}[E_{c}]}{\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell}[A_{c}]}\leq\frac{1-2\delta}{1-\epsilon}.

Thus, by taking
\epsilon\in(0,1)
sufficiently small such that
\frac{1-2\delta}{1-\epsilon}\leq 1-\delta
, we obtain that there exists
c_{0}\geq 1
sufficiently large such that
p_{c}\leq 1-\delta
, for each
c\geq c_{0}
.

Next, let
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
have law
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
conditioned on having area at least
c
.
Let
E_{c}
denote the event that the metric exploration starting at
x
, targeted at
y

and run for one unit of time cuts off a component from
y
with area at least
c
. Then
recalling that
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\cdot]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=a}[\cdot]c_{0}a^{-3/2}\,da

as explained in Subsection
3.1.1
, we obtain

(4.16)

\displaystyle p_{c}=\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[E_{c}\mid\nu(\mathcal{S})\geq c]

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}[\nu(B^{\bullet}(x,1))\geq c\mid\nu(\mathcal{S})\geq c]

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{c}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=a}[\nu(B^{\bullet}(x,1))\geq c]a^{-3/2}\,da}{\int_{c}^{\infty}a^{-3/2}\,da}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}^{A=b}\bigl[\nu(B^{\bullet}(x,c^{-1/4}))\geq 1\bigr]b^{-3/2}\,db\xrightarrow{c\to\infty}0,

proving the assertion for
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
with law
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
.
∎

In the following two lemmas and the completion of the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.1
given below, we will consider the following exploration. Suppose that
r\in(0,1/e]
,
c>1
and
u>0
. Fix
\ell\in[0,r^{2}(\log r^{-1})^{3+4u}]
. If
\ell>0
, we suppose that we have a Brownian disk
(\mathcal{D},d,\nu)
with boundary length
\ell
conditioned on having area at least
cr^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{6+8u}
. Consider the center exploration from
\partial\mathcal{D}
. Let
\tau_{1}
be the first time that the center exploration has cut off a component with area at least
cr^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{6+8u}
. Given that
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}
have been defined, let
\tau_{k+1}
be the first time after
\tau_{k}
that the center exploration cuts off another component with area at least
cr^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{6+8u}
. If
\ell=0
, we suppose that we have a Brownian map instance
(\mathcal{S},d,\nu,x,y)
conditioned on having area at least
cr^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{6+8u}
and define the exploration analogously except starting at
x
and targeted at
y
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that we have the setup described just above. Let

E_{r}=\Bigl\{\sup_{s\in[0,r]}L_{s}\leq r^{2}(\log r^{-1})^{3+4u}\Bigr\}

denote the event that the boundary length
L_{s}
of the metric exploration is at most
r^{2}(\log r^{-1})^{3+4u}
for all
s\in[0,r]
. There exist
c_{0}>1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
which are independent of
r,u,\ell
so that for all
c\geq c_{0}
and
r\in(0,1/e]
the following is true. With
N_{r}=\sup\{k:\tau_{k}\leq r\}
(
\sup\emptyset:=0
) we have that the probability of
E_{r}\cap\{N_{r}\geq n\}
is at most
(1-\delta)\widetilde{p}_{c}^{n-1}
where
\widetilde{p}_{c}\in(0,\infty)
depends only on
c
and satisfies
\widetilde{p}_{c}\to 0
as
c\to\infty
.

Proof.

Recall that by rescaling
boundary lengths by
(r^{2}(\log r^{-1})^{3+4u})^{-1}
, areas by
(r^{4}(\log r^{-1})^{6+8u})^{-1}

and distances by
(r(\log r^{-1})^{3/2+2u})^{-1/2}
, we obtain a sample from the law
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=\ell_{r}}

with
\ell_{r}
given by
\ell_{r}=\ell(r^{2}(\log r^{-1})^{3+4u})^{-1}\in(0,1]
conditioned on having area at least
c
if
\ell>0
,
and a sample from the law
\mu_{\mathrm{BM}}
conditioned on having area at least
c
if
\ell=0
.
Under this rescaling, the event
E_{r}
and the random variable
N_{r}
become

(4.17)

\displaystyle E_{r}=\Bigl\{\sup_{s\in[0,r_{0}]}L_{s}\leq 1\Bigr\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{r}=\sup\{k:\tau_{k}\leq r_{0}\},

respectively, where
r_{0}=(\log r^{-1})^{-3/2-2u}
, and we are to prove the assertion for these
E_{r}
and
N_{r}
instead.

Set
E_{k}=\{\sup_{s\in[0,\tau_{k}]}L_{s}\leq 1\}
for each
k\geq 1
. For each
t\in[0,\infty)
,
let
\mathcal{F}_{t}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{L_{r}\}_{r\in[0,t]}
and the components cut off
by the exploration up to time
t
. Then for each
k\geq 1
, a.s. on the event
\{\tau_{k}<\infty\}
,
the conditional law given
\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{k}}
of the target component at time
\tau_{k}

is
\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=L_{\tau_{k}}}
if
\ell>0
and
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=L_{\tau_{k}}}
if
\ell=0
,
and therefore by (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) we have, a.s.,

\displaystyle\prob[\tau_{k+1}\leq r_{0},\,E_{r}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{k}}]

\displaystyle\leq\prob[\tau_{k+1}\leq r_{0},\,E_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{k}}]\leq\prob[\tau_{k+1}\leq r_{0},\,E_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{k}}]

\displaystyle\leq\prob[\nu(\text{the target component at $\tau_{k}$})\geq c\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{k}}]\,{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{k}\leq r_{0}\}\cap E_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\mu_{\mathrm{BD}}^{L=L_{\tau_{k}}}[\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq c]\,{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{k}\leq r_{0}\}\cap E_{k}}&\text{if $\ell>0$,}\\
\mu_{\mathrm{BD,W}}^{L=L_{\tau_{k}}}[\nu(\mathcal{D})\geq c]\,{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{k}\leq r_{0}\}\cap E_{k}}&\text{if $\ell=0$,}\end{cases}

(4.18)
