. Suppose that the former case holds. Then (
7.3
) implies that

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{h}}(z,w)+\epsilon\geq d_{\widehat{h}}(z,x)\geq C^{-1}|z-x|^{1/\beta}\geq C^{-1}2^{-1/\beta}|z-w|^{1/\beta}.

Similarly if the latter case holds, (
7.4
) implies that

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{h}}(z,w)+\epsilon\geq d_{\widehat{h}}(w,x)\geq C^{-1}|w-x|^{1/\beta}\geq C^{-1}2^{-1/\beta}|z-w|^{1/\beta}.

Hence since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, we obtain that

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{h}}(z,w)\geq C^{-1}2^{-1/\beta}|z-w|^{1/\beta}

in either case. Combining we obtain that it is a.s. the case that there exists
C>1
such that

(7.5)

\displaystyle C^{-1}|z-w|^{1/\beta}d_{\widehat{h}}(z,w)\leq C|z-w|^{\beta}\quad\text{for all}\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

Step 2. Conclusion of the proof.

Next we will combine Step 1 with the argument in the proof of Lemma
6.5
to complete the proof of the lemma.

Recall that
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that the following is true a.s. Suppose that
f
is sampled from the group
\text{conf}(\mathbb{H})
of conformal automorphisms of
\mathbb{H}
when the latter is endowed with the Haar measure, and let
h_{1}
be sampled from the infinite measure of a weight-2 quantum disk with
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
weighted by
\nu_{h_{1}}(\partial\mathbb{H})^{-2}
. Then there exists a deterministic constant
C>0
such that the law of the field
h_{1}\circ f^{-1}+Q\log|(f^{-1})^{\prime}|
is given by
C
times the law of
\widetilde{h}-2Q\log|\cdot|_{+}+c
where
c
is sampled independently from the infinite measure on
\mathbb{R}
given by
\exp(-Qc)dc
, and recall that
\widetilde{h}
denotes a free boundary GFF on
\mathbb{H}
normalized so that its average on
\mathbb{H}\cap\partial\mathbb{D}
is equal to zero. It follows that that the field
h_{1}\circ f^{-1}\circ F^{-1}+Q\log|(F\circ f)^{-1})^{\prime}|
has the same law as
C
times the law of the field

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}\circ F^{-1}-2Q\log|F^{-1}(\cdot)|_{+}+Q\log|(F^{-1})^{\prime}|+c

where
\log|x|_{+}=\log\max(|x|,1)
.

Since
d_{\widetilde{h}\circ F^{-1}}=\exp\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}(i)/\sqrt{6}\right)d_{\widehat{h}}
, we have by (
7.5
) that it is a.s. the case that there exists
M>1
such that

\displaystyle M^{-1}|z-w|^{1/\beta}\leq d_{\widetilde{h}\circ F^{-1}}(z,w)\leq M|z-w|^{\beta}\quad\text{for all}\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

Moreover as explained in the proof of Lemma
6.5
, we have that

\displaystyle-2Q\log|F^{-1}(\cdot)|_{+}+Q\log|(F^{-1})^{\prime}(\cdot)|=O(1)

uniformly in
\mathbb{D}
. Thus it follows that almost everywhere, there exists
M>1
such that

\displaystyle M^{-1}|z-w|^{1/\beta}\leq d_{h_{1}\circ(F\circ f)^{-1}+Q\log|((F\circ f)^{-1})^{\prime}|}(z,w)\leq M|z-w|^{\beta}\quad\text{for all}\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

Combining with the fact that the event in the lemma statement is invariant under the coordinate change formula of quantum surfaces with disintegration with respect to the total boundary length of a quantum disk sampled from the infinite measure, we obtain the lemma statement for a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}^{L=\ell}
. Therefore, it also holds a.s. for a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=\ell}
since the measures
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=\ell}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}^{L=\ell}
are mutually absolutely continuous. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

By absolute continuity, it suffices to prove the claim for
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}^{L=\ell}
instead.
Let
\beta\in(0,1)
be the constant of Lemma
7.4
. Then, Lemma
7.4
implies that
\mu_{\mathrm{QD}}^{L=\ell}
-a.e., there exists a random constant
C>1
such that
C^{-1}|z-w|^{1/\beta}\leq d_{h}(z,w)\leq C|z-w|^{\beta}
for all
z,w\in\mathbb{D}
. Pick
\widetilde{M}>1
deterministic such that
\widetilde{M}\beta>1
. Then, we have that
\mathbb{D}\cap B(z,r^{\widetilde{M}})\subseteq B_{h}(z,r)
for all
z\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and all
r>0
sufficiently small, which implies that there exists
w\in\mathbb{D}
such that
B(w,r^{\widetilde{M}}/2)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\cap B_{h}(z,r)
. Fix
M>\beta^{-1}\widetilde{M}
. Then, we have that
B_{h}(w,r^{M})\subseteq B(w,r^{\widetilde{M}}/2)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\cap B_{h}(z,r)
for all
r>0
sufficiently small (independent of
z,w
) and this completes the proof.
∎

Now we proceed on proving that condition (
I
) holds with high probability for
M
large enough. As in the first paragraph of the proof of Lemma
6.10
, the main idea is to compare locally the laws of a quantum disk and a quantum wedge of weight 2, and then deduce the claim by proving that condition (
I
) holds with high probability when the surface
\mathcal{N}
is drawn on top of a sample from
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=1}
. The purpose of the following lemma is to show the latter claim.

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose that we have the same setup as in the definition of the good event just before the statement of Proposition
7.2
, where the surface
\mathcal{N}
is drawn on top of a sample
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbb{D},h,0)
from
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=1}
. Then, it is a.s. the case that there exists
\mathcal{M}_{0}\geq 1
such that condition (
I
) holds for all
M\geq\mathcal{M}_{0}
when
\delta=1
.

The main idea of the proof of Lemma
7.5
is the following. Suppose that we have the same setup as in the statement of Lemma
7.5
. Fix
M>1
sufficiently large and let
x,y\in\mathcal{N}
be such that
\text{dist}_{d_{h}}(\{x,y\},\partial\mathcal{N})\geq r
, where
r\in(0,M^{-1})
. Let also
T_{B_{h}(y,r)}
denote the total amount of time that the Liouville Brownian motion starting from
x
with respect to
h
and killed upon exiting
\mathcal{N}
spends in
B_{h}(y,r)
. Then we will show that if
M
is sufficiently large, it is very likely that there exists
t\in I_{M,r}
with

(7.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|I_{M,r}|}\int_{B_{h}(y,r)}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)d\mu_{h}(w)\gtrsim E_{x}[T_{B_{h}(y,r)}],

where

\displaystyle I_{M,r}:=[M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{-M},M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}].

Moreover we will show that with high probability (if
M
is large enough), we have that

(7.7)

\displaystyle E_{x}[T_{B_{h}(y,r)}]\gtrsim r^{M/2}.

Therefore combining (
7.6
) with (
7.7
), the proof of Lemma
7.5
will be complete.

We start with proving a lower bound for
E_{x}[T_{B_{h}(y,r)}]
. This is the content of the following lemma.

Lemma 7.6
.

Suppose that we have the same setup as in the definition of the good event just before the statement of Proposition
7.2
, where the surface
\mathcal{N}
is drawn on top of a sample
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbb{D},h,0)
from
\mu_{\mathrm{QD,W}}^{L=1}
. Set
\widetilde{h}:=h|_{\mathcal{N}}\circ\varphi^{-1}+Q\log|(\varphi^{-1})^{\prime}|
. Then there exists a deterministic constant
\alpha>0
such that it is a.s. the case that there exist
M>1,c_{0}>0
such that the following is true for all
r\in(0,M^{-1})
and all
x,y\in\mathbb{D}
such that
\text{dist}_{d_{\widetilde{h}}}(\{x,y\},\partial\mathbb{D})\geq r
. If
T_{A}
denotes the amount of time that the Liouville Brownian motion in
\mathbb{D}
with respect to
\widetilde{h}
spends in the set
A\subseteq\mathbb{D}
before exiting
\mathbb{D}
, then we have that

\displaystyle E_{x}[T_{B_{\widetilde{h}}(y,r)}]\geq c_{0}r^{\alpha+M/4}.

Let us first briefly describe the setup of the proof of Lemma
7.6
before proceeding with its proof. The setup is similar to the setup of the proof of Lemma
6.13
. Let
I\subseteq\partial\mathbb{D}
be a fixed countable and dense subset of
\partial\mathbb{D}
. Then it is a.s. the case on the event that
\sigma=\widetilde{\sigma}
, that there exist
z,w\in I
such that
\eta^{\prime}([0,\sigma])=\eta^{\prime}([0,\tau])
, where
\tau
is the first time that
\eta^{\prime}
disconnects
z
from
w
. Also we have that
z
lies in the boundary of the connected component of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,\tau])
which contains
0
. Note that
\tau
is also the first time that
\eta^{\prime}
disconnects
w
from
0
and so
\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,\tau]}
has the same law as a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
process in
\mathbb{D}
from
x
to
w
, stopped at the first time at which it disconnects
w
from
0
, and the latter time is the same time at which the chordal process disconnects
w
from
z
. Moreover the locality property of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
implies that the latter chordal process has the same law as a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
process
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{D}
from
x
to
z
, stopped at the first time
\widetilde{\tau}
at which it disconnects
z
from
w
. In particular we have that
\mathcal{N}
has the same law with the hull of
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}|_{[0,\widetilde{\tau}]}
. Combining with the rotational invariance of the law of radial
{\rm SLE}_{6}
, we obtain that it suffices to prove the claim of the lemma when
\mathcal{N}
is replaced by the hull of
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}|_{[0,\widetilde{\tau}]}
, where
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
is a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
in
\mathbb{D}
from
-i
to
z
stopped at the first time
\widetilde{\tau}
that it disconnects
z
from
w
, where
z,w
are fixed and distinct points in
I
and
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
is independent from
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

Step 1. Outline and setup.

Suppose that we have the same setup as in the paragraph just after the statement of the lemma and let
(\widetilde{K}_{t})
denote the family of hulls of
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
. Again by the locality property, we have that
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
can be coupled with a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}

\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{D}
from
-i
to
w
stopped at the first time
\widehat{\tau}
that
\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}
disconnects
z
from
w
such that
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}|_{[0,\widetilde{\tau}]}=\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}|_{[0,\widehat{\tau}]}
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
w
lies in the counterclockwise arc of
\partial\mathbb{D}
from
-i
to
z
. Let
\widehat{\eta}
denote the left outer boundary of
\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}
when viewed as a curve from
w
to
-i
. It follows from
[
70
, Theorem 1.4]
that
\widehat{\eta}
has the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{\frac{8}{3}}(\frac{8}{3}-2;\frac{8}{3}-4)
process in
\mathbb{D}
from
w
to
-i
with the force points located at
w^{-}
and
w^{+}
respectively. Let
U
be the connected component whose boundary contains
-i
of the complement in
\mathbb{D}
of the curve
\widehat{\eta}
stopped at the first time that it disconnects
-i
from
z
. Similarly we let
V
be the connected component whose boundary contains
w
of the complement in
\mathbb{D}
of the time-reversal of
\widehat{\eta}
stopped at the first time that it disconnects
w
from
z
. Let also
G
be the connected component of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\widehat{\eta}
lying to the left of
\widehat{\eta}
. Note that Proposition
A.1
implies that
\text{int}(\widetilde{K}_{\widetilde{\tau}}),U,V,G
are all Jordan domains such that
U\cup V\cup G\subseteq\text{int}(\widetilde{K}_{\widetilde{\tau}})
and let
\phi:\text{int}(\widetilde{K}_{\widetilde{\tau}})\to\mathbb{D},f:U\to\mathbb{D},g:V\to\mathbb{D}
and
\psi:G\to\mathbb{D}
be conformal transformations chosen in some arbitrary but fixed way. Let also
\widehat{I}
be the arc traced by
\widehat{\eta}
up until the last time that it hits the counterclockwise arc of
\partial\mathbb{D}
from
-i
to
z
and let
\widehat{J}
be the arc traced by the time-reversal of
\widehat{\eta}
stopped at the last time that it hits the clockwise arc of
\partial\mathbb{D}
from
-i
to
z
. Note that
\widehat{I}\cap\widehat{J}=\emptyset
. Note also that
[
85
, Theorem 5.2]
combined with the time-reversal symmetry of the law of
\widehat{\eta}
(see
[
71
, Theorem 1.1]
)) implies that there exists a deterministic constant
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that all of the maps
f^{-1},g^{-1}
and
\psi^{-1}
are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous.

Fix
M\in(1,\infty)
sufficiently large (to be chosen) and let
r\in(0,M^{-1})
. Let
x,y\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}:=\text{int}(\widetilde{K}_{\widetilde{\tau}})
be such that
\text{dist}_{d_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}}(\{x,y\},\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})\geq r
. In Step 2, we will show that the probability that a complex Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time is
\gtrsim r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
, where the implicit constant is independent of
r
and
\beta\in(0,1)
is the constant in the statement of Lemma
7.4
. Then we will conclude the proof in Step 3 as follows. Suppose that we are working on the event that the Brownian motion intersects
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time. Using the
\alpha
-Hölder continuity of the maps
\psi^{-1},f^{-1},g^{-1}
and
\phi^{-1}
, we will show that the probability that a Brownian motion starting from a point
u\in\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
intersects
B_{h}(y,r)
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time is
\gtrsim r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
, where the implicit constant is uniform in
u
and
r
. Therefore the proof will be complete by combining with the Markov property of the Brownian motion and the fact that
\mu_{h}(B_{h}(y,r))\geq r^{M/4}
if
M
is sufficiently large (see
[
46
, Lemma 3.3]
).

Step 2. Lower bound on the probability that the Brownian motion hits
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before leaving
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
.

We have the following cases.

Case 1.
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\cap(\widehat{I}\cup\widehat{J})=\emptyset
.

In that case, we have that
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\subseteq G
and so
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)=B_{h|_{G}}(x,r)
. Lemma
7.4
implies that there exists deterministic constant
\beta\in(0,1)
such that possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we have that

(7.8)

\displaystyle B(x,r^{1/\beta})\subseteq B_{h|_{G}}(x,r)\subseteq B(x,r^{\beta}).

Let
C>0
be such that
|\psi^{-1}(u)-\psi^{-1}(v)|\leq C|u-v|^{\alpha}
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{D}
. Then combining with (
7.8
) we obtain that

\displaystyle B(\psi(x),C^{-1/\alpha}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)})\subseteq B_{h|_{G}\circ\psi^{-1}+Q\log|(\psi^{-1})^{\prime}|}(\psi(x),r).

Note that the probability that starting from
\psi(x)
the Brownian motion intersects
B(0,1/2)
before leaving
\mathbb{D}
is at least a constant times
-\log|\psi(x)|
. In particular, combining with conformal invariance, we obtain that there exists a (random) constant
c_{0}>0
such that the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time is at least
c_{0}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
.

Case 2.
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\cap\widehat{I}\neq\emptyset
.

Possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we can assume that
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\cap\widehat{J}=\emptyset
and hence
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\subseteq V
, which implies that
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)=B_{h|_{V}}(x,r)
. Similarly to Case 1, we have that

\displaystyle B(g(x),C^{-1/\alpha}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)})\subseteq B_{h|_{V}\circ g^{-1}+Q\log|(g^{-1})^{\prime}|}(g(x),r),

where
C>0
is such that

\displaystyle|g^{-1}(u)-g^{-1}(v)|\leq C|u-v|^{\alpha}\quad\text{for all}\quad u,v\in\mathbb{D}.

In particular, we have that
\text{dist}(g(x),\partial\mathbb{D})\geq C^{-1/\alpha}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
and so possibly by taking the constant
c_{0}>0
in Case 1 to be smaller and combining with conformal invariance, we can assume that the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
is at least
c_{0}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
.

Case 3.
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\cap\widehat{J}\neq\emptyset
.

As in Case 2, possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we can assume that
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\cap\widehat{I}=\emptyset
and so
B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)\subseteq U
, which implies that
B_{h|_{U}}(x,r)=B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(x,r)
. Since
f^{-1}
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous, by arguing as in Case 2, we obtain that possibly by taking the constant
c_{0}>0
to be smaller, we have that the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time is at least
c_{0}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
.

It follows that in every case, there exists a (random) constant
c_{0}>0
such that the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
\psi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time is at least
c_{0}r^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
.

Step 3. Conclusion of the proof.

Next we set
A:=B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(y,r)
. Recall that Lemma
7.4
implies that possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we can assume that

\displaystyle B(u,r^{1/\beta})\subseteq B_{h}(u,r)=B_{h|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}}(u,r)\subseteq B(u,r^{\beta})

for all
u\in\mathbb{D}
such that
\text{dist}_{d_{h}}(u,\partial\mathbb{D})\geq r
.

Suppose first that
B_{h}(y,r)\cap(\widehat{I}\cup\widehat{J})=\emptyset
. Then arguing as in Case 1, we obtain that there exists a constant
C>1
such that

\displaystyle B(\psi(y),C^{-1/\alpha}(r/2)^{1/(\alpha\beta)})\subseteq\psi(B(y,(r/2)^{1/\beta})).

Then
[
60
, Exercise 2.7]
implies that there exists a (random) constant
c>0
such that for all
u\in B(0,1/2)
, the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
u
intersects
B(\psi(y),C^{-1/\alpha}(r/2)^{1/(\alpha\beta)})
before exiting
\mathbb{D}
for the first time is at least
cr^{1/(\alpha\beta)}
. Therefore combining with Step 2 and the Markov property of the Brownian motion, we obtain that the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
A
before exiting
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
for the first time is at least
cc_{0}r^{2/(\alpha\beta)}
.

Note that
[
46
, Lemma 3.3]
implies that possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we have that

\displaystyle\mu_{h}(B(y,(r/4)^{1\beta}))\geq\mu_{h}(B_{h}(y,(r/4)^{1/\beta^{2}}))\geq r^{M/4}.

It follows that

\displaystyle E_{x}[T_{A}]

\displaystyle\geq cc_{0}r^{2/(\alpha\beta)}\inf_{u\in\partial B(y,(r/2)^{1/\beta})}E_{u}[T_{B(y,(r/4)^{1/\beta})}]

\displaystyle\geq cc_{0}r^{2/(\alpha\beta)}\inf_{u\in\partial B(y,(r/2)^{1/\beta})}\int_{B(y,(r/4)^{1/\beta})}G_{B(y,r^{1/\beta})}(u,v)d\mu_{h}(v)\gtrsim r^{\frac{2}{\alpha\beta}+\frac{M}{4}}

where
G_{B(y,r^{1/\beta})}
denotes the Green’s function on
B(y,r^{1/\beta})
and in the latter inequality we also used that
G_{B(y,r^{1/\beta})}(u,\cdot)
is bounded from below on
B(y,(r/4)^{1/\beta})
by a universal constant which is uniform on
u\in\partial B(y,(r/2)^{1/\beta})
.

By arguing in the same way in the cases that either
B_{h}(y,r)\cap\widehat{I}\neq\emptyset
or
B_{h}(y,r)\cap\widehat{J}\neq\emptyset
, we obtain that

\displaystyle E_{x}[T_{A}]\gtrsim r^{\frac{2}{\alpha\beta}+\frac{M}{4}}

in both cases and so this completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

Step 1. Outline and setup.

Let
\alpha>0
be the deterministic constant in Lemma
7.6
and let
M>0
be sufficiently large such that the statement of Lemma
7.6
holds. Set
\widetilde{h}:=h|_{\mathcal{N}}\circ\varphi^{-1}+Q\log|(\varphi^{-1})^{\prime}|
and fix
x,y\in\mathbb{D},r\in(0,M^{-1})
such that
\text{dist}_{d_{\widetilde{h}}}(\{x,y\},\partial\mathbb{D})\geq r
. Set also
A:=B_{\widetilde{h}}(x,r)
and as in Lemma
7.6
, we let
T_{A}
denote the amount of time that the Liouville Brownian motion in
\mathbb{D}
with respect to
\widetilde{h}
spends in
A
. Then Lemma
7.6
implies that there exists a (random) constant
c_{0}>0
depending only on
M
such that

(7.9)

\displaystyle E_{x}[T_{A}]\geq c_{0}r^{\alpha+\frac{M}{4}}.

Moreover
[
46
, Lemma 3.3]
implies that it is a.s. the case that possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we have that

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{h}(B_{h}(u,r))\geq r^{\frac{M}{4}}\quad\text{for all}\quad u\in\mathbb{D}\quad\text{such that}\quad\text{dist}_{d_{h}}(u,\partial\mathbb{D})\geq r.

Set

\displaystyle f_{1}(M,r):=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)dtd\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)

\displaystyle f_{2}(M,r):=\int_{A}\int_{0}^{M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{-M}}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)dtd\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w).

In Step 2, we will show that
f_{1}(M,r)\to 0
as
r\to 0
faster than any positive power of
r
while in Step 3 we will complete the proof of the lemma by bounding
f_{2}(M,r)
from above and combining with (
7.9
).

Step 2.
f_{1}(M,r)
tends to zero as
r\to 0
faster than any positive power of
r
.

First we note that
u\mapsto E_{u}[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}]=\int_{\mathbb{D}}G(u,w)\,d\mu_{h}(w)
is an a.s. continuous function on
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and is therefore a.s. bounded, where
G
denotes the Green’s function on
\mathbb{D}
. By Markov’s inequality, we therefore have that

P_{u}[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}\geq t]\leq\frac{E_{u}[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}]}{t}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty

uniformly in
u\in\mathbb{D}
. In particular, by increasing the value of
M
if necessary, we have that that
P_{u}[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}\geq M]\leq 1/2
for all
u\in\mathbb{D}
. By the Markov property, we therefore have that
P_{u}[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}\geq tM]\leq 2^{-t}
for all
u\in\mathbb{D}
and
t\geq 1
. We therefore have by applying the Markov property again that

\displaystyle f_{1}(M,r)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{D}}\int_{M^{M/2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{M}}^{\infty}p_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}(\varphi^{-1}(x),w)\,dt\,d\mu_{h}(w)

\displaystyle=E_{\varphi^{-1}(x)}\big[(\tau_{\mathbb{D}}-M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}){\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\tau_{\mathbb{D}}>M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}\}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}}E_{z}\big[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}\big]P_{\varphi^{-1}(x)}\big[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}>M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}\big]\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}}E_{z}\big[\tau_{\mathbb{D}}\big]\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{M^{M/2-1}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}

which tends to zero as
r\to 0
faster than any positive power of
r
, provided
M
is sufficiently large.

Step 3. Conclusion of the proof.

Now we will complete the proof of the lemma by bounding from above the term
f_{2}(M,r)
.

First we note that a sample from
\mathcal{D}
can be produced as follows. Let
(\mathbb{S}^{2},\widehat{h},0,\infty)
be a doubly marked quantum sphere and let
\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}
be a whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{6}
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
from
0
to
\infty
parameterized by quantum natural time with respect to
\widehat{h}
. Let
\tau
be the first time
t
that the quantum boundary length of the
\infty
-containing connected component of
\mathbb{S}^{2}\setminus\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}([0,t])
is equal to
1
and we condition on the event that
\tau<\infty
. Let
U
be that component and let
\psi:U\to\mathbb{D}
be the conformal transformation such that
\psi(\infty)=0
and
\psi^{\prime}(\infty)>0
. Then conditional on
\tau<\infty
, we set

\displaystyle h:=\widehat{h}|_{U}\circ\psi^{-1}+Q\log|(\psi^{-1})^{\prime}|\quad\text{and}\quad d_{h}(z,w)=d_{\widehat{h}|_{U}}(\psi^{-1}(z),\psi^{-1}(w))\quad\text{for all}\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

Set
\widetilde{x}:=\varphi^{-1}(x),\widetilde{y}:=\varphi^{-1}(y)
. Then we have that either
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{x}),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))\geq 2M^{-1}
or
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{x}),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))<2M^{-1}
. We will prove the claim of the lemma in each different case.

Case 1.
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{x}),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))\geq 2M^{-1}
.

Suppose that
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{x}),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))\geq 2M^{-1}
. Let also
\widehat{p}_{t}(u,v)
(resp.
\widehat{p}_{t}^{U}(u,v)
) denote the heat kernel for the LBM on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
(resp.
U
) with respect to
\widehat{h}
(resp.
\widehat{h}|_{U}
). Then Theorem
1.1
implies that there exists a deterministic constant
\kappa>0
and there a.s. exist random constants
c_{1},c_{2}
such that

(7.11)

\widehat{p}_{t}^{U}(u,v)\leq\widehat{p}_{t}(u,v)\leq\frac{c_{1}(\log t^{-1})^{\kappa}}{t}\,\exp\Biggl(-c_{2}\biggl(\frac{d_{\widehat{h}}(u,v)^{4}}{t}\biggr)^{\!\frac{1}{3}}\biggl(\log\biggl(e+\frac{d_{\widehat{h}}(u,v)}{t}\biggr)\biggr)^{\!-\kappa}\Biggr)

for all
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{2},t\in(0,1/2]
. Moreover, by
[
13
, Theorem 1.3]
,
p_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}(u,v)=\widehat{p}_{t}^{U}(\psi^{-1}(u),\psi^{-1}(v))
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{D},t>0
. It follows that for
M
sufficiently large and since
\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(A))=B_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}),r)
, we have that

\displaystyle f_{2}(M,r)\leq\int_{\varphi^{-1}(A)}\int_{0}^{M^{-M/2}/(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{M}}p_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{x},w)\,dt\,d\mu_{h}(w)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\mu_{h}(\mathbb{D})\int_{0}^{M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{-M}}\frac{\log(t^{-1})^{\kappa}}{t}\exp\left(-c_{2}((2M)^{-4}t^{-1})^{1/3}\log(e+(2Mt)^{-1})^{-\kappa}\right)dt

and the right-hand side tends to
0
as
r\to 0
faster than any positive power of
r
. Letting
f:=f_{1}+f_{2}
and
I_{M,r}:=[M^{-M/2}/(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{M},M^{M/2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{M}]
, we thus have that for
M
sufficiently large,

f(M,r)+\int_{A}\int_{I_{M,r}}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)\,dt\,d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)\geq E_{x}[T_{A}],

where
f(M,r)\to 0
as
r\to 0
faster than any positive power of
r
by combining with Step 2. Since
|I_{M,r}|
is of order
M^{M/2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{M}
, by dividing both sides by
|I_{M,r}|
and combining with (
7.9
) and (
7.10
), we see that for
r\in(0,M^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|I_{M,r}|}\int_{I_{M,r}}\int_{A}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)\,d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)\,dt\geq\frac{r^{\alpha+M/2}}{M^{M/2}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{M}}\geq r^{\alpha+3M/4}(\log\tfrac{1}{r})^{-M}\geq r^{M},

provided
M
is sufficiently large. Thus, there exists
t\in I_{M,r}
such that the claimed lower bound in the statement of the lemma holds.

Case 2.
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{x}),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))<2M^{-1}
.

Suppose now that
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{x}),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))<2M^{-1}
. Note that we have already shown in Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
7.6
that there exists a deterministic constant
\beta\in(0,\infty)
and there a.s. exist (random)
z_{0}\in\mathbb{D},c_{0},s>0
such that
B(z_{0},s)\subseteq\mathbb{D}
and the probability that a complex Brownian motion starting from
x
intersects
B(z_{0},s/4)
before exiting
\mathbb{D}
for the first time is at least
c_{0}r^{\beta}
.

Let
(X_{t}^{\mathbb{D}})
denote the Liouville Brownian motion on
\mathbb{D}
with respect to
\widetilde{h}
, killed when it first exits
\mathbb{D}
. Suppose first that
x\in B(z_{0},s/3)
and set
T:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}^{\mathbb{D}}\in\partial B(z_{0},s/2)\}
. Possibly by taking
M
to be larger, we can assume that

\displaystyle\text{dist}_{d_{\widehat{h}}}(\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(B(z_{0},s/3))),\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(B(z_{0},s/2))))\geq 100M^{-1}.

Then we have that
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(X_{T}^{\mathbb{D}})),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))\geq 2M^{-1}
. Similarly if
x\notin B(z_{0},s/3)
, we let
T
be the first time that
X^{\mathbb{D}}
intersects
B(z_{0},s/4)
before exiting
\mathbb{D}
. Then we have that
P[T<\infty]\geq c_{0}r^{\beta}
and
d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(X_{T}^{\mathbb{D}})),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))\geq 2M^{-1}
, if we take
M
sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle\text{dist}_{d_{\widehat{h}}}(\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(B(z_{0},s/4))),\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(B(z_{0},s/3))))\geq 100M^{-1}.

Thus we have that

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{h}}(\psi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(X_{T}^{\mathbb{D}})),\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{y}))\geq 2M^{-1}

in every case. Also Lemma
7.6
implies that

\displaystyle E_{X_{T}^{\mathbb{D}}}[T_{A}]\gtrsim r^{\alpha+M/4}\quad\text{a.s. on}\quad\{T<\infty\}

and so we obtain that

\displaystyle E_{x}\big[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{T<\infty\}}E_{X^{\mathbb{D}}_{T}}\big[T_{A}\big]\big]\gtrsim r^{\alpha+\beta+M/4}.

Also we have that

\displaystyle E_{x}\left[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2\leq T<\infty\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{X^{\mathbb{D}}_{t+T}\in A\}}\,dt\right]\leq\int_{A}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2}^{\infty}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)\,dt\,d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)

\displaystyle\mspace{36mu}\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}}\left(\int_{A}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2}^{\infty}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(z,w)\,dt\,d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)\right)

which implies that

\displaystyle r^{\alpha+\beta+M/4}\lesssim\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}}\left(\int_{A}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2}^{\infty}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(z,w)\,dt\,d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)\right)+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3},

where

\displaystyle I_{1}=E_{x}\left[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{T\leq M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2\}}\int_{0}^{M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{X^{\mathbb{D}}_{t+T}\in A\}}\,dt\right],

\displaystyle I_{2}=E_{x}\left[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{T\leq M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2\}}\int_{M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}^{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{X^{\mathbb{D}}_{t+T}\in A\}}\,dt\right],

\displaystyle I_{3}=E_{x}\left[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{T\leq M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2\}}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}^{\infty}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{X^{\mathbb{D}}_{t+T}\in A\}}\,dt\right].

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle I_{1}+I_{3}

\displaystyle\leq E_{x}\left[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{T<\infty\}}\int_{A}\int_{0}^{M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(X^{\mathbb{D}}_{T},w)dtd\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+E_{x}\left[\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}^{\infty}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{X^{\mathbb{D}}_{t}\in A\}}\,dt\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{-M}}\frac{\log(r^{-1})^{\kappa}}{t}\exp(-c_{2}(2Mt)^{-1/3})dt

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{A}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}}^{\infty}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)\,dt\,\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w).

Thus, by arguing as in Step 2, we obtain that
I_{1}+I_{3}\to 0
as
r\to 0
faster than any positive power of
r
. Furthermore, we have that

\displaystyle I_{2}\leq\int_{A}\int_{M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2+M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{-M}}^{3M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}/2}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)\,dt\,d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w).

Hence, by arguing as in Case 1, we get that there exists
t\in[M^{-M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{-M},M^{M/2}\log(r^{-1})^{M}]
such that
\int_{A}p_{t}^{\mathcal{N}}(x,w)d\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(w)\geq r^{M}
.
∎

We next record the following which will be used in Subsection
7.2
(see in particular the proof of Lemma
7.9
) in order to bound from below the probabilities that the Liouville Brownian motion intersects certain fixed LQG metric balls. We chose to state and prove the lemma at this point since its proof follows from the same argument used to prove Lemma
7.5
.

Lemma 7.7
.

For every
M>1
there exist constants
K_{1},K_{2}>1
, depending on
M
, so that the following is true. For
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
a.e. instance of
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
there exists
\Delta_{0}>0
so that for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta_{0})
and
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
with
d_{h}(u,v)\leq\delta
there exists
t\in[\delta^{K_{1}},\delta^{1/K_{1}}]
so that

P_{u}[X_{t}\in B_{h}(v,\delta^{M})]\geq\delta^{K_{2}},

where
X
denotes Liouville Brownian motion and
P_{u}
is the law under which
X
starts from
u
.

Proof.

This follows from the same argument used to prove Lemma
7.5
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

First we note that by scaling and as in the proof of Proposition
6.2
, it suffices to prove the claim of the proposition in the case that
\delta=1
. Moreover, combining Lemma
7.5
with the argument in the first paragraph of the proof of Lemma
6.10
in order to compare locally the laws of a quantum disk and a quantum wedge of weight
2
, we obtain that condition (
I
) occurs with as high probability as we want provided we choose
\mathcal{M}_{0}
sufficiently large. Furthermore, combining Lemma
7.3
with the arguments in Steps 3 and 4 in the proof of Lemma
6.9
and the argument in the second paragraph of the proof of Lemma
6.10
, we obtain that condition (
II
) holds with high probability as well provided we choose
\mathcal{M}_{0}
large enough. Therefore, the proof is complete by combining with Proposition
6.2
.
∎

7.2.
Definition and properties of the good annuli

The main goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 7.8
.

There exists a constant
\kappa>0
so that for
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
-a.e.
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
there exists
\Delta_{0}>0
so that for all
\delta\in(0,\Delta_{0})
the following is true. For every
z\in\mathcal{S}
there exists an annulus
\mathcal{A}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathcal{A}
is contained in
B_{h}(z,\delta^{1/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{\kappa})\setminus B_{h}(z,\delta^{1/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{-\kappa})
.

(ii)

The
d_{h}
-distance between the inner and outer boundaries of
\mathcal{A}
is at least
\delta^{1/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{-\kappa}
.

(iii)

If
u,v\in\mathcal{A}
have
d_{h}
-distance at least
\delta^{1/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{-\kappa}/2
from
\partial\mathcal{A}
, then there exists an element
s
of
[\delta^{4/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{-\kappa},\delta^{4/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{\kappa}]
so that

\int_{B_{h}(v,\delta^{1/3}(\log\delta^{-1})^{-\kappa})}p_{s}^{\mathcal{A}}(u,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-(\log\delta^{-1})^{\kappa})

where
p^{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the heat kernel for Liouville Brownian motion on
\mathcal{A}
.

(iv)

For each
u\in\mathcal{A}
there exists
s\leq\delta^{1/\kappa}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta^{1/3})}p_{s}(z,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-(\log\delta^{-1})^{\kappa})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta^{1/3})}p_{s}(a,z)\,d\mu_{h}(a)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-(\log\delta^{-1})^{\kappa}).

As explained just after the proof of Lemma
7.1
, we will use Proposition
7.8
in order to prove that the following is true for
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
-a.e.
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
. Fix
u,v\in\mathcal{S}
distinct points and
0<t\lesssim d_{h}(u,v)
. Then Proposition
7.8
implies that we can find a finite and connected chain of topological annuli
\mathcal{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{N}
connecting
u
to
v
and each of them satisfying properties (
i
)-(
iv
) and consisting of unions of good chunks in the sense of Proposition
7.2
. Moreover we have that
N\asymp\bigl(\frac{d_{h}(u,v)^{4}}{t}\bigr)^{1/3}
and
\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\asymp\bigl(\frac{t}{d_{h}(u,v)}\bigr)^{1/3}
for all
1\leq j\leq N
, and the Brownian motion can move between the annuli with uniformly positive probability. The reason for introducing properties (
i
) - (
iv
) is that they will ensure that the required bounds on the heat kernel of the Liouville Brownian motion hold and hence complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
in Subsection
7.3
.

We will focus on proving Proposition
7.8
for the rest of the section.
From now on we fix
M\geq 1
so that
p_{0}\in(0,1)
from Proposition
7.2
is large enough so that the assertion of Proposition
5.1
holds.

We start by describing the setup of the proof of Proposition
7.8
. Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
has distribution
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
, let
\kappa>0
be the constant from the statement of Lemma
6.22
and fix
u\in(0,1/3)
. Fix also
r_{0}\in(0,1)
and from now on, we assume that we are working on the event that

\displaystyle r^{4}\log(r^{-1})^{-6-u}\leq\mu_{h}(B_{h}(z,r))\leq r^{4}\log(r^{-1})^{8+u},\quad\frac{\mu_{h}(B_{h}(z,r))}{\mu_{h}(\mathcal{S})}\geq r^{4+u}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\text{diam}}(\mathcal{S})\geq 6r

for all
z\in\mathcal{S},r\in(0,r_{0})
, where
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
has law
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
. We note that Theorem
4.1
implies that we can find such
r_{0}
satisfying the above properties for
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
-a.e. instance
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large such that
2^{-k}<r_{0}
and set
N_{k}=2^{4k+u}
. Let
(z_{j})
be a sequence chosen i.i.d. from
\mu_{h}
. Then, we know from the proof of Lemma
6.22
that
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\cup_{j=1}^{N_{k}}B_{h}(z_{j},2^{-k})
off an event whose
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
measure tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
faster than any power of
2^{-k}
. We set
\delta_{k}=2^{-3k}
and for all
1\leq i,j\leq N_{k}
such that
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
, suppose that we define stopping times
\tau_{m}^{i,j}
and events
A_{1}^{i,j},\ldots,A_{m}^{i,j},B_{1}^{i,j},\ldots,B_{m}^{i,j}
as in the proof of Lemma
6.22
with
r=\delta_{k}^{1/3}
. We also define collections of chunks
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{i,m,j},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{N}^{i,m,j}
in a similar way, where
z_{i}
plays the role of
x
in the proof of Lemma
6.22
. Then, arguing in the same way as in the proof of Lemma
6.22
, we obtain that off an event whose
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
measure tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
faster than any power of
2^{-k}
, it holds that we can find an annulus
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
consisting only of good chunks such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}\subseteq B_{h}(z_{i},2\delta_{k}^{1/3})\setminus B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3}),

\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
disconnects
B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3})
from
z_{i}
, and the distance between the inner and outer boundaries of
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
with respect to the interior-internal metric in
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
is at least
\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}
. Therefore by the Borel-Cantelli lemma, we have that
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
-a.e., there exists
\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\geq\mathcal{K}_{0}
implies that
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
satisfies both (
i
) and (
ii
) from Proposition
7.8
. We now show that the
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
satisfy the other properties.

We start by proving that condition (
iii
) holds with high probability. This will follow from applying a union bound using the Borel-Cantelli lemma and the following lemma combined with the fact that the number of chunks in
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
is at most
\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c}
for some constant
c>0
by (
i
).

Lemma 7.9
.

Fix
b>0
. Then there exists a deterministic constant
c>0
, depending on
M
and
b
so that the following is true off an event whose
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
measure tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
. Suppose that we have the setup described just after the statement of Proposition
7.8
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that we are working on the event that
k\geq\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\delta}_{k}:=\delta_{k}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-b}\leq\Delta_{0}
where
\Delta_{0}
is as in Lemma
7.7
. Fix
1\leq i,j\leq N_{k}
and further suppose we are working on the event that
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2}
are two adjacent chunks in
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
for which the event
E
occurs and let
\mathcal{N}
be the quantum surface parameterized by the interior of
\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{1}\cup\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. For each
u,v\in\mathcal{N}
with distance at least
\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}/M
from
\partial\mathcal{N}
there exists
s\in[M^{-M}\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}/(\log\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{-1})^{M},M^{M}\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}(\log\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{-1})^{M}]
so that

\displaystyle P_{u}\big[X_{s}\in B_{h}(v,\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}M^{-1})\big]\geq\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{c},

where
X
denotes Liouville Brownian motion and
P_{u}
is the law under which
X
starts from
u
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{N}_{1}
,
\mathcal{N}_{2}
,
\mathcal{N}
are as in the statement of the lemma. We also let
K_{1},K_{2}
be as in the statement of Lemma
7.7
. We take
p=8K_{1}/3
so that
\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{p/K_{1}}=\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{8/3}
is of lower order than
\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}
. Suppose that
u,v\in\mathcal{N}
both have distance at least
\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}/M
from
\partial\mathcal{N}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
u\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
. By condition (
II
) in the definition of
E
for the chunk
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, there exists
u_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
such that
d_{h}(u,u_{1})\leq\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{p}
and
B_{h}(u_{1},2\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Let
u_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
have distance
\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{p}
from
\partial\mathcal{N}_{2}
with
B_{h}(u_{2},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{1}
and let
v_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}
be such that
d_{h}(u_{2},v_{2})\leq 2\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{p}
with
B_{h}(v_{2},2\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{2}
(we apply again condition (
II
) in the definition of
E
but for the chunk
\mathcal{N}_{2}
in place of
\mathcal{N}_{1}
). If
v\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
, then the claim of the lemma follows from condition (
I
) in the definition of
E
for the chunk
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. If
v\in\mathcal{N}_{2}
, then for
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}>0
and
s=s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}+s_{4}
, we have that
P_{u}[X_{s}\in B_{h}(v,\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}M^{-1})]\geq p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}p_{4}
where

\displaystyle p_{1}

\displaystyle=P_{u}[X_{s_{1}}\in B_{h}(u_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})],

\displaystyle p_{2}

\displaystyle=\inf_{w\in B_{h}(u_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})}P_{w}[X_{s_{2}}\in B_{h}(u_{2},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})],

\displaystyle p_{3}

\displaystyle=\inf_{w\in B_{h}(u_{2},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})}P_{w}[X_{s_{3}}\in B_{h}(v_{2},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})],

\displaystyle p_{4}

\displaystyle=\inf_{w\in B_{h}(v_{2},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM})}P_{w}[X_{s_{4}}\in B_{h}(v,\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}/M)].

By Lemma
7.7
, there exists a choice of
s_{1},s_{3}\in[\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pK_{1}},\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{p/K_{1}}]
so that
p_{1},p_{3}\geq\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pK_{2}}
. Furthermore, by condition (
I
) in the definition of the event
E
for both chunks
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
, applied with
r=\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM}<\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}/M
, we can find
s_{2},s_{4}\in[M^{-M}\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}/(\log\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{-1})^{M},M^{M}\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}(\log\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{-1})^{M}]
so that
p_{2},p_{4}\geq\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{pM^{2}}
. Altogether, this implies that there exists
s\in[\tfrac{1}{2}M^{-M}\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}/(\log\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{-1})^{M},2M^{M}\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{4/3}(\log\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{-1})^{M}]
and a constant
c>0
depending on
M
and
b
so that
P_{u}[X_{s}\in B_{h}(v,\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{1/3}M^{-1})]\geq\widetilde{\delta}_{k}^{c}
.
∎

Next we focus on proving that condition (
iv
) holds with high probability. The main ingredient of the proof of the claim is the following lemma which states that with probability tending to
1
as
k\to\infty
, we have that condition (
iv
) holds for all
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
with
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
and
B_{h,z_{j}}^{\bullet}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa})\subseteq B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{0}})
for some fixed and deterministic constant
\kappa_{0}\in(0,\infty)
.

Lemma 7.10
.

There exists a constant
\widetilde{c}>0
such that the following is true. Suppose that we have the setup described just after the statement of Proposition
7.8
. Then, off an event whose
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
measure tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
, we have that the following holds. Fix
1\leq i,j\leq N_{k}
and further suppose that we are working on the event that
k\in\mathcal{K}_{0},d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
and
B_{h,z_{j}}^{\bullet}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa})\subseteq B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{0}})
for some fixed and deterministic constant
\kappa_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. Then, for all
z\in B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})
, there exists
s\in\bigl(0,\delta_{k}^{4/\widetilde{c}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\widetilde{c}}\bigr]
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(z,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\widetilde{c}})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(a,z)\,d\mu_{h}(a)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\widetilde{c}})

for all
u\in\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
.

Proof.

Since
k\in\mathcal{K}_{0}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\subseteq B_{h,z_{j}}^{\bullet}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa})\subseteq B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{0}})\subseteq B_{h}(z,\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{1}}),

where
0<\kappa_{1}<\kappa_{0}
is fixed and deterministic. Let
X
be the LBM and let
\tau=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in\mathcal{A}\}
. Then, under
P_{z}
,
\tau
is clearly bounded from above by the first exit time from
B_{h}(z,\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{1}})
so that Theorem
6.1
implies that
E_{w}\big[\tau\big]\leq\delta_{k}^{4/\kappa_{1}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa_{2}}
for all
w\in B_{h}(z,\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{1}})
, for some fixed and deterministic constant
\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
. In particular, if
c_{1}>\kappa_{2}+1
, we have that
P_{z}\big[\tau\geq\delta_{k}^{4/\kappa_{1}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{1}}\big]\to 0
as
k\to\infty
faster than any power of
2^{-k}
. Indeed, Markov’s inequality implies that if
\kappa_{3}\in(\kappa_{2},c_{1}-1)
is fixed and deterministic, then

\displaystyle P_{z}\big[\tau\geq\delta_{k}^{4/\kappa_{1}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa_{3}}\big]\leq\delta_{k}^{-4/\kappa_{1}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa_{3}}E_{z}\big[\tau\big]\leq\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{3}}.

Hence, the Markov property of
X
implies that

\displaystyle P_{z}\big[\tau\geq\delta_{k}^{4/\kappa_{1}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{1}}\big]\leq\exp(-(\kappa_{3}-\kappa_{2})\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{1}-\kappa_{3}+1}),

which proves the claim. Once
X
hits
\mathcal{A}
, we know from Lemma
7.9
by taking
c_{1}
to be larger if necessary and depending only on
\kappa
and
M
, that
X
can move from one chunk in
\mathcal{A}
to an adjacent chunk in
\mathcal{A}
in time between a constant depending only on
M
times
\delta_{k}^{4/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-c_{1}}
and a constant depending only on
M
times
\delta_{k}^{4/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{1}}
with probability at least
\delta_{k}^{c_{1}}
. Note that the number of chunks which make up
\mathcal{A}
is at most
\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa(2/3-u)}
which is at most
\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{1}}
by taking
c_{1}
to be larger if necessary, where
u\in(0,1/3)
is as in the statement of Proposition
5.1
. Hence, we can find
c_{2}>c_{1}
fixed and deterministic, depending only on
c_{1}
and
c
(where
c
is the constant in the statement of Lemma
7.9
), such that
X
hits
B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}/M)
in time at most
(\delta_{k}^{4/\kappa_{1}}+\delta_{k}^{4/3})\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}}
with probability at least
\exp(-\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}})
. Suppose that
w\in B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}/M)
and
t\in[\delta_{k}^{1/c_{2}},1/2]
. Then, by taking
c_{2}
to be larger if necessary, we can assume using Lemma
7.1
that there exists a random constant
C\geq 1
such that
p_{t}(w,w)\geq\frac{1}{Ct\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}}}
.

Suppose that
t^{\prime}=\delta_{k}^{4/3}\log(t^{-1})^{-2c_{2}}
. Note that

\displaystyle p_{t}(w,w)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}p_{t-t^{\prime}}(w,a)p_{t^{\prime}}(a,w)d\mu_{h}(a).

Then, Theorem
1.1
implies that by taking
c_{2}
to be larger if necessary, there exist random constants
A_{1},A_{2}>0
such that for all
a\in\mathcal{S}\setminus B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})
, we have that

\displaystyle p_{t^{\prime}}(a,w)\leq\frac{A_{1}\log((t^{\prime})^{-1})^{c_{2}}}{t^{\prime}}\exp\Biggl(-A_{2}\biggl(\frac{d_{h}(a,w)^{4}}{t^{\prime}}\biggr)^{1/3}\Biggr)

and so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}\setminus B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{t-t^{\prime}}(w,a)p_{t^{\prime}}(a,w)d\mu_{h}(a)\to 0

as
k\to\infty
, faster than any power of
2^{-k}
. Therefore, by increasing
C
if necessary, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{t-t^{\prime}}(w,a)p_{t^{\prime}}(w,a)d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\frac{1}{Ct\log(t^{-1})^{c_{2}}}.

Applying again Theorem
1.1
to
p_{t^{\prime}}(w,a)
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{C\log((t^{\prime})^{-1})^{c_{2}}}{t^{\prime}}\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{t-t^{\prime}}(w,a)d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\frac{1}{Ct\log(t^{-1})^{c_{2}}}.

After possibly increasing
C
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{t-t^{\prime}}(w,a)d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\frac{t^{\prime}}{Ct\log(t^{-1})^{c_{2}}}.

Note that for all
t\in[\delta_{k}^{4/c_{2}},2\delta_{k}^{4/c_{2}}]
, we have
t^{\prime}\gtrsim\delta_{k}^{4/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-2c_{2}}
and
t\log(t^{-1})^{2c_{2}}\lesssim\delta_{k}^{4/c_{2}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}}
with the implicit constants depending only on
c_{2}
, and so

\displaystyle I:=\int_{\delta_{k}^{4/c_{2}}}^{2\delta_{k}^{4/c_{2}}}\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{t-t^{\prime}}(w,a)d\mu_{h}(a)\gtrsim\exp(-\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}})

by taking
c_{2}
to be larger if necessary. It follows that the expected amount of time that the LBM
X
starting from
z
spends in
B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})
in the time interval
[0,\delta_{k}^{4/c_{2}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}}]
is at least
\exp(-2\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}})
. Therefore, taking
c_{3}>c_{2}
depending only on
c_{2}
and dividing by
\delta^{4/c_{2}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{2}}
, we obtain that there exists
s\in\bigl(0,\delta_{k}^{4/c_{3}}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{3}}\bigr]
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(z,a)d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c_{3}}).

This proves the first inequality of the lemma. The second inequality follows from the symmetry of the heat kernel and the first inequality.
∎

Recall that in order to be able to apply Lemma
7.10
, we need to have with high probability as
k\to\infty
that

\displaystyle B_{h,z_{j}}^{\bullet}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa})\subseteq B_{h}(z_{i},\delta_{k}^{1/\kappa_{0}})

whenever
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
for some fixed and deterministic constant
\kappa_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. This will be a consequence of the following lemma together with the Hölder continuity of
d_{h}
with respect to the Euclidean metric.

Lemma 7.11
.

There exists a deterministic constant
p>0
so that for
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
-a.e. instance of
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. For every set
S\subseteq\mathcal{S}
with
d_{h}
-diameter at most
\epsilon^{p}
there exists at most one connected component of
\mathcal{S}\setminus S
which contains a
d_{h}
-ball of radius
\epsilon
.

Proof.

We can assume that
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
is parameterized by
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Recall that the metric
d_{h}
is Hölder continuous with respect to the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. This implies that there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
deterministic and
C\geq 1
so that for all
u,v\in\mathcal{S}
we have that

(7.12)

C^{-1}d(u,v)^{1/\alpha}\leq d_{h}(u,v)\leq Cd(u,v)^{\alpha},

where
d
denotes the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Fix
\epsilon,p>0
and suppose that
S\subseteq\mathcal{S}
has
d_{h}
-diameter at most
\epsilon^{p}
. Then (
7.12
) implies that
S
has Euclidean diameter at most
C\epsilon^{\alpha p}
. All components of
\mathbb{S}^{2}\setminus S
except one have Euclidean diameter at most
C\epsilon^{\alpha p}
. Therefore by (
7.12
) all components of
\mathbb{S}^{2}\setminus S
except one have
d_{h}
-diameter at most
C^{1+\alpha}\epsilon^{\alpha^{2}p}
. The result thus follows by assuming that
p
is sufficiently large so that
\alpha^{2}p>1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.8
.

Fix
\delta>0
and suppose that
(z_{j})
is an i.i.d. sequence of points in
\mathcal{S}
chosen independently from
\mu_{h}
. We explained after the statement of Proposition
7.8
why for
k\geq\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
we have that
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
satisfies properties (
i
) and (
ii
). Lemma
7.9
and the fact that the number of chunks in
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
is at most
\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{c}
for some constant
c>0
by (
i
) together with another union bound and the Borel-Cantelli lemma implies that, after possibly increasing the value of
\mathcal{K}_{0}
, we have that (
iii
) also holds for each such
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
by possibly increasing
\kappa
. Lemma
7.11
together with the Hölder continuity of
d_{h}
with respect to the Euclidean metric implies that by possibly increasing the values of
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\kappa
, we have that if
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
then
B_{h,z_{j}}^{\bullet}(z_{i},2^{-k}k^{\kappa})\subseteq B_{h}(z_{i},2^{-k/\kappa})
. It thus follows from Lemma
7.10
that by possibly increasing
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\kappa
further we have that (
iv
) holds for each
\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
with
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
.

By possibly increasing
\mathcal{K}_{0}
further, for every
1\leq i\leq N_{k}
there exists
1\leq j\leq N_{k}
so that
d_{h}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq 2^{-k/2}
. For
z\in B_{h}(z_{i},2^{-k})
we take
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{i,j,k}
(breaking ties in an arbitrary way). This choice of
\mathcal{A}
then satisfies properties (
i
), (
ii
), (
iii
), and (
iv
).
∎

7.3.
Completion of the proof

We now turn to complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{S},h,x,y)
has distribution
\mu_{\mathrm{QSPH}}
. Fix
u,v\in\mathcal{S}
and let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
2^{k}\leq d_{h}(u,v)<2^{k+1}
. Fix
t\in(0,\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{0}d_{h}(u,v))
where
\Delta_{0}>0
is as in Proposition
7.8
. For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
\delta_{j}=t2^{-j}
, in particular
\delta_{k}\leq\Delta_{0}
. Let
\gamma
be a geodesic from
u
to
v
. For each
j\geq k
we consider the following chain of annuli. We let
z_{0,j}=u=\gamma(0)
and we let
\mathcal{A}_{0,j}
be an annulus which is centered at
z_{0,j}
and satisfies the properties from Proposition
7.8
with parameter
\delta_{j}
and let
\kappa>0
be the constant from the statement of Proposition
7.8
. Given that we have defined
z_{0,j},\ldots,z_{n,j}
we let
z_{n+1,j}
be a point on the interval of
\gamma
from
z_{n,j}
to
v
which is in
\mathcal{A}_{n,j}
and has
d_{h}
-distance at least
\delta_{j}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{-\kappa}
from
\partial\mathcal{A}_{n,j}
. Note that
z_{n+1,j}
is well-defined since by possibly increasing
\kappa
, we can assume that the
d_{h}
-distance between the inner and outer boundaries of
\mathcal{A}_{n,j}
is at least
2\delta_{j}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{j}^{-1})^{-\kappa}
, by condition (
ii
) in the statement of Proposition
7.8
. We then let
\mathcal{A}_{n+1,j}
be an annulus which satisfies the properties from Proposition
7.8
centered at the point
z_{n+1,j}
with parameter
\delta_{j}
. We continue this until we find the point
z_{N_{j},j}
so that
d_{h}(z_{N_{j},j},v)\leq\delta_{j}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{-\kappa}
. Since we have that
\delta_{j}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{-\kappa}\leq d_{h}(z_{i,j},z_{i+1,j})\leq\delta_{j}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{\kappa}
, it follows that
d_{h}(u,v)/(\delta_{j}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{\kappa})\leq N_{j}\leq d_{h}(u,v)/(\delta_{j}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{-\kappa})
.

We take
j_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
to be the smallest
j\in\mathbb{Z}
so that
\delta_{j}^{1/\kappa}\leq\delta_{k}^{4/3}
. We note that
\log\delta_{j}^{-1}
is comparable to
\log\delta_{k}^{-1}
for
k\leq j\leq j_{0}
. Therefore we will phrase estimates from Proposition
7.8
in terms of
\log\delta_{k}^{-1}
. Suppose that we have picked
w_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{0,j}
for each
k\leq j\leq j_{0}
. By Proposition
7.8
-(
iv
) we know that there exists
s_{j_{0}}\leq\delta_{j_{0}}^{1/\kappa}\leq\delta_{k}^{4/3}
so that

(7.13)

\int_{B_{h}(w_{j_{0}},\delta_{j_{0}}^{1/3})}p_{s_{j_{0}}}(u,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}).

For each
k+1\leq j\leq j_{0}
, we let
m_{j}
be the first index
i
so that
\mathcal{A}_{i,j}\cap\mathcal{A}_{0,j-1}\neq\emptyset
. Then we have that
m_{j}\leq(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}
. By iterating Proposition
7.8
-(
iii
)
m_{j}
times, we see that by increasing the value of
\kappa
if necessary, there exists
\delta_{j}^{4/3}\log(\delta_{j}^{-1})^{-\kappa}\leq s\leq\delta_{j}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{j}^{-1})^{\kappa}
so that

(7.14)

\int_{B_{h}(w_{j},\delta_{j}^{1/3})}p_{s}(w_{j+1},a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{2\kappa}).

By applying the semigroup property and iterating (
7.14
) over
j_{0}\leq j\leq k
and combining with (
7.13
), we thus see that by possibly taking
\kappa
to be larger, there exists
\delta_{k}^{4/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}\leq s\leq\delta_{k}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}
so that

(7.15)

\int_{B_{h}(w_{k},\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(u,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-(k-j_{0})(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{2\kappa}).

For each
i
, we let
v_{i,k}
be a point in
\mathcal{A}_{i,k}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i+1,k}
whose
d_{h}
-distance from both
\partial\mathcal{A}_{i,k}
and
\partial\mathcal{A}_{i+1,k}
is at least
\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}/2
. We let
v_{0,k}=w_{k}
. It follows from Proposition
7.8
-(
iii
) that there exists
\delta_{k}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}\leq s\leq\delta_{k}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}
so that

(7.16)

\int_{B_{h}(v_{i,k},\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(v_{i-1,k},a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}).

By applying the semigroup property and iterating (
7.16
) over
1\leq i\leq N_{k}
, we see that there exists
N_{k}\delta_{k}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}\leq s\leq N_{k}\delta_{k}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}
so that

(7.17)

\int_{B_{h}(v_{N_{k},k},\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(v_{0,k},a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-N_{k}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}).

Arguing in the same way as (
7.15
), we have that there exists
\delta_{k}^{4/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}\leq s\leq\delta_{k}^{4/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}
so that

(7.18)

\int_{B_{h}(v_{N_{k},k},\delta_{k}^{1/3})}p_{s}(a,v)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\exp(-(k-j_{0})(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{2\kappa}).

Combining (
7.15
), (
7.17
) and (
7.18
), increasing the value of
\kappa
if necessary, and applying the semigroup property we see that there exists

t_{0}\in[t(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa},t(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}]

so that

(7.19)

p_{t_{0}}(u,v)\geq\exp(-N_{k}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{\kappa}).

Therefore, the claim of Theorem
1.2
holds with
t_{0}
in place of
t
. We will now complete the proof by establishing the result for
t
in place of
t_{0}
. If we apply the argument described above but with
t(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa}/2
in place of
t
, then (
7.19
) implies that there exists
t_{1}\in[t(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-2\kappa}/2,t/2]
so that
p_{t_{1}}(u,v)
satisfies the desired lower bound. We have that

p_{t}(u,v)\geq\int_{B_{h}(v,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})}p_{t-t_{1}}(u,a)\,p_{t_{1}}(a,v)\,d\mu_{h}(a).

By arguing as above, we have that for all
a\in B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}\log(\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})
,
p_{t_{1}}(a,u)
satisfies the desired lower bound. Therefore we just need to get a lower bound on

\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})}p_{t-t_{1}}(u,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)=P_{u}[X_{t-t_{1}}\in B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})].

Take
t_{2}=t(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-2\kappa}
. From Lemma
7.1
, we have that

\int_{\mathcal{S}}p_{t-t_{1}}(u,a)\,p_{t_{2}}(a,u)\,d\mu_{h}(a)=p_{t-t_{1}+t_{2}}(u,u)\geq\frac{1}{Ct(\log t^{-1})^{\kappa}}

for some random constant
C>0
. The upper heat kernel estimate in Theorem
1.1
implies that
p_{t_{2}}(a,u)
is negligible if
a\notin B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})
. Thus by possibly adjusting the value of
C
, we have that

\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})}p_{t-t_{1}}(u,a)\,p_{t_{2}}(a,u)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\frac{1}{Ct(\log t^{-1})^{\kappa}}.

Applying Theorem
1.1
to
p_{t_{2}}(a,u)
yields that

\frac{C(\log t_{2}^{-1})^{\kappa}}{t_{2}}\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})}p_{t-t_{1}}(u,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\frac{1}{Ct(\log t^{-1})^{\kappa}}.

Rearranging and increasing the value of
C
gives

\int_{B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})}p_{t-t_{1}}(u,a)\,d\mu_{h}(a)\geq\frac{t_{2}}{Ct(\log t^{-1})^{2\kappa}}.

Combining this with the above implies that
P_{u}[X_{t-t_{1}}\in B_{h}(u,\delta_{k}^{1/3}(\log\delta_{k}^{-1})^{-\kappa})]
satisfies the desired lower bound.
∎

Appendix A
{\rm SLE}_{6}
hulls at the times when the tip is on the boundary are Jordan domains

In Sections
5
,
6
and
7
,
we make use of the hulls of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
to construct nice cellular decompositions of quantum disks
and wedges. An essential feature of the hulls of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
, on which our arguments heavily rely,
is that
they are Jordan domains at the times when the tip of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
is located on the boundary
.
For completeness, here we give a detailed proof of this fact on the basis of some
fundamental results from the theory of imaginary geometry developed in
[
70
,
72
]
.

We first give the precise formulation of this property in the case of radial
{\rm SLE}_{6}
,
since our arguments in Sections
5
,
6

and
7
require it mainly for this case. In the same way as in
Subsection
3.2.4
, for a radial
{\rm SLE}_{6}

\eta^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{D}
targeted at
0

and
t\in[0,\inf(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}(0))
, we define the
hull

K_{t}
of
\eta^{\prime}([0,t])

as the complement in
\mathbb{D}
of the
0
-containing component of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
x\in\partial\mathbb{D}
and let
\eta^{\prime}
be a radial
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{D}
from
x

targeted at
0
. Then a.s., for any
t\in(0,\inf(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}(0))
with

\partial K_{t}\cap\partial\mathbb{D}\not=\partial\mathbb{D}
and
\eta^{\prime}(t)\in\partial\mathbb{D}
,

(A.1)

K_{t}\setminus\partial K_{t}
is a Jordan domain in
\mathbb{C}
with boundary

\partial K_{t}
, and
\partial K_{t}\cap\partial\mathbb{D}
is a compact interval in

\partial\mathbb{D}
containing
\eta^{\prime}(0)=x
in its interior.

Proposition
A.1
can be obtained by combining with a version of the
locality of
{\rm SLE}_{6}
the analogous statement for chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
stated as follows.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}
be a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
\infty
.
Then a.s., for any
t\in(0,\infty)
with
\eta^{\prime}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
,

(A.2)

K_{t}\setminus\partial K_{t}
is a Jordan domain in
\mathbb{C}
with boundary

\partial K_{t}
, and
\partial K_{t}\cap\mathbb{R}
is a compact interval in

\mathbb{R}
containing
0
in its interior.

Note that each
t\in(0,\infty)
with
\eta^{\prime}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
and the property
(
A.2
) also satisfies

(A.3)

\eta^{\prime}(t)\in\{\max(\partial K_{t}\cap\mathbb{R}),\min(\partial K_{t}\cap\mathbb{R})\},

since for any
t,s\in[0,\infty)
with
t<s
we have
K_{t}\not=K_{s}
and hence

\eta^{\prime}((t,s])\cap\mathbb{H}\not\subset K_{t}
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, Sections 7–9]
).

Proof.

Step 1.
We first recall some basic properties of the chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}

\eta^{\prime}
.
Set
T_{a}:=\inf\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid\eta^{\prime}(t)\in[a,\infty)\}
and

T_{-a}:=\inf\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid\eta^{\prime}(t)\in(-\infty,-a]\}
for
a\in(0,\infty)

and
T_{0}:=0
, so that
T_{a}=\inf\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid a\in\partial K_{t}\}

for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
since
\partial K_{t}\cap\mathbb{R}
is non-empty and connected
for any
t\in(0,\infty)
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, Sections 7–9]
). Note that

\lim_{b\downarrow 0}T_{-b}<T_{a}\leq T_{a}\vee T_{-a}<\infty
a.s. for each
a\in(0,\infty)

(see, e.g.,
[
60
, Propositions 6.33 and 6.34]
or
[
16
, Proposition 10.3-(b)]
),
which together with the scale invariance of
\eta^{\prime}
under the parameterization by half-plane capacity
(see, e.g.,
[
60
, Proposition 6.5]
or
[
16
, Proposition 9.3]
)
easily implies that
\lim_{b\downarrow 0}T_{b}\vee T_{-b}=0
a.s. In particular, a.s.,

(A.4)

\eta^{\prime}([0,\infty))\cap(-\infty,0)
and
\eta^{\prime}([0,\infty))\cap(0,\infty)

are unbounded and their boundaries in
\mathbb{C}
contain
0
.

We also easily see, for
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
from

[
60
, Proposition 6.34]
or
[
16
, Proposition 10.3-(b)]
,
and for
a=0
from
\lim_{t\to\infty}|\eta^{\prime}(t)|=\infty
a.s. (see, e.g.,

[
60
, Proposition 6.10]
or
[
16
, Proposition 11.7]
)
and the above-mentioned scale invariance of
\eta^{\prime}
, that

(A.5)

a\not\in\eta^{\prime}((0,\infty))
a.s. for each
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

It is also known by
[
79
, Remark 5.3]
that a.s.

(A.6)

no
(s,t,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
0\leq s<t<u
satisfies
\eta^{\prime}(s)=\eta^{\prime}(t)=\eta^{\prime}(u)
.

Step 2.

Let
\eta^{\prime}_{L}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{R}
denote the
left
and
right boundaries
of
\eta^{\prime}([0,\infty))
,
respectively, i.e.,
\eta^{\prime}_{L}:=\partial U_{L}\setminus i(-\infty,0)
and

\eta^{\prime}_{R}:=\partial U_{R}\setminus i(-\infty,0)
, where

i(-\infty,0):=\{ia\mid a\in(-\infty,0)\}
and
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
denote
the components of
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\eta^{\prime}([0,\infty))\cup i(-\infty,0))

containing
-1-i
and
1-i
, respectively. We claim that a.s.
\eta^{\prime}_{L}
and

\eta^{\prime}_{R}
are simple curves starting from
0
and tending to
\infty

and satisfy
\eta^{\prime}_{L}\cap(0,\infty)=\emptyset=\eta^{\prime}_{R}\cap(-\infty,0)
.
Indeed, let
\psi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
denote the conformal map given by
\psi(z):=-1/z
,
set
\eta_{R}:=(\psi(\eta^{\prime}_{L})\cup\{0\})\setminus\{\infty\}
and

\eta_{L}:=(\psi(\eta^{\prime}_{R})\cup\{0\})\setminus\{\infty\}
.
By
[
70
, Theorem 1.4]
(see also
[
79
, Fig. 5]
),

\eta_{R}
(resp.
\eta_{L}
) is a (chordal)
{\rm SLE}_{8/3}((8/3)/2-2;8/3-4)

(resp.
{\rm SLE}_{8/3}(8/3-4;(8/3)/2-2)
) curve (on
\mathbb{H}
) from
0
to
\infty
with force points

0^{+}
and
0^{-}
, a variant of
{\rm SLE}_{8/3}
introduced in
[
70
, Section 2]
,
then
\eta_{R}\cap(-\infty,0)=\emptyset=\eta_{L}\cap(0,\infty)
a.s. by
[
70
, Remark 5.3]

and hence
\eta^{\prime}_{L}\cap(0,\infty)=\emptyset=\eta^{\prime}_{R}\cap(-\infty,0)
a.s.
Moreover, a.s.
\eta^{\prime}_{L}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{R}
are simple curves starting from
0
and tending to
\infty
,
because
\eta_{R}
and
\eta_{L}
are continuous curves starting from
0
and tending to
\infty

a.s. by
[
70
, Proposition 7.3]
and are simple curves a.s. by the fact that
an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho^{L};\rho^{R})
curve
\eta
with force points
0^{+}
and
0^{-}

is a simple curve a.s. for any
\kappa\in(0,4)
and any
\rho^{L},\rho^{R}\in(-2,\infty)
.

This last fact can be verified as follows.
[
70
, Lemma 7.1]
1
1

1

We remark that “let
\eta_{\psi}
be the flow line of
h_{\psi}
starting from

0
and targeted at
\infty
” in
[
70
, p. 668, lines 18–19]
should read
“let
\eta_{\psi}
denote the time reparameterization of
\psi\circ\eta_{C}
by half-plane capacity”.

implies that the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho^{L};\rho^{R})
curve
\eta
can be realized as a certain
conditional law of a conformal image of a segment of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\widehat{\rho}^{L};\widehat{\rho}^{R})
curve

\widehat{\eta}
from
0
to
\infty
with force points
0^{+}
and
0^{-}
for a suitable choice of

\widehat{\rho}^{L},\widehat{\rho}^{R}\in[\kappa/2-2,\infty)
.
Then since a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
\infty

is a simple curve a.s. (see, e.g.,
[
60
, Propositions 6.9 and 6.12]
or

[
16
, Propositions 11.3 and 11.5]
) and has law mutually absolutely continuous
with respect to that of
\widehat{\eta}
on any compact time interval in
(0,\infty)

under the parameterization by half-plane capacity by

\widehat{\rho}^{L},\widehat{\rho}^{R}\in[\kappa/2-2,\infty)
and
[
70
, Remark 2.3]
,
it follows that
\widehat{\eta}
and thereby
\eta
are simple curves a.s.

Step 3.
Next, we verify that for each
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
we a.s. have
(
A.2
) with
t=T_{a}
. Indeed, let
g
be the Möbius transformation
given by
g(z):=az/(z+a)
, which maps
\mathbb{H},0,\infty,-a
onto
\mathbb{H},0,a,\infty
, respectively. Then

g\circ\eta^{\prime}
is (a time reparameterization of) a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
a

by the conformal invariance of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, Proposition 9.3]
),
and the reparameterization of
g\circ\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,T_{-a}]}
by its half-plane capacity
has the same law as
\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,T_{a}]}
by the locality of
{\rm SLE}_{6}

(see, e.g.,
[
60
, Proposition 6.14]
or
[
16
, Theorem 13.2]
).
In particular,
g^{-1}(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{T_{a}})
has the same law as the component

U_{-a}
of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,T_{-a}])
whose boundary contains
-a
,
but
U_{-a}
coincides with the component of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([0,\infty))

whose boundary contains
-a
by
\eta^{\prime}((T_{-a},\infty))\cap K_{T_{-a}}\subset\partial K_{T_{-a}}
.
Then by (
A.4
), (
A.5
) and
Step 2, a.s.
U_{-a}
is a Jordan domain whose boundary
\partial U_{-a}

is of the form
\gamma((0,1))\cup[\alpha,\beta]
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}

with
\alpha<-a<\beta
and
0\not\in[\alpha,\beta]
and some simple curve

\gamma:(0,1)\to\mathbb{H}
with
\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\gamma(s)=\alpha
and
\lim_{s\uparrow 1}\gamma(s)=\beta
.
Thus a.s.
K_{T_{a}}\setminus\partial K_{T_{a}}
is a Jordan domain with boundary
\partial K_{T_{a}}
,
which is of the form
g\bigl(\gamma((0,1))\cup(\mathbb{R}\setminus[\alpha,\beta])\bigr)\cup\{a\}

for some such
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, proving (
A.2
) with
t=T_{a}
.

Step 4.

By using the reversibility of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
proved in
[
72
, Theorem 1.1]
,
which states that
\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}:=\iota\circ\eta^{\prime}((\cdot)^{-1})

(
\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}(0):=\infty
and
\iota(z):=-\overline{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
)
is (a time reparameterization of) a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
\infty
to
0
, the results
of Step 2 can be (partially) extended to
\eta^{\prime}([0,s))
for each
s\in(0,\infty)
as follows.
Let
K_{s,\infty}
denote the complement in
\mathbb{H}
of the component of

\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}([s,\infty))=\iota(\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}([0,s^{-1}]))

whose boundary in
\mathbb{C}
contains
0
.
Noting that
-1/\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}
is (a time reparameterization of) a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}

on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
\infty
by the reversibility and the conformal invariance of chordal

{\rm SLE}_{6}
and applying to
-1/\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}
the fact that
\lim_{b\downarrow 0}T_{b}\vee T_{-b}=0

a.s. mentioned in Step 1 and the result of Step 3 for each
a\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}
,
we easily see that, a.s.,

(A.7)

\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s,\infty}
is a bounded simply connected domain in
\mathbb{C}
.

Let
g_{s}:\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s,\infty}\to\mathbb{H}
be the unique conformal map such that

\lim_{|z|\to\infty}(g_{s}(-1/z)-z)=0

(see, e.g.,
[
60
, Proposition 3.36]
or
[
16
, Theorem 4.3]
).
Then the domain Markov property of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, Proposition 9.4]
)
applied to
\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}
at time
s^{-1}
implies that the conditional law of

(\iota\circ g_{s}\circ\iota)\circ\eta^{\prime}_{\leftarrow}|_{[s^{-1},\infty)}=\iota\circ g_{s}\circ\eta^{\prime}((\cdot)^{-1})|_{[s^{-1},\infty)}

given
\eta^{\prime}|_{[s,\infty)}
is (a time reparameterization of)
a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
\iota\circ g_{s}(\eta^{\prime}(s))
to
\infty
.
Applying the reversibility
[
72
, Theorem 1.1]
of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
to

\iota\circ g_{s}\circ\eta^{\prime}((\cdot)^{-1})|_{[s^{-1},\infty)}
and then using the
domain Markov property of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
, we further see that
the conditional law of
\varphi_{s}\circ\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,s)}
given
\eta^{\prime}|_{[s,\infty)}

is (a time reparameterization of) a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
\infty
,
where
\varphi_{s}:\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s,\infty}\to\mathbb{H}
is the conformal map defined by

\varphi_{s}(z):=(g_{s}(\eta^{\prime}(s))-g_{s}(z))^{-1}
. It thus follows from Step 2 that
for each
s\in(0,\infty)
, a.s.

(A.8)

the left and right boundaries
\eta^{\prime s}_{L},\eta^{\prime s}_{R}
of
\varphi_{s}(\eta^{\prime}([0,s)))

are simple curves starting from
0
and tending to
\infty
and satisfy

\eta^{\prime s}_{L}\cap(0,\infty)=\emptyset=\eta^{\prime s}_{R}\cap(-\infty,0)
.

Recall (see, e.g.,
[
91
, Theorems II.(4.1) and VI.(2.2)]
and
[
81
, Theorem 2.1]
)
that
\varphi_{s}^{-1}
has a continuous extension to
\overline{\mathbb{H}}\cup\{\infty\}
,
so that
\varphi_{s}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\})=\partial(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s,\infty})
.
Note that, while
(\varphi_{s}^{-1})^{-1}(z)
is a singleton for any

z\in\partial(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s,\infty})\setminus\partial K_{s,\infty}
by an application
of Carathéodory’s theorem (see, e.g.,
[
81
, Theorem 2.6 and Proposition 2.14]
),

(\varphi_{s}^{-1})^{-1}(z)
may have two or more elements for

z\in\partial(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s,\infty})\cap\partial K_{s,\infty}
.

Step 5.
From (
A.5
), Step 3, Step 4 and the domain Markov
property of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
, we choose as follows an event for
\eta^{\prime}
of probability
1

on which the assertion of the proposition will be verified in Steps 6–8 below.
For each
s,t\in[0,\infty)
with
s\leq t
, let
K_{s,t}

denote the hull of
\eta^{\prime}([s,t])
in
\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s}
, i.e.,
the complement in
\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s}
of the unbounded component of

(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})\setminus\eta^{\prime}([s,t])
, so that
K_{s,t}=K_{t}\setminus K_{s}
,
and set
\partial^{\mathrm{top}}K_{s,t}:=\partial K_{s,t}\cap(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})
and

\partial^{\mathrm{bot}}K_{s,t}:=\partial K_{s,t}\cap\partial(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})
.
Then for each
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with either
0\leq a<b
or
b<a\leq 0
, it follows from Step 3
and the domain Markov property
[
16
, Proposition 9.4]
of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}

that, a.s. on
\{T_{a}<T_{b}\}
, (
\partial(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{T_{a}})
is a simple curve,)

(A.9)

K_{T_{a},T_{b}}\setminus\partial K_{T_{a},T_{b}}
is a Jordan domain in
\mathbb{C}

with boundary
\partial K_{T_{a},T_{b}}
, and

\partial^{\mathrm{bot}}K_{T_{a},T_{b}}
is a compact interval in

\partial(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{T_{a}})
containing
\eta^{\prime}(T_{a})
in its interior

(note that (
A.9
) with
a=0
is the same as (
A.2
) with
t=T_{b}
).
Also for each
b\in\mathbb{R}
and each
s\in(0,\infty)
, we easily see from
the domain Markov property of chordal
{\rm SLE}_{6}
, (
A.5
)
and (
A.9
) with
a=0
that, a.s.,

(A.10)

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(T_{b})\not\in\eta^{\prime}((T_{b},\infty)),

(A.11)

\displaystyle\max(\partial K_{s}\cap\mathbb{R})^{+},\min(\partial K_{s}\cap\mathbb{R})^{-}\not\in\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{s}((s,\infty));

here
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{s}
denotes the lift of
\eta^{\prime}|_{[s,\infty)}
to

(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})\cup\widetilde{\partial}(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})
, with

\widetilde{\partial}(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})
representing the set of prime ends of
\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s}
,
and
\max(\partial K_{s}\cap\mathbb{R})^{+}
and
\min(\partial K_{s}\cap\mathbb{R})^{-}

denote the two elements of the boundary of
(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\})\setminus\partial K_{s}

in
\widetilde{\partial}(\mathbb{H}\setminus K_{s})
corresponding to

\max(\partial K_{s}\cap\mathbb{R})
and
\min(\partial K_{s}\cap\mathbb{R})
,
respectively.

Recalling Step 4, we can thus choose an event
\Omega_{0}
for
\eta^{\prime}
of probability
1

such that every instance of
\eta^{\prime}
from
\Omega_{0}
satisfies (
A.4
),

\lim_{t\to\infty}|\eta^{\prime}(t)|=\infty
, (
A.6
), (
A.7
),
(
A.8
) and (
A.11
) for any
s\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
,
(
A.10
) for any
b\in\mathbb{Q}
, and either
T_{a}=T_{b}
or
(
A.9
) for any
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
with either
0\leq a<b
or
b<a\leq 0
.

Step 6.
Now we can proceed to the conclusion of the proof as follows.
Fix any instance of
\eta^{\prime}
from
\Omega_{0}
, and let
t\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
\eta^{\prime}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
t=T_{b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}
, then (
A.2
)
holds by (
A.9
) with
a=0
. Therefore we may assume that
t\not=T_{a}

for any
a\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}
, and we further set
b:=\eta^{\prime}(t)
, so that

b\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
by (
A.10
) with
0
in place of
b
.
By considering
\iota\circ\eta^{\prime}
instead of
\eta^{\prime}
when
b<0
, where

\iota(z):=-\overline{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we may and do assume that
b>0

in the rest of this proof.

Let
a\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
, so that
\eta^{\prime}(T_{a})\geq a
.
If
\eta^{\prime}(T_{a})>b
, then
b
belongs to the interior of
the compact interval
\partial^{\mathrm{bot}}K_{0,T_{a}}
in
\mathbb{R}
, hence

\eta^{\prime}(t)=b\not\in\eta^{\prime}([T_{a},\infty))
by (
A.9
) with
0
in place of
a
and thus
t<T_{a}
.
Combining this observation with
t\not=T_{a}
and (
A.10
), we easily obtain

(A.12)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle<T_{a}

for any
a\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(b,\infty)
,

(A.13)

\displaystyle T_{a}<t\quad\text{and}\quad a

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{\prime}(T_{a})<b

for any
a\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,b)
.

In particular,

(A.14)

\eta^{\prime}(s)\leq b\qquad\text{for any $s\in[0,t]$ with $\eta^{\prime}(s)\in\mathbb{R}$,}

since for any
a\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(b,\infty)
we have
s\leq t<T_{a}

by (
A.12
) and hence
\eta^{\prime}(s)<a
. Moreover,
by the monotonicity of
T_{a}
in
a\in(0,\infty)
,
\eta^{\prime}(t)=b
,
the continuity of
\eta^{\prime}
and (
A.13
) we have
\lim_{a\uparrow b}T_{a}\leq T_{b}\leq t
,

\eta^{\prime}(\lim_{a\uparrow b}T_{a})=\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni a\uparrow b}\eta^{\prime}(T_{a})=b
, therefore

(A.15)

\lim_{a\uparrow b}T_{a}=T_{b}\leq t,\quad\eta^{\prime}(T_{b})=b\quad\text{and}\quad\text{$T_{a}<T_{b}$ for any $a\in(0,b)$.}

Step 7.
We claim that
b=\eta^{\prime}(t)
satisfies (
A.9
) with
a=0

under the setting of Step 6. To see this, we inductively construct a sequence

\{b_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,b)
as follows.
First, noting (
A.15
), choose
b_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,b)
so that

|\eta^{\prime}(s)-b|<b
for any
s\in[T_{b_{0}},T_{b}]
, and set
a:=\min(\partial K_{T_{b_{0}}}\cap\mathbb{R})
,
which satisfies
a<0
by (
A.9
) with
0,b_{0}
in place of
a,b
.
Next, letting
n\geq 0
, supposing that
b_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,b)
is given, and noting
(
A.13
), (
A.15
) and that we have
(
A.3
) with

by (
A.9
) with

0,b_{n}
in place of

, choose
